ARRT Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Implied consent, inferred from the patient's cooperative actions during the exam): implied consent is inferred from a patient's behavior, such as climbing onto the table and holding still, rather than from a spoken or signed statement. Written consent is reserved for invasive procedures such as arthrograms, not routine diagnostic radiography. Oral consent requires an actual verbal agreement to the specific exam, which was never stated here. Proxy consent only applies when a legal representative is making decisions on behalf of an incapacitated patient.
  2. The correct answer is C (A general hospital marketing brochure describing available imaging services): protected health information requires an identifiable individual linked to health-related data, and a generic marketing brochure contains neither. A patient's name paired with an appointment time links identity to a health service and counts as protected information. A labeled image tying identity to a medical record is protected. A requisition combining a name with a diagnosis is a textbook example of protected health information.
  3. The correct answer is C (Involvement in decisions about their own plan of care): the Patient Care Partnership specifically promises patients a role in decisions affecting their own care, along with high quality care, privacy, and help with discharge planning. A guaranteed private room is not part of this document and depends on hospital capacity. Free transportation home is not a listed right, though help arranging discharge is included. Automatic billing waivers are not part of the partnership, which instead promises help understanding and resolving billing questions.
  4. The correct answer is D (A living will, stating the patient's future treatment preferences in advance): a living will is exactly this kind of advance directive, written while the patient is competent to spell out desired and undesired treatments for a future time when they cannot speak for themselves. A HIPAA authorization deals with releasing records, not treatment preferences. An informed consent form addresses a specific procedure happening now, not future hypothetical care. A health care proxy names a decision-maker rather than listing the patient's own treatment wishes directly.
  5. The correct answer is C (It is a HIPAA violation because there was no legitimate need to know): HIPAA restricts discussion of protected health information to staff with a legitimate treatment, payment, or operations purpose, and idle curiosity about a public figure does not qualify, even if the conversation stays inside the building. Staying within the department does not make the disclosure acceptable, since coworkers without a need to know are still unauthorized listeners. No physical records need to change hands for a verbal disclosure to violate HIPAA. The violation exists at the moment of disclosure regardless of whether a complaint is ever filed.
  6. The correct answer is B (The emergency doctrine, which permits treatment when delay would cause harm): the emergency doctrine allows an unconscious or incapacitated patient to be treated without consent when a delay to obtain it would likely cause serious harm, exactly the scenario described. Respondeat superior is a doctrine about employer liability for an employee's actions, not about obtaining consent. Res ipsa loquitur concerns proving negligence after an injury has already occurred, not authorizing treatment. Beneficence is an ethical principle about acting in the patient's interest, and it does not itself require written consent.
  7. The correct answer is D (Signed written consent specific to the invasive contrast procedure being performed): invasive procedures involving contrast instillation, such as a hysterosalpingogram, generally require documented written consent describing the risks, benefits, and alternatives before the study begins. Implied consent from scheduling an appointment does not meet the documentation standard for an invasive contrast procedure. Oral consent to a scheduling clerk addresses appointment logistics, not informed agreement to the medical risks involved. Calling the study purely diagnostic does not remove the requirement for written consent when contrast is instilled invasively.
  8. The correct answer is D (A Do Not Resuscitate order documenting the patient's resuscitation wishes): a valid DNR order tells the entire care team, including the radiographer, that CPR or advanced life support must not be initiated if the patient's heart or breathing stops, which directly changes the emergency response plan. The requisition explains why the exam was ordered but says nothing about resuscitation status. Insurance authorization is an administrative matter unrelated to emergency response. A prior unrelated imaging report has no bearing on how a sudden arrest during this exam should be handled.
  9. The correct answer is C (No, research participation requires separate, specific informed consent for the study): patients have the right to decide independently whether to participate in research, and a general admission consent form does not substitute for the detailed, study-specific informed consent that research requires. General admission consent covers routine, standard care, not experimental or investigational procedures with distinct risks. Insurance approval addresses payment for hospitalization and has nothing to do with research consent requirements. Research participation is not prohibited for hospitalized patients; it simply requires its own separate consent process.
  10. The correct answer is B (Explain that patients have a right to access their own health records): patients have a recognized right to access their own health information, including imaging studies and reports, and the technologist should acknowledge that right and direct her through the proper request process. Restricting access to only the ordering physician ignores the patient's own right to their information. A health care proxy is only needed when the patient cannot act for themselves, which is not the case here. There is no requirement that the patient wait until after a future exam to see records that already exist.
  11. The correct answer is B (Explaining the procedure, its risks and benefits, and alternatives, then obtaining a signature): true informed consent for an invasive procedure requires explaining the procedure, its risks, benefits, and alternatives in language the patient understands, followed by a signature confirming agreement. A brief verbal description of room setup does not cover risks, benefits, or alternatives as informed consent requires. Simply checking in at the front desk is administrative and does not constitute consent to an invasive procedure. A physician mentioning the exam during an earlier unrelated visit does not satisfy the documented, procedure-specific consent requirement.
  12. The correct answer is A (Arrange for a qualified medical interpreter rather than relying on the family member): a qualified medical interpreter should be used whenever possible because family members may filter information, lack accurate medical vocabulary, or compromise the patient's privacy during a sensitive discussion of consent. Knowing the family history well does not make the son a substitute for a trained interpreter's accuracy and neutrality. Having her sign without genuine understanding undermines the entire purpose of informed consent. Postponing indefinitely is unnecessary and unhelpful when an interpreter can typically be arranged promptly.
  13. The correct answer is A (Honor the patient's refusal, since a competent adult has the right to decline care): a competent adult patient retains the right to refuse any treatment or procedure, even one that has already been ordered and explained, and proceeding despite her clear refusal could constitute battery. A physician's order does not override a competent patient's right to decline, no matter how frustrated the physician becomes. Persuasion is acceptable only up to a point, but ultimately the decision belongs to the patient once she has refused. Physically forcing or coercing a patient into a procedure is never appropriate and is legally and ethically indefensible.
  14. The correct answer is C (Reposition or shield the screen, since visible protected health information is a HIPAA risk): protected health information visible to anyone without a legitimate need to know is a HIPAA exposure regardless of whether a formal complaint has ever been filed, so the screen should be repositioned or shielded proactively. Leaving it unchanged simply because no one has complained ignores the real privacy risk already occurring. Eliminating electronic requisitions entirely is an overcorrection when a simple repositioning solves the problem. Waiting for a complaint means tolerating an ongoing violation that should be corrected immediately.
  15. The correct answer is A (No, a health care proxy only acts when the patient truly cannot make decisions): a health care proxy is activated when a patient genuinely cannot participate in decision-making, and brief sedation-related unresponsiveness during an otherwise stable, expected procedure does not by itself trigger that authority. Unresponsiveness alone does not automatically activate the document if the situation is expected and the patient remains medically stable. Sedation itself does not create an automatic legal trigger for proxy authority absent an actual decision that needs to be made. Restricting proxy authority only to cardiac or respiratory arrest is too narrow and not how these documents function in practice.
  16. The correct answer is D (Battery, meaning unauthorized physical contact carried out without valid consent): battery is intentional, unauthorized physical contact, and continuing to touch or position a patient after they have refused meets this definition even if the technologist believes the exam is medically appropriate. Negligence involves an unintentional failure to meet a standard of care, not an intentional act of unauthorized touching. Malpractice is professional negligence, which again describes an unintentional lapse rather than a deliberate act against a stated refusal. Beneficence is an ethical principle about doing good, and violating a patient's refusal is the opposite of respecting their autonomy.
  17. The correct answer is B (Respondeat superior, which places liability on the employer for an employee's actions): respondeat superior is exactly this doctrine, holding an employer such as a hospital responsible for a negligent act committed by an employee acting within the scope of their job. Res ipsa loquitur addresses proving negligence when an injury type would not normally occur without it, a different legal concept entirely. Informed consent concerns disclosure before a procedure, not employer liability for staff negligence. Standard of care defines the expected level of professional conduct rather than assigning liability to an employer.
  18. The correct answer is C (Res ipsa loquitur, since this type of injury rarely happens without negligence): res ipsa loquitur, 'the thing speaks for itself,' applies when an injury such as a fall from an imaging table is of a type that would not normally occur absent negligence, shifting the burden toward the defendant to explain. Respondeat superior addresses employer liability once negligence is established, not the initial presumption of negligence from the injury itself. Beneficence is an ethical principle unrelated to burden of proof in a legal claim. Implied consent concerns agreement to a procedure, not the legal inference drawn from an unexplained injury.
  19. The correct answer is D (Beneficence, the ethical obligation to act for the patient's genuine benefit): beneficence is precisely this principle, requiring the radiographer to actively promote the patient's wellbeing and avoid harm throughout the imaging encounter. Negligence describes a failure to meet a standard of care, which is the opposite of the positive duty beneficence describes. Malpractice is professional negligence, again a failure rather than an affirmative ethical duty. Battery describes unauthorized contact, an unrelated legal concept rather than an ethical principle of doing good.
  20. The correct answer is C (Negligence, since a reasonably prudent radiographer would have raised the rail first): negligence is the failure to act as a reasonably prudent radiographer would under similar circumstances, and failing to raise a side rail on a sedated patient before leaving the room fits this definition exactly. Battery requires unauthorized physical contact, but no such contact occurred here since the injury resulted from an omission, not a touch. Beneficence describes an ethical duty to do good, and this scenario instead illustrates a failure of care, not intent. Res ipsa loquitur is a legal doctrine about proof, not a description of the underlying conduct itself.
  21. The correct answer is A (Respondeat superior, since the technologist was acting within the scope of employment): respondeat superior holds an employer liable for an employee's negligent acts committed within the scope of their job duties, which is exactly why the hospital, not just the individual technologist, can be named as a defendant. Res ipsa loquitur addresses proving negligence from the nature of an injury, not the separate question of who bears liability once negligence exists. Beneficence is an ethical principle, not a legal basis for employer liability. Implied consent relates to agreeing to a procedure, not to accepting the risk of a technologist's error.
  22. The correct answer is B (Malpractice, since this is professional negligence committed by a licensed provider): malpractice is professional negligence committed by someone acting in a professional capacity, and using outdated technique factors that cause an avoidable repeat exposure fits this definition precisely. Battery requires unauthorized contact, but the patient consented to the exam itself, so the error here is technical rather than a matter of unauthorized touching. Beneficence describes acting for the patient's benefit, and while intent may have been good, the outcome reflects a failure of professional standard, not the presence of the ethical principle. Respondeat superior is a doctrine about employer liability, not a description of the technologist's own conduct.
  23. The correct answer is D (Battery, since the exam proceeded despite the patient's explicit refusal): battery is intentional, unauthorized physical contact, and performing the exam after a clear refusal, regardless of the physician's medical opinion, is exactly this kind of intentional unauthorized contact. Negligence describes an unintentional lapse in care, but here the technologist knowingly proceeded despite a stated refusal, making the act intentional rather than merely careless. Malpractice specifically describes unintentional professional negligence, which does not fit a deliberate act taken against a known refusal. Res ipsa loquitur is a doctrine about inferring negligence from an unexplained injury, not about a knowing, intentional act.
  24. The correct answer is C (The negligent acts of employees performed within the scope of their duties): respondeat superior holds an employer responsible for the negligent acts of personnel, including trainees under supervision, performed within the scope of their assigned duties, which covers this dropped cassette incident. Limiting liability to only fully certified staff ignores that students working under institutional supervision are also acting within the scope of hospital-sanctioned duties. Respondeat superior specifically applies to acts within the scope of employment, not acts falling outside it. The doctrine applies broadly to negligent conduct, not exclusively to intentional acts such as battery.
  25. The correct answer is B (Beneficence, since remaining silent failed to act in the affected patient's interest): beneficence requires actively promoting patient wellbeing, and staying silent about a known unsafe practice that ultimately harmed a patient represents a failure to uphold that duty to act in patients' best interest. Battery requires unauthorized physical contact, which silence does not involve at all. Respondeat superior is a legal doctrine about employer liability, not something that silence transfers or triggers on its own. Res ipsa loquitur addresses inferring negligence from an injury's nature, not proving intent, and it does not apply to a decision to stay silent.
  26. The correct answer is D (Decline and escalate the staffing problem, since practicing outside scope creates liability): practicing outside one's certified scope of practice exposes both the patient and the technologist to serious risk and is grounds for disciplinary action under the ARRT Rules of Ethics, so the appropriate response is to decline and raise the staffing issue through proper channels. A supervisor's request does not override legal scope-of-practice limits, no matter how urgent the staffing situation feels. Documenting that the supervisor ordered it does not remove the technologist's own legal and ethical responsibility for the outcome. Patient agreement cannot expand a technologist's legal scope of practice, since consent addresses the procedure's risks, not who is qualified to perform it.
  27. The correct answer is A (Document the repeat exposure accurately before leaving, since the record is a legal document): any repeated exposure must be documented accurately and promptly because the medical record is a legal document, and an incomplete record can later be used as evidence of negligence or concealment. Skipping documentation simply because the exam and patient encounter are finished does not eliminate the legal requirement to record what actually occurred. Waiting for someone to ask creates an unacceptable delay and risks the information never being recorded at all. Documentation should not depend on whether a physician happens to request it, since the duty to record exists independently of any specific request.
  28. The correct answer is D (Res ipsa loquitur, since the injury itself suggests negligence without direct proof): res ipsa loquitur applies precisely to this situation, where an injury of a type that would not normally occur without negligence shifts the burden of proof toward the defendant even absent an eyewitness account. Respondeat superior addresses whether an employer is liable once negligence is established, not whether negligence can be inferred from the injury itself. Beneficence is an ethical principle with no bearing on burden of proof in litigation. Implied consent concerns agreement to a procedure, an entirely separate legal question from how negligence is proven.
  29. The correct answer is C (A positioning aid, since it is used routinely to reduce motion, not restrict freedom): positioning aids like sandbags, sponges, and straps are used routinely without special orders to eliminate motion and improve image quality, and they do not restrict the patient's freedom since the patient can remove them. Simply touching or limiting the leg's movement for imaging purposes does not make a device a restraint, since restraints are specifically about restricting freedom for behavioral or safety reasons. Not every device used during imaging is automatically a restraint, which is exactly the distinction the exam tests. Positioning aids do not require a physician's order, unlike true restraints.
  30. The correct answer is D (A physician's order is required except in a genuine emergency situation): true restraints require a physician's order except in emergencies, and they must be applied correctly and monitored regularly to avoid injury. Claiming no order is ever needed simply because a device helps image quality confuses restraints with positioning aids, which do not require orders. A verbal order from any staff member does not meet the requirement, since restraint orders follow specific facility protocols tied to a physician. The order requirement is not limited to pediatric patients; it applies broadly whenever a true restraint is used on any patient.
  31. The correct answer is C (Because it is used for image quality, not to restrict the patient against their will): the defining feature of a positioning aid, including a Velcro strap the patient can loosen, is that it serves image quality purposes and does not restrict the patient's freedom against their wishes, unlike a true restraint. The physical strength of the material is irrelevant to the legal classification; the purpose and the patient's ability to remove it are what matter. The wrist or ankle location of a device does not determine whether it legally qualifies as a restraint. There is no blanket physician pre-approval that applies to every patient for positioning aids, since none is required in the first place.
  32. The correct answer is C (A restraint, since their purpose is to prevent the patient's freedom of movement for safety): these wrist restraints are applied specifically to prevent a confused patient from getting off the table against medical advice, which is the defining behavioral-safety purpose of a true restraint, not a positioning aid. Even though the practical effect is stillness, the underlying purpose, preventing the patient's freedom of movement due to confusion, defines it as a restraint rather than a positioning aid. A confused, combative patient cannot meaningfully request removal in the same voluntary sense a cooperative patient could, which is part of why an order is required. The category of device does not change based on which discipline physically applies it, since the purpose behind its use is what matters.
  33. The correct answer is B (Yes, because positioning aids like sponges do not require a physician's order): sponges used to steady anatomy for a sharper image are positioning aids, and positioning aids are used routinely without special orders because they do not restrict the patient's freedom for behavioral reasons. The requirement for an order applies to true restraints, not to any device that happens to touch a patient's hand for image quality. Technologists routinely and appropriately apply positioning aids themselves as part of standard exam technique. The patient's age has no bearing on whether a sponge used for image quality qualifies as a positioning aid rather than a restraint.
  34. The correct answer is C (Using an untrained family member to forcibly hold a patient bypasses proper restraint protocol): manually and forcibly holding an agitated patient down functions as a restraint, and bypassing the physician's order requirement and proper trained personnel by relying on an untrained family member skips necessary safety oversight. Family members are not an acceptable substitute for a properly ordered and monitored restraint, since the requirement exists to protect the patient from injury and inappropriate use. Physically holding a resistant patient still functions as restraint regardless of who performs the holding or what it is called informally. While radiation exposure to a family member is a real secondary concern requiring shielding and distance, it is not the primary issue with bypassing restraint protocol described here.
  35. The correct answer is B (The compression band is a positioning aid, while true immobilization needs a physician's order): a compression band used purely to steady anatomy for image quality is a positioning aid requiring no special order, while a papoose-style immobilizer used specifically to control a child's behavioral movement functions as a restraint requiring a physician's order except in an emergency. Not every device automatically requires an order regardless of purpose, since the purpose itself is what determines the classification. Frequency of use in pediatric radiography does not exempt a true restraint from the order requirement. Reversing the rule, requiring an order for the band but never for the immobilizer, gets the underlying distinction backwards.
  36. The correct answer is A (The exposure indicator, reflecting the actual radiation dose reaching the detector): the exposure indicator specifically reflects how much radiation reached the detector, and falsifying it to hide a repeat exposure or an overexposure is a serious violation of the medical record's integrity. Source-to-image distance is a technical setup parameter, not a record of actual radiation received by the detector. Milliampere-seconds is a selected technique factor before exposure, not a measurement of what the detector actually received. Body mass index describes patient size and has no direct bearing on the exposure indicator value itself.
  37. The correct answer is D (Cropping or masking anatomy specifically to hide a positioning error): cropping or masking anatomy specifically to conceal a positioning error or make an unacceptable image appear acceptable is fraudulent alteration of the medical record, since it misrepresents what was actually captured. Applying a standard vendor post-processing algorithm is a routine, accepted part of digital image optimization. Adjusting window and level to see soft tissue better is normal diagnostic viewing, not fraudulent alteration. Zooming in on a workstation to review detail does not change the underlying image data at all.
  38. The correct answer is D (It misrepresents image quality by hiding a positioning error rather than repeating the exam): cropping specifically to conceal a positioning error rather than repeating the exposure misrepresents the diagnostic quality of the image and is considered fraudulent alteration of the medical record. Cropping itself is not inherently a problem, but using it specifically to disguise an error crosses an ethical and legal line. File size is a technical, storage-related concern and is not the actual problem with this action. Whether the physician notices has no bearing on whether the underlying act of concealment was wrong.
  39. The correct answer is C (Go back and accurately document the repeat exposure as soon as possible): any repeated exposure must be documented accurately and promptly, since the record is a legal document and accurate repeat tracking supports both patient safety and quality improvement. Skipping documentation because the patient has left does not remove the requirement to record what actually happened during the exam. Logging it only if specifically asked risks the repeat never being documented at all and undermines the integrity of the record. Estimating rather than using the actual recorded exposure value introduces inaccurate information into a legal document.
  40. The correct answer is B (Decline to alter images to disguise quality issues and raise the concern through proper channels): cropping or masking anatomy to disguise a quality issue is fraudulent alteration of the medical record regardless of how long the practice has been occurring, and the technologist should decline and escalate the concern appropriately rather than comply. Longstanding department practice does not make an unethical or fraudulent action acceptable. Reasoning that the diagnosis is unchanged does not address the deeper problem of misrepresenting what the actual image quality was. Simply repeating every borderline exam without addressing the underlying instruction to disguise quality avoids the direct ethical conversation that needs to happen with the supervisor.
  41. The correct answer is D (Provide clear verbal instructions and describe the room and steps as the exam proceeds): for a patient who is blind, clear verbal explanation of the environment and each step of the exam is the appropriate accommodation, since visual materials of any kind cannot be read regardless of print size. A larger printed sheet still relies on vision, which does not help a patient who cannot see it at all. Relying on posted signage assumes a level of visual access the patient does not have. Asking an untrained stranger to relay medical instructions raises both accuracy and privacy concerns and is not an appropriate substitute for staff communication.
  42. The correct answer is D (Facing the patient directly so they can read lips and see facial expressions): facing a hearing-impaired patient directly allows them to read lips and pick up facial cues, which is a key adaptation for this specific impairment. Speaking from behind the patient removes any visual cues entirely and makes understanding much harder, not easier. Writing everything down can help in some cases but is not always necessary or practical if the patient can read lips reasonably well, so it is not the single most direct strategy. Simply raising volume without facing the patient does not address the core issue if the patient relies partly on visual cues rather than volume alone.
  43. The correct answer is A (The teach-back method, asking the patient to repeat the instructions back): the teach-back method specifically involves asking the patient to explain the instructions in their own words, which reveals genuine understanding rather than polite nodding. Using more medical jargon would likely reduce understanding rather than confirm it, since plain language is the recommended approach instead. Passive listening does not actively verify anything, since a confused patient may simply stay silent rather than ask. A signature confirms receipt of information but does not verify that the patient actually understood what was explained.
  44. The correct answer is B (Recognize it as a cultural communication difference rather than disinterest or confusion): cultural and social factors affect comfort with eye contact, and some cultures view direct eye contact with an unfamiliar adult as disrespectful, so avoiding it does not indicate disinterest. Assuming inattention and repeating loudly misreads a cultural norm as a hearing or attention problem. Interpreting avoided eye contact as cognitive impairment is an unsupported and inappropriate leap without other clinical evidence. Avoided eye contact says nothing about consent, which should be assessed through the patient's actual verbal and behavioral responses to the explained procedure.
  45. The correct answer is B (Use simple, concrete language with short steps and repeat information as needed): patients with cognitive impairment benefit from simple, concrete language, one step at a time, and repetition, which helps them process and retain the information. Speaking more quickly works against a patient who already struggles to process complex information, making understanding harder rather than easier. Repeating the exact same complex wording, just louder, does not address the actual problem, which is complexity rather than volume. Bypassing the patient entirely to speak only to family disregards the patient's own right to be informed and involved to the extent they are able.
  46. The correct answer is A (Use age-appropriate, simple explanations and allow a parent to remain nearby if possible): pediatric patients need age-appropriate explanations and often benefit from having a parent stay nearby for reassurance and comfort during an unfamiliar exam. Detailed clinical terminology is inappropriate for a child and is more likely to confuse or frighten them further. Removing a comforting parent from the room typically increases rather than decreases a frightened child's anxiety. Skipping explanation entirely tends to increase fear of the unknown rather than reduce it, even in young patients.
  47. The correct answer is A (Acknowledge her emotional state gently, then explain the exam calmly with simple, honest language): emotional status, including denial related to a diagnosis, changes how much detail a patient can absorb, so gently acknowledging her state while still providing calm, honest, simple explanation respects both her emotional needs and her right to be informed. Avoiding her diagnosis entirely ignores the emotional context shaping how she will process information about the exam. Insisting on more active engagement before explaining anything is not a reasonable precondition and could increase her distress unnecessarily. Assuming she is incapable of understanding based on emotional withdrawal is an unsupported leap, since denial does not equal a cognitive inability to process information.
  48. The correct answer is A (Wait for the qualified interpreter rather than relying on the minor family member): a qualified medical interpreter should be used whenever possible rather than a family member, and using a minor child specifically raises additional concerns about medical vocabulary, emotional burden, and privacy, so waiting a reasonable twenty minutes for a non-urgent exam is appropriate. Using the daughter is not automatically better than a short wait when the exam is not urgent and a qualified alternative is available soon. Proceeding with only gestures risks miscommunication about important steps like positioning or breath-holding instructions. Permanently canceling is an overreaction when a brief, reasonable wait solves the problem entirely.
  49. The correct answer is C (The purpose of the exam, its approximate duration, and any relevant dose or risk information): patient education appropriately covers the exam's purpose, expected duration, and relevant dose or risk information explained in terms the patient can understand without causing unnecessary alarm. Detailed technical exposure settings are not meaningful or necessary information for the patient and belong to the technical record instead. Reciting every conceivable complication regardless of likelihood can cause unnecessary alarm rather than helping the patient make an informed decision. Billing codes and insurance coverage are administrative matters, not part of core clinical patient education about the exam itself.
  50. The correct answer is A (Nothing by mouth, or NPO, for several hours before the exam): patients are typically instructed to remain NPO for several hours before an upper GI series so the stomach is empty and the barium can properly coat the mucosa without interference from food. Drinking a large amount of water beforehand is not the correct preparation and could dilute or interfere with the study. Eating a full meal shortly before the exam is the opposite of correct preparation and would compromise image quality. Barium studies generally do require specific dietary restriction beforehand, so claiming none is needed is inaccurate.
  51. The correct answer is B (Metformin may need to be held around the time of contrast administration): medication reconciliation should flag that metformin may need to be held around iodinated contrast administration due to a rare but serious risk related to kidney function, and this should be confirmed with her physician. Stating there is no interaction at all is inaccurate and could put the patient at risk if kidney function is impaired. Doubling her dose is never an appropriate instruction from a technologist and is not related to the actual precaution. There is no requirement or benefit to taking metformin together with an oral contrast agent, since the concern involves iodinated contrast and renal clearance, not absorption timing.
  52. The correct answer is B (Increase fluid intake to help the body clear the contrast media more effectively): post-exam instructions after iodinated contrast administration typically include increasing fluid intake, which helps the kidneys clear the contrast media from the body more efficiently. Telling a patient to avoid fluids entirely is the opposite of appropriate guidance and could slow contrast clearance. Instructing a patient to alter their own medication dosing, such as doubling a diuretic, is outside a technologist's scope and not standard discharge guidance. Contrast media is not cleared instantly, so claiming no instructions are needed ignores a real and useful piece of patient education.
  53. The correct answer is D (Give a basic, accurate explanation and refer detailed medical questions to her physician): radiographers should be able to give a basic, accurate answer about why a particular modality was chosen while directing more detailed medical decision-making questions to the ordering physician or radiologist. Claiming X-ray is universally superior to MRI is factually incorrect, since each modality has different strengths depending on the clinical question, such as bone detail versus soft tissue detail. Speculating that cost drove the decision is unhelpful and likely inaccurate, since clinical appropriateness typically drives modality choice for a suspected fracture. Refusing to engage at all is unnecessarily dismissive when a simple, accurate answer is entirely appropriate for a technologist to provide.
  54. The correct answer is A (She should arrange for someone to drive her home after receiving sedation): a scheduling and discharge consideration for sedated procedures is arranging a ride home, since sedation can impair reaction time and judgment for hours afterward even if the patient subjectively feels alert. Feeling alert is not a reliable indicator of full recovery from sedating medication, so self-assessment alone should not guide the decision to drive. Sedation does affect reaction time and coordination, which is exactly why the instruction exists in the first place. The need for a ride does not depend on the specific dose given, since standard practice requires arranging transportation for any sedating procedure regardless of dose.
  55. The correct answer is B (The non-barium exam should generally be scheduled first, since retained barium can obscure other studies): scheduling considerations require planning non-barium exams before barium studies whenever possible, since retained barium can obscure other imaging studies for days afterward. Claiming it makes no difference ignores the real, well-documented interference retained barium can cause on subsequent imaging. Barium studies should not automatically be scheduled first; the correct sequencing principle runs the opposite direction. Requiring both exams on the same day is unnecessary and does not address the actual scheduling concern about barium interference over subsequent days.
  56. The correct answer is B (Balance, maintaining a wide stance with feet positioned shoulder-width apart): balance specifically refers to maintaining a wide, stable base of support with feet positioned shoulder-width apart, which is one of the three core principles of good body mechanics. Alignment refers to keeping the back straight and using the legs rather than the back to lift, a related but distinct principle. Controlled movement refers to moving smoothly rather than jerking and seeking help when needed, also a separate principle. Leverage reversal is not one of the three recognized body mechanics principles taught in this context.
  57. The correct answer is D (A gait belt, worn around the waist to support an unsteady walking patient): a gait belt is precisely this device, a fabric belt around the waist that provides a secure handhold for a technologist assisting an unsteady but ambulatory patient. A Hoyer lift is a mechanical, sling-based lift for patients who cannot assist at all with movement. A transfer board is a smooth, rigid board used to slide a patient between surfaces, not a walking aid. A restraint vest is used to limit torso movement for behavioral or safety purposes, an entirely different function.
  58. The correct answer is C (A Hoyer lift, a sling-based device for a fully dependent patient): a Hoyer lift is a mechanical, sling-based device designed for patients who cannot assist with their own movement at all, such as a paralyzed patient, lifting them safely without relying on their own strength. A gait belt assumes the patient has some ability to stand and walk with support, unlike a fully dependent patient. A transfer board is a rigid sliding surface used for a lateral slide, not a lifting mechanism for a fully dependent patient. A wheelchair brake is a safety feature that locks wheels and has no role in lifting or transferring a patient.
  59. The correct answer is C (A sliding transfer using a rigid transfer board bridged between the two surfaces): a patient who cannot bear weight needs a mechanical lift or a sliding transfer board, and since additional help is available and the patient has some upper body strength to assist, a board-assisted slide with staff support is appropriate. A standing pivot transfer requires the patient to bear weight through the legs, which this patient cannot do. Asking a non-weight-bearing patient to move unassisted risks a fall and is inappropriate regardless of available help. A gait belt is designed to support an ambulatory patient's balance while walking, not to substitute for a lift or slide when a patient cannot bear weight at all.
  60. The correct answer is C (Alignment, using the legs rather than the back to perform the lift): alignment specifically means keeping the back straight and using the legs, by bending the knees, to perform a lift, protecting the spine from injury. Balance refers to foot positioning and stance width, a related but separate principle from how the lift itself is executed. Controlled movement refers to the smoothness of the motion during the lift, not which muscle groups bear the load. Keeping a load close to the body, not far from it, is the correct technique, so describing distance from the body as the principle here is backwards.
  61. The correct answer is B (Controlled movement, which includes getting help for any patient that cannot be safely moved alone): controlled movement specifically includes recognizing when a patient cannot be safely moved alone and getting help rather than attempting it solo, which is exactly what was skipped here. The scenario does not describe a foot-positioning problem, so balance is not the principle most directly violated. Even a technically straight back does not prevent injury if the load itself is simply too much for one person, so alignment alone was not the issue. Asking for help with an unsafe patient move is not optional based on time pressure; it is a core safety principle regardless of how busy the department is.
  62. The correct answer is A (Lock the stretcher wheels first to prevent unintended movement during the transfer): fall prevention includes locking wheelchair and stretcher wheels before any transfer, since an unlocked stretcher can shift unexpectedly and cause the patient to fall during the move. Locking wheels is not limited to wheelchairs; it applies equally and importantly to stretchers during any transfer. Asking the patient to hold still does not address the mechanical hazard of an unlocked, movable stretcher. Locking the wheels only after the transfer is already complete defeats the entire safety purpose, since the risk exists specifically during the movement itself.
  63. The correct answer is B (Use the available mechanical lift rather than attempting a risky manual lift to save time): a fully dependent patient needs a mechanical lift, and when one is readily available it should be used rather than risking injury to staff and patient with a manual lift chosen only to save time. Having two staff members present does not make a manual lift of a fully dependent, heavy patient safe if a mechanical lift is the appropriate tool for that level of dependency. Waiting unnecessarily for a third person is not required when a mechanical lift, the actually appropriate device, is already accessible. Asking a fully dependent patient to bear any weight contradicts the very definition of their care needs and risks injury to the patient.
  64. The correct answer is A (Above the level of the patient's heart throughout the entire transport): IV pumps and infusion catheters must be kept above the level of the heart to prevent backflow into the tubing, and the pump should be repositioned rather than disconnected whenever possible during transport. Requiring the exact same level as the heart is unnecessarily precise and not the actual clinical guideline. Keeping it below heart level is the opposite of correct practice and increases the risk of backflow. The position relative to the heart is clinically significant and does matter for preventing complications during transport.
  65. The correct answer is B (Below the level of the bladder at all times during the exam): a Foley catheter drainage bag must always be kept below the level of the bladder to prevent urine from flowing backward into the bladder, which increases infection risk. Keeping it above bladder level is precisely the error that risks reflux and should be avoided. Requiring an exact level match is not the actual clinical standard, which calls for the bag to remain lower, not equal. Bag position relative to the bladder is clinically important and directly affects infection risk, so it is not irrelevant.
  66. The correct answer is D (The tube should not be clamped or pulled, and its radiopaque tip should remain visible): nasogastric tubes should not be clamped or pulled, and since this exam specifically exists to confirm radiopaque tip placement, keeping the tube undisturbed and positioned correctly on the image is essential. Clamping the tube during the exposure is unnecessary and not part of standard handling for this type of exam. Pulling the tube back would directly interfere with the very purpose of the exam, which is to confirm the tube's actual resting position. The tube's position is the entire clinical reason for the exam, so claiming it has no bearing is incorrect.
  67. The correct answer is A (Use alternative communication methods and keep suction equipment accessible nearby): tracheostomy patients cannot speak normally and may need alternative communication methods, such as writing or gesturing, and suction equipment should remain accessible nearby in case of airway needs during the exam. Speaking loudly does not restore a tracheostomy patient's ability to produce normal speech, since the physical airway itself has been altered. Assuming the patient cannot communicate at all ignores the availability of alternative methods and is disrespectful to the patient's ability to participate. Removing a tracheostomy tube is well outside a radiographer's scope of practice and would create a dangerous airway emergency.
  68. The correct answer is D (Keep the cannula connected and flowing continuously, and check the portable tank level beforehand): oxygen delivery systems must stay connected and flowing throughout the exam, and portable tank levels should be checked before leaving the department to avoid running out during transport or imaging. Disconnecting oxygen, even briefly, risks patient desaturation and is not an appropriate convenience measure. Switching delivery devices is a clinical decision outside a radiographer's scope and not something to do on arrival without orders. Oxygen delivery is directly relevant to patient safety throughout the exam and cannot simply be ignored.
  69. The correct answer is B (Reposition the bag so it hangs below bladder level but off the floor to avoid contamination): the drainage bag should be kept below bladder level to prevent reflux, but it should never be set on the floor or hung on a moving part of the table, both of which risk contamination or accidental disconnection. Leaving the bag on the floor ignores real infection control concerns associated with floor contact. Hanging the bag on a moving table part risks tension, disconnection, or injury as the table moves during the exam. Raising the bag above bladder level directly contradicts the correct positioning principle and increases reflux risk.
  70. The correct answer is D (Reposition or extend the tubing to avoid pulling it taut during patient movement): tubing must never be pulled taut during patient movement, since tension can dislodge the IV catheter or cause injury, so the technologist should reposition or add slack to the line before the patient shifts further. Allowing tension to persist risks dislodging the IV site, which is exactly the outcome proper tubing management is meant to prevent. Disconnecting IV tubing is not appropriate without orders and interrupts the patient's ongoing infusion. Asking the patient to avoid all movement is not a reliable solution and does not address the underlying tubing management problem.
  71. The correct answer is B (Keep the IV pump above heart level and the drainage bag below bladder level): correct equipment management requires the IV pump to stay above heart level to prevent backflow while the drainage bag stays below bladder level to prevent urine reflux, and both principles must be maintained simultaneously during any transfer. Raising the drainage bag above the patient's body directly contradicts correct catheter management and increases infection risk through reflux. Disconnecting the IV line is not an appropriate shortcut and interrupts necessary therapy without clinical justification. Setting the bag on the mattress, while off the floor, does not achieve the required below-bladder-level positioning relative to the patient's body during the move.
  72. The correct answer is A (A reading of approximately 120 over 80 millimeters of mercury): a blood pressure around 120/80 mm Hg is the generally accepted normal reading for an adult at rest and is the benchmark radiographers should recognize. A reading of 220/140 mm Hg is dramatically elevated and represents a hypertensive emergency, not a normal value. A reading of 70/40 mm Hg is dangerously low and indicates significant hypotension, not a normal finding. A reading of 300/200 mm Hg is an extreme and life-threatening value, far outside any normal range.
  73. The correct answer is D (A normal resting rate of about 12 to 20 breaths per minute): a normal adult resting respiratory rate falls in the range of about 12 to 20 breaths per minute, and rates significantly outside this range warrant attention. A rate of 40 to 60 breaths per minute would represent severe tachypnea, far above normal. A rate of 2 to 5 breaths per minute would represent dangerously slow, inadequate breathing, not a normal finding. A rate of 80 to 100 breaths per minute is not physiologically consistent with normal adult breathing and is far too high.
  74. The correct answer is A (Approximately 98.6 degrees Fahrenheit, equal to about 37 degrees Celsius): 98.6 degrees Fahrenheit, equivalent to 37 degrees Celsius, is the classically cited normal average oral body temperature for an adult. A reading of 108.6 degrees Fahrenheit would represent an extreme, life-threatening hyperthermia far beyond a normal or even febrile temperature. A reading of 88.6 degrees Fahrenheit would represent severe hypothermia, well below normal. A reading of 78.6 degrees Fahrenheit is not physiologically compatible with survival and is far outside any realistic clinical range.
  75. The correct answer is C (Report this concerning vital sign to the appropriate staff before proceeding with the exam): a resting pulse well above the normal range of about 60 to 100 beats per minute is a concerning finding that should be reported to appropriate staff before continuing, since it could signal an underlying problem needing attention. Proceeding without reporting ignores a clinically significant finding that could affect patient safety during the exam. While a technologist does not diagnose the cause, recognizing that a value is outside the expected range and escalating it is well within the recognized scope of monitoring and reporting duties. Asking the patient to hold their breath repeatedly is not an appropriate or effective way to address an elevated pulse rate.
  76. The correct answer is D (Observe and report the change in mental status to appropriate staff before proceeding): observing physical signs such as level of consciousness is part of patient monitoring, and a noticeable change from the previous day should be reported to appropriate staff before proceeding with the exam. Mental status changes are clinically significant and should never be dismissed as irrelevant to the patient's care. Waiting until after the exam to document or report a concerning change delays potentially important intervention. Assuming an unconfirmed cause like fatigue without reporting the observation risks missing a more serious underlying problem.
  77. The correct answer is A (Never leaving a confused or sedated patient unattended on an imaging table): fall prevention specifically includes never leaving a confused or sedated patient unattended on an imaging table, even briefly, since a fall can happen in just moments. Wheel locking is a separate fall-prevention principle related to transfer devices, not to leaving a patient unattended on the table itself. Table height matters throughout the encounter, not only at first arrival, particularly whenever a patient is getting on or off. Age-based restriction of side rail use is not an accurate description of fall-prevention practice, which applies broadly based on patient risk factors, not age alone.
  78. The correct answer is C (Stop the exam, notify the radiologist or appropriate staff, and continue monitoring the patient closely): a significant, symptomatic change from baseline vital signs during an ongoing procedure warrants stopping to notify appropriate staff and closely monitoring the patient rather than pushing forward. Continuing the exam to finish quickly prioritizes completion over a clear safety concern that has just emerged. Reassurance alone does not address a physiologic change that could indicate a developing complication requiring evaluation. Waiting until after the exam to document delays the escalation and monitoring the patient actually needs in the moment.
  79. The correct answer is D (Anaphylaxis, a severe reaction involving airway compromise and circulatory collapse): anaphylaxis is the severe, life-threatening allergic reaction involving airway swelling and shock that requires immediate emergency response, including epinephrine and activation of the emergency response system. A single mild hive with no other symptoms is a mild reaction requiring only observation, not an emergency response. A brief feeling of warmth is a well-recognized mild, expected sensation during contrast injection and not itself an allergic emergency. Mild nausea that resolves on its own is also classified as a mild reaction, not the severe reaction described in the question.
  80. The correct answer is D (Protect the patient from injury by clearing the area and cushioning the head): the correct response to a seizure is to protect the patient from injury by clearing nearby objects and cushioning the head, never restraining the limbs or placing anything in the mouth. Restraining the limbs can actually cause injury and does not stop the seizure itself. Placing any object in the mouth during a seizure is a well-known, dangerous myth that can cause choking or dental injury. Holding the patient completely still is not appropriate and can cause harm; the goal is protecting the patient from the environment, not stopping their movement by force.
  81. The correct answer is B (Sudden confusion, noticeable sweating, and visible shakiness developing over a few minutes): hypoglycemia classically causes relatively sudden confusion, sweating, and shakiness, requiring prompt recognition and response. Excessive thirst with fruity-smelling breath is instead the classic pattern of hyperglycemia, high blood sugar, not low blood sugar. Hyperglycemia, not hypoglycemia, is the condition that develops more slowly with symptoms building over time. A diabetic reaction, whether hypoglycemia or hyperglycemia, does produce a detectable behavior change, so claiming no change occurs is inaccurate.
  82. The correct answer is A (A moderate reaction requiring close monitoring and possible antihistamine treatment): more extensive hives combined with wheezing fits the description of a moderate reaction, which requires close monitoring and possibly antihistamine treatment, distinct from a mild reaction. A mild reaction involves only a few hives or nausea with warmth, not wheezing, and requires only observation and reassurance, less than what this presentation calls for. Severe reactions involve laryngeal edema, severe bronchospasm, loss of consciousness, or cardiac arrest, which is more than what is described here. Hives and wheezing are not a normal, expected response to contrast and should never be dismissed as such.
  83. The correct answer is B (Call for help or activate the emergency response system immediately): the first action in a cardiac or respiratory arrest is to call for help or activate the emergency response system, followed by CPR if trained and use of an AED as soon as one is available. Administering intravenous medication is outside a radiographer's scope of practice and is not the appropriate first action regardless of training. Waiting silently for someone else to notice wastes critical time in a true emergency. Moving the patient off the table before securing help and starting appropriate response delays the actions that matter most in the first moments of an arrest.
  84. The correct answer is D (Stop the exam and notify appropriate staff so blood glucose can be checked and treated): sudden confusion, pallor, and sweating in a diabetic patient suggests possible hypoglycemia, which requires stopping the exam and notifying appropriate staff so blood glucose can be assessed and treated promptly. Continuing the exam dismisses a recognizable and potentially serious pattern of symptoms that requires timely response. Giving water does not address hypoglycemia, which requires appropriate glucose treatment, not general hydration. Waiting until the exam is finished delays care for a patient who may be developing a true medical emergency.
  85. The correct answer is D (Use the department's latex-free supply cart for all equipment involved in her care): a known latex allergy requires using the department's latex-free supply cart for all equipment involved in her care, since latex is found in many common supplies including gloves, tourniquets, and catheter balloons. Latex reactions can be significant and are a recognized clinical concern, so proceeding with standard supplies is inappropriate for a patient with a known allergy. Using latex gloves while only avoiding the tourniquet still exposes the patient to a known allergen and does not fully address the risk. Latex-free alternatives are a standard part of most departments' emergency preparedness and should be readily available rather than requiring postponement.
  86. The correct answer is B (Stabilize the patient and avoid unnecessary movement, especially with a suspected spine injury): physical injury or trauma during care, especially with a suspected spine injury, requires stabilizing the patient without unnecessary movement, along with immediately notifying the radiologist and nursing staff. Quickly repositioning the patient to stay on schedule risks worsening a potential spinal injury and prioritizes efficiency over safety. Moving the patient into a seated position for imaging convenience is inappropriate when a spinal injury is suspected. While avoiding unnecessary movement is correct, taking absolutely no action and failing to notify appropriate staff is also inappropriate, since prompt notification is part of the correct response.
  87. The correct answer is C (Recognize the progression toward a severe reaction and immediately activate the emergency response system): throat tightness and wheezing indicate progression toward a severe reaction involving airway compromise, such as laryngeal edema, which requires immediately stopping the injection and activating the emergency response system. Continuing to treat this as a mild reaction ignores the clear escalation in symptoms that has just occurred. Waiting to see if throat tightness resolves on its own wastes critical time in what could become a life-threatening airway emergency. Finishing the planned contrast injection while the patient is developing airway symptoms is dangerous and directly contradicts the appropriate emergency response.
  88. The correct answer is C (Clear equipment away from the patient, cushion her head, and prevent bystanders from restraining her limbs): the priority during a seizure is protecting the patient from nearby hazards like equipment, cushioning the head, and stopping well-meaning bystanders from restraining the limbs, since restraint can cause injury without stopping the seizure. Encouraging bystanders to hold her limbs more firmly is exactly the wrong instinct and increases injury risk. Placing any object in the mouth during a seizure is a dangerous myth that risks choking or dental trauma. Waiting until after the seizure to move hazardous equipment away delays protecting the patient during the actual period of risk.
  89. The correct answer is B (Notify the radiologist before proceeding, since the missed premedication changes the risk assessment): premedication is specifically ordered in advance for patients with a known contrast allergy history to reduce reaction risk, so discovering it was missed changes the clinical risk picture and requires notifying the radiologist before proceeding. Assuming premedication rarely matters ignores its documented role in reducing reaction severity for at-risk patients. Administering medication doses is outside a radiographer's scope and should not happen without physician direction. Permanently canceling without even discussing the situation with the radiologist skips a reasonable step, since the radiologist may choose to proceed with extra precautions, delay, or reschedule based on clinical judgment.
  90. The correct answer is C (Apply the AED and follow its voice prompts while continuing CPR as directed): once an AED is available, it should be used as soon as possible, following its voice prompts, while CPR continues as directed by the device between analyses and shocks. Setting the AED aside until transport wastes valuable time in an arrest where early defibrillation improves outcomes. Ignoring the AED entirely once it has arrived contradicts the standard emergency response sequence, which specifically calls for its use as soon as available. An AED is designed to be used by any trained rescuer, not exclusively a physician, and waiting for one to arrive delays potentially lifesaving defibrillation.
  91. The correct answer is A (The pathogen, meaning the actual disease-causing organism responsible for the infection): the pathogen is the first link in the chain of infection, referring to the actual disease-causing organism such as a specific bacterium or virus. The reservoir is where the pathogen lives and multiplies, a separate link describing its environment. The portal of exit describes how the pathogen leaves the reservoir, such as through respiratory secretions, a different step in the chain. The susceptible host is the final link, describing a person whose defenses cannot prevent infection, unrelated to identifying the organism itself.
  92. The correct answer is B (The susceptible host, a person whose defenses cannot resist the pathogen): the susceptible host is the final link in the chain of infection, describing a person whose immune defenses cannot adequately resist the pathogen, a description that fits many hospitalized patients with weakened immunity. The portal of entry describes how the pathogen actually enters a new host's body, a distinct earlier link. The mode of transmission describes how the pathogen travels to reach the new host, not the host's own vulnerability. The reservoir is where the pathogen lives and multiplies, unrelated to describing the vulnerability of a new host.
  93. The correct answer is A (Indirect transmission, the broader category that includes airborne, vehicle, and vector spread): airborne spread is a form of indirect transmission, along with vehicle-borne and vector-borne spread, because the pathogen travels through the environment rather than passing directly between people. Direct contact transmission involves touching an infected person or their bodily fluids directly, a different category from airborne spread. Droplet transmission is a form of direct transmission involving larger particles over short distances, distinct from the small, far-traveling particles of airborne spread. Vector-borne transmission specifically involves an insect or animal carrier, which is not how airborne spread occurs.
  94. The correct answer is C (Standard Precautions, applied to every patient encountered regardless of known status): Standard Precautions apply to every patient, every time, regardless of known infection status, precisely because infectious status cannot always be known in advance. Airborne Precautions are transmission-based precautions used only for specific patients known or suspected to carry pathogens like tuberculosis, not universally applied. Droplet Precautions are similarly reserved for patients with suspected or confirmed droplet-spread organisms, not applied to every patient. Neutropenic Precautions protect a specific immunocompromised patient from the environment and are not universally applied either.
  95. The correct answer is C (Sterilization, which destroys every microorganism present including resistant bacterial spores): sterilization destroys all microorganisms, including resistant spores, and is typically achieved with an autoclave using pressurized steam, required for anything entering sterile tissue or the vascular system. Disinfection reduces the number of pathogens on a surface but does not necessarily kill all spores, a lower level of processing than sterilization. Medical asepsis, or clean technique, reduces the number of organisms present through practices like hand hygiene, but does not eliminate all organisms the way sterilization does. Standard precautions are a broad infection-control framework applied to every patient, not a specific process for destroying microorganisms.
  96. The correct answer is D (MRSA, a classic organism spread primarily by touch requiring Contact Precautions): MRSA is a classic example of an organism spread by touch, requiring Contact Precautions with a gown and gloves whenever a patient is known or suspected to carry it. Influenza spreads primarily through larger respiratory droplets and requires Droplet Precautions instead. Tuberculosis travels on small airborne particles and requires Airborne Precautions with a fitted respirator, not simply contact protection. Measles is also spread by the airborne route and requires Airborne Precautions rather than Contact Precautions.
  97. The correct answer is C (A fitted N95 or higher respirator, ideally paired with a negative pressure room): tuberculosis requires Airborne Precautions, meaning staff must wear a fitted N95 or higher respirator, ideally in a negative pressure room, because the organism travels on small particles over longer distances. A standard surgical mask is appropriate for Droplet Precautions, such as influenza, but does not provide adequate protection against airborne particles like tuberculosis. Gloves and a gown alone address Contact Precautions but do not protect against the respiratory route by which tuberculosis actually spreads. No special equipment beyond routine gloves is far below the level of protection an active tuberculosis case requires.
  98. The correct answer is D (Yes, disinfection is appropriate for equipment surfaces that do not enter sterile tissue): disinfection, which reduces the number of pathogens on a surface, is the appropriate level of cleaning for equipment like the table and control panel between patients, while full sterilization is reserved for items entering sterile tissue or the vascular system. Requiring sterilization for every surface a patient touches goes well beyond the actual standard and is not practical or necessary for routine equipment. Cleaning surfaces between patients is a core part of Standard Precautions and is always necessary, not optional. This level of cleaning is required for every patient encounter under Standard Precautions, not only when a prior patient had a confirmed infection.
  99. The correct answer is B (Use a new sterile needle and syringe for every injection, never reusing one across patients): safe injection practices require a new sterile needle and syringe for every injection and prohibit reusing a syringe on more than one patient, even when drawing from a shared multi-dose vial. Changing only the needle while reusing the syringe still risks contaminating the shared vial and does not meet the safety standard. Labeling the vial with a date does not address the core risk of cross-contamination from syringe reuse. Reusing a syringe is never appropriate regardless of whether both patients are receiving the same type of contrast agent.
  100. The correct answer is B (Contact Precautions, meaning a protective gown and gloves for every interaction): C. difficile is spread by touch and contamination of surfaces, requiring Contact Precautions with a gown and gloves in addition to Standard Precautions. Airborne Precautions with a respirator are reserved for pathogens like tuberculosis that spread through small airborne particles, not C. difficile. Droplet Precautions address organisms spread through larger respiratory droplets, which is not how C. difficile spreads. Neutropenic Precautions protect an immunocompromised patient from outside pathogens and are the reverse purpose of precautions used for a patient who is themselves infectious.
  101. The correct answer is A (Neutropenic Precautions, which protect the immunocompromised patient from staff and the environment): neutropenic precautions, or reverse isolation, are used for an immunocompromised patient with very low white blood cell counts and essentially flip the usual direction of precautions to protect the vulnerable patient from staff and the environment rather than the other way around. Airborne, contact, and droplet precautions are all designed to protect staff and other patients from an infectious patient, which is the opposite purpose from what this patient's situation requires. This distinction, protecting the patient rather than protecting others from the patient, is exactly what makes neutropenic precautions unique among these options.
  102. The correct answer is C (A healthcare-associated, or nosocomial, infection acquired during this hospital stay): a healthcare-associated, or nosocomial, infection is one a patient acquires during a stay in a healthcare facility that they did not have on admission, exactly matching this scenario. A community-acquired infection would have originated outside the healthcare setting before admission, not five days into her hospital stay. Since she did not have this infection at admission, describing it as present on admission is factually incorrect based on the scenario. A congenital infection is present from birth and has no relationship to a recent hospital stay.
  103. The correct answer is A (No, hand hygiene with soap and water should last at least twenty seconds): Standard Precautions call for washing with soap and water for at least twenty seconds, or using alcohol-based hand sanitizer, before and after every patient contact, so a five-second wash falls well short of the recommended standard. Any brief contact with soap and water is not considered sufficient regardless of how busy the department is. Alcohol-based sanitizer is an acceptable alternative to soap and water, not a mandatory replacement for it in every situation. Being busy does not lower the actual hand hygiene standard required to protect patients and staff from infection transmission.
  104. The correct answer is D (Perform hand hygiene, remove PPE properly, and don fresh PPE before the second room): moving from a droplet-precautions room to a neutropenic patient's room requires proper hand hygiene and fresh PPE, since carrying influenza on soiled equipment or unwashed hands into a highly vulnerable, immunocompromised patient's room could cause serious harm. Wearing the same gown and mask into both rooms risks transmitting the influenza virus directly to a patient whose weakened immune system makes even mild pathogens dangerous. Gloves alone do not replace the need for hand hygiene, which addresses contamination that gloves cannot fully prevent. The order in which the rooms are entered does not eliminate the need for proper hand hygiene and PPE changes between them regardless of sequence.
  105. The correct answer is C (Use Airborne Precautions with a fitted N95 respirator immediately, since tuberculosis is not yet ruled out): when tuberculosis has not been ruled out in a coughing patient, prudent practice is to implement Airborne Precautions right away, since TB spreads via the airborne route and only a fitted N95 respirator (not a surgical mask used for Droplet Precautions) provides adequate protection. Assuming no precautions are needed simply because none are yet documented ignores a real, unconfirmed risk that warrants caution. Waiting indefinitely outside the room is not practical when the patient needs timely imaging and appropriate precautions can be applied instead. Entering with only routine gloves does not address the respiratory transmission risk a productive cough with unconfirmed tuberculosis presents.
  106. The correct answer is B (Gloves can become contaminated during donning if hands are not clean, still enabling transmission): if hands are not clean before donning gloves, contamination can transfer onto the outside of the gloves during the donning process itself, meaning skipped hand hygiene still allows a pathway for transmission despite glove use. Hand hygiene is not optional simply because a barrier precaution like gloves is also being used, since both work together rather than substituting for one another. Hand hygiene is actually one of the most effective ways to break the chain of infection at the mode of transmission link, so claiming it cannot break the chain is incorrect. Gloves alone do not eliminate every link in the chain, since contamination can still occur through improper donning, doffing, or other unprotected contact points.
  107. The correct answer is D (A Safety Data Sheet, commonly abbreviated SDS, for that specific hazardous chemical): a Safety Data Sheet, formerly called a Material Safety Data Sheet, must be available for every hazardous chemical and lists hazards, handling and storage instructions, required PPE, and first aid measures. An informed consent form addresses a patient's agreement to a procedure and has nothing to do with hazardous chemical information. An equipment quality control log tracks imaging equipment performance, not chemical hazard information. A general employee handbook covers broad workplace policies but does not provide the specific chemical hazard detail an SDS provides.
  108. The correct answer is D (Through the correct designated waste stream, never poured down a standard sink drain unless approved): hazardous materials must be disposed of through the correct designated waste stream and never poured down a standard sink drain unless specifically approved for that particular substance. Pouring hazardous chemicals down a sink assumes dilution neutralizes hazard, which is not a reliable or approved practice for regulated hazardous waste. Mixing hazardous waste with regular trash risks environmental contamination and violates proper hazardous waste handling requirements. Storing hazardous waste indefinitely in an unlabeled container creates a safety hazard and violates proper labeling and disposal timelines.
  109. The correct answer is C (The Safety Data Sheet for that specific chemical product): the Safety Data Sheet for that specific chemical lists first aid measures for exposure incidents, including eye contact, and every radiographer should know where the SDS binder or database is located for exactly this kind of situation. The patient's medical chart has no information about chemical safety or first aid for staff exposure. A vacation policy section of an employee handbook is entirely unrelated to chemical exposure first aid. An imaging equipment service manual addresses machine troubleshooting, not chemical safety information.
  110. The correct answer is B (Use appropriate PPE and dispose of the chemicals through the facility's approved hazardous waste process): film processing chemicals are a recognized hazardous material requiring appropriate PPE such as gloves and eye protection and disposal through the correct designated hazardous waste stream, never through routine methods. Pouring developer chemicals down a sink drain is inappropriate unless specifically approved for that substance, and assuming mild irritation makes it acceptable is not a safe assumption. Combining different chemicals together can create dangerous reactions and should never be done casually to save storage space. Regular trash disposal does not meet the requirements for hazardous chemical waste handling.
  111. The correct answer is B (Insist on locating the SDS resource anyway, since every hazardous chemical in the department requires one): every radiographer should know where the SDS binder or database is located regardless of whether an incident has ever occurred, since the requirement exists to prepare for a possible future emergency, not merely to reflect past experience. The absence of a past incident does not eliminate the ongoing requirement to maintain accessible SDS information for every hazardous chemical present. Verbal experience, however extensive, does not provide the detailed, chemical-specific first aid and handling information an actual SDS document contains. Waiting for an actual exposure incident before addressing a missing safety resource creates unnecessary risk that should be corrected proactively instead.
  112. The correct answer is A (Ionic contrast, which dissociates into charged particles and carries higher osmolality): ionic contrast dissociates into charged particles in solution, producing a higher osmolality that is associated with a higher rate of adverse reactions compared to non-ionic contrast. Non-ionic contrast does not dissociate, has lower osmolality, and is generally safer and more commonly used today despite its higher cost. Barium sulfate is a completely different category of contrast, an insoluble suspension used for GI studies, not an ionic or non-ionic iodinated agent. Oral positive contrast refers to a route and appearance category, not to the ionic versus non-ionic chemical distinction being described.
  113. The correct answer is C (Barium sulfate, an agent that can cause severe peritonitis if it leaks free): barium sulfate is never used when bowel perforation is suspected because free barium in the peritoneal cavity can cause a severe inflammatory reaction called barium peritonitis. Non-ionic iodinated contrast is actually the preferred alternative in a suspected perforation, not the agent to avoid. Ionic iodinated contrast is water-soluble and, like non-ionic contrast, is used instead of barium when perforation is a concern.
