Water-soluble iodinated contrast in general is the appropriate substitute for barium in this exact clinical situation, not the agent that must be avoided.
  114. The correct answer is D (eGFR, or estimated glomerular filtration rate, the most direct kidney measure): eGFR is specifically described as the most direct measure used to screen for contrast-induced nephropathy risk before administering IV iodinated contrast, reflecting how well the kidneys are filtering. Hemoglobin reflects oxygen-carrying capacity of the blood and does not directly assess kidney filtration function relevant to contrast clearance. Platelet count reflects clotting ability and is unrelated to screening for contrast-induced kidney injury risk. White blood cell count reflects infection or immune status, not kidney filtration function relevant to contrast administration.
  115. The correct answer is B (Extravasation, contrast leaking from the vein into the surrounding soft tissue): extravasation is the local complication where contrast leaks out of the vein into surrounding tissue, causing swelling and pain at the injection site. Anaphylaxis is a severe, life-threatening systemic allergic reaction, not a local injection-site complication. Laryngeal edema is throat swelling associated with a severe systemic reaction, unrelated to a localized injection-site leak. Bronchospasm is a severe systemic respiratory reaction, not a description of contrast leaking into local tissue.
  116. The correct answer is A (It does not indicate true iodine sensitivity, since iodine is not an allergen and shellfish proteins do not cross-react with contrast, but any significant allergy history modestly raises general reaction risk): iodine is a required trace element, not an allergen, so there is no such thing as a true iodine allergy, and shellfish allergens do not cross-react with iodinated contrast at all. A shellfish allergy is still noted on screening only because any history of significant allergic reactions, food or drug, is linked to a modestly higher general risk of a contrast reaction, the same way an unrelated allergy such as penicillin would be. It does not confirm a specific iodine allergy, since shellfish are not a meaningful dietary source of iodine relevant to this reaction and no cross-reactivity mechanism exists between shellfish proteins and contrast molecules. It is not irrelevant to document, since any significant allergy history still factors into the general risk assessment. Cancelling the exam outright is not warranted by a shellfish allergy alone.

Non-ionic contrast is actually more expensive than ionic contrast, not less, so cost is not the reason it was chosen. There is a real, clinically meaningful difference in osmolality and reaction rates between the two types, so claiming no difference exists is inaccurate. Ionic contrast has higher osmolality and a higher associated reaction rate, making it the less safe option of the two, not the safer one.

  161. The correct answer is B (The space charge, the free electron cloud hovering near the filament before exposure): Thermionic emission releases electrons from the heated tungsten filament, and these electrons accumulate in a cloud near the cathode surface even before the exposure begins. This resting cloud is called the space charge and represents a ready supply of electrons waiting to be accelerated once voltage is applied. The focal spot is a physical location on the anode, not an electron cloud, and remnant radiation refers to the exiting beam after patient interaction.
  162. The correct answer is D (Approximately 2,200 degrees Celsius, hot enough for electrons to boil off the filament): The electric current heats the tungsten cathode filament to roughly 2,200 degrees Celsius, and at that temperature electrons gain enough thermal energy to escape the metal surface. This process, called thermionic emission, produces the space charge that supplies electrons for every exposure. The other temperatures listed are either far too low to release electrons or closer to the anode's melting point rather than the filament's operating heat.
  163. The correct answer is A (It repels the electron cloud and shapes it into a narrow, controlled stream): Because the focusing cup carries a negative charge, it repels the negatively charged electron cloud emitted by the filament and forces those electrons into a tightly confined beam. This shaping ensures the electrons strike a small, precise area of the anode called the focal spot rather than scattering. Cooling the filament and absorbing stray electrons are not functions of the focusing cup, and the cup does not itself convert energy into photons.
  164. The correct answer is C (The nucleus attracts and deflects the electron, slowing it and releasing a photon): The positively charged nucleus pulls on the passing negative electron, bending its path and reducing its speed, and the kinetic energy lost in that deflection is released as an x-ray photon. Because the amount of deflection varies with how close the electron passes to the nucleus, bremsstrahlung photons can carry almost any energy value. Option A describes the photoelectric effect, option B describes characteristic radiation production, and option D describes coherent scattering, none of which is the mechanism behind bremsstrahlung.
  165. The correct answer is B (An outer shell electron drops down to fill the vacancy and emits a photon): Once the K-shell vacancy exists, an electron from a less tightly bound outer shell falls inward to fill it, and the energy difference between the two shells is released as a single photon of fixed, discrete energy. This is why characteristic radiation appears as a sharp spike rather than a continuous curve on the x-ray spectrum. The ejected electron does not return to the atom, and the nucleus itself plays no role in emitting the characteristic photon.
  166. The correct answer is A (Approximately 69.5 keV, the K-shell binding energy of a tungsten atom): Tungsten's K-shell electrons are bound to the nucleus with roughly 69.5 keV of energy, so an incoming electron must carry at least that much kinetic energy to eject one and create a vacancy. Because of this threshold, characteristic x-rays only appear in the beam once the kVp is set above roughly 70. Below that kVp setting, no electron in the tube has enough energy to overcome the K-shell binding energy, so the entire beam consists of bremsstrahlung radiation alone.
  167. The correct answer is D (Quality refers to penetrating power, controlled by kVp and measured by half-value layer): Beam quality describes how well the x-ray beam penetrates through tissue, and it is controlled primarily by the kVp setting selected at the console. The standard way to measure quality is half-value layer, defined as the thickness of a material needed to reduce the beam's intensity by half. Beam quantity, by contrast, simply refers to the number of photons in the beam and is controlled primarily by mAs, which is a separate concept from penetrating power.
  168. The correct answer is C (The number of photons in the beam, controlled primarily by mAs): Beam quantity simply describes how many photons make up the x-ray beam, and mAs is the exposure factor that most directly determines that number. Raising kVp also increases beam quantity as a side effect, since more electrons reach the target with enough energy to produce photons, but mAs remains the primary control. Penetrating power, wavelength distribution, and scatter fraction all describe other beam characteristics rather than quantity itself.
  169. The correct answer is B (The useful beam as it leaves the tube and travels toward the patient): Primary radiation is the term for the useful x-ray beam at the point it exits the tube and travels toward the patient, before it has interacted with any tissue. This primary beam carries the energy that will eventually form the diagnostic image. Once the beam has passed through the patient and lost photons to absorption and scatter, it is instead called remnant or exit radiation, which is a separate and later stage of the beam's path.
  170. The correct answer is A (Remnant or exit radiation, and it is what actually reaches the image receptor): Once the primary beam has passed through the patient and lost some photons to absorption while others were deflected as scatter, what remains is called remnant radiation, also known as exit radiation. It is this remnant beam, not the original primary beam, that actually strikes the image receptor and forms the picture. Bremsstrahlung and characteristic radiation describe how photons are produced at the target, not where the beam is along its path toward the patient.
  171. The correct answer is D (They travel in straight lines at the speed of light and can ionize matter): X-rays are a form of electromagnetic energy that travels in straight lines at the speed of light, and they have the ability to ionize matter by knocking electrons out of the atoms they pass through. They cannot be seen, heard, or felt by the human body, which is precisely why radiation protection practices are necessary. X-rays also do not travel in curved paths, and they do not lose all of their energy immediately upon contacting skin, since many photons pass through or scatter within the body.
  172. The correct answer is C (A rotating anode that continuously spreads the heat load across a larger surface area): Because electron deceleration at the target converts more than 99 percent of the energy into heat, the anode rotates rapidly so that the electron beam constantly strikes a fresh area of the target surface instead of one fixed spot. This spreads the intense heat load over a much larger total area and greatly reduces the risk of melting or pitting the target. A stationary target, the focusing cup, and the filament heating temperature all play different roles in tube operation but do not solve the anode heat dissipation problem.
  173. The correct answer is A (More than 99 percent converts to heat and fewer than 1 percent becomes x-rays): Electron deceleration at the anode target is remarkably inefficient by design, with well over 99 percent of the electrons' kinetic energy converting to heat and less than 1 percent converting into usable x-ray photons. This is exactly why heat management, through rotating anodes, angled target surfaces, and tube cooling systems, is one of the single biggest engineering challenges in x-ray tube design. The other listed proportions dramatically overstate how much of the beam's energy actually becomes diagnostic x-ray photons.
  174. The correct answer is B (A small, well-defined focal spot produces sharper spatial resolution on the final image): Concentrating the electron stream onto a small, precise focal spot on the anode minimizes geometric blur and produces a sharper, more spatially resolved diagnostic image. A tightly focused stream does not by itself reduce total photon output, prevent characteristic radiation, or change the kVp required to accelerate electrons. Focal spot size is instead primarily a factor in recorded detail and image sharpness, which is why tube design prioritizes a small, controlled target area.
  175. The correct answer is D (How closely each electron passes to the nucleus and how much it deflects): Bremsstrahlung photon energy depends on how close a given electron happens to pass to the positively charged nucleus and how sharply its path is bent, and since this distance varies randomly from electron to electron, the resulting photon energies form a continuous range. The fixed binding energy difference described in option A instead explains characteristic radiation's discrete spikes, not bremsstrahlung's continuous curve. Filament temperature and anode rotation speed influence electron supply and heat dissipation, not the specific energy of an individual bremsstrahlung photon.
  176. The correct answer is A (The bremsstrahlung share decreases steadily as kVp is increased at the console): as kVp rises above the tungsten K-shell threshold of roughly 70 kVp, an increasing proportion of electrons carry enough energy to eject K-shell electrons, so characteristic radiation makes up a growing percentage of the total beam. Because bremsstrahlung and characteristic radiation together make up the whole beam, this means bremsstrahlung's relative share correspondingly decreases as kVp increases, even though bremsstrahlung remains the numerically dominant contributor throughout the diagnostic range. The share does not stay fixed, does not increase, and does not vanish above 70 kVp, since that threshold is when characteristic radiation first appears, not when bremsstrahlung disappears.
  177. The correct answer is A (The beam consists entirely of bremsstrahlung radiation with no characteristic photons present): Characteristic radiation from tungsten requires an incoming electron with enough energy to overcome the K-shell binding energy of approximately 69.5 keV, which only happens once kVp is set above roughly 70. Since this exposure uses only 60 kVp, no electron in the tube carries enough energy to eject a K-shell electron, so the entire beam is produced through the bremsstrahlung mechanism alone. Options B, C, and D all incorrectly assume characteristic radiation is present at a kVp setting below the required threshold.
  178. The correct answer is B (The difference between the binding energies of the two involved electron shells): The photon released during characteristic radiation carries an energy exactly equal to the difference between the binding energy of the shell the electron fell from and the binding energy of the shell it fell into. Because these binding energies are fixed properties of the tungsten atom, the resulting photon energy is a single discrete value rather than a range. Nucleus distance during deflection determines bremsstrahlung photon energy instead, and neither anode rotation speed nor mAs affects the specific energy of an individual characteristic photon.
  179. The correct answer is C (The continuous bremsstrahlung curve with sharp characteristic spikes layered on top of it): The real emission spectrum is formed by combining the continuous, sloping bremsstrahlung curve, which spans a wide range of photon energies, with the sharp, discrete characteristic spikes that appear only once kVp exceeds the K-shell binding energy threshold. This combination produces the specific graphed shape radiographers learn to recognize in physics coursework. A flat baseline with one spike, two identical overlapping curves, or a series of spikes with no continuous curve at all does not describe the true diagnostic spectrum.
  180. The correct answer is D (Both frequency and energy increase as the photon wavelength gets shorter): Frequency and wavelength are inversely related, so as wavelength gets shorter, frequency rises, and because photon energy is directly proportional to frequency, shorter wavelength always corresponds to higher energy as well. This relationship explains why higher kVp technique produces a beam containing more short-wavelength, high-frequency, high-energy photons mixed in with the rest. Frequency and energy cannot move in opposite directions from each other, since both are tied directly to the same underlying wavelength change.
  181. The correct answer is B (The beam contains proportionally more short-wavelength, high-energy photons than before): Raising kVp accelerates the electrons to higher speeds before they strike the target, which shifts the resulting spectrum toward more short-wavelength, high-frequency, high-energy photons mixed into the beam. This does not mean the beam loses its longer-wavelength photons entirely, since the bremsstrahlung mechanism still produces a full continuous range of energies. The photon energy mixture is directly affected by kVp, so option C describing no change at all is incorrect.
  182. The correct answer is A (Half-value layer, the thickness of material needed to reduce intensity by half): Half-value layer is defined specifically as the thickness of a given material required to reduce the beam's intensity by half, and it is the standard way beam quality, meaning penetrating power, is measured and expressed. Milliampere-seconds describes the exposure factor that controls beam quantity rather than quality. Coulombs per kilogram measures exposure in air, and gray measures absorbed dose, neither of which directly quantifies how penetrating the beam itself is.
  183. The correct answer is D (The number of photons produced roughly doubles compared to the original exposure): Because mAs is the exposure factor that primarily controls beam quantity, doubling mAs while holding kVp constant roughly doubles the total number of photons produced at the target. This relationship is direct and predictable, so the photon count does not stay the same, decrease, or become unpredictable. Although raising kVp also increases quantity as a side effect, that variable was held constant in this scenario, leaving mAs as the sole factor driving the change in photon output.
  184. The correct answer is C (Intensity is cut to one quarter compared to the original, closer distance): The inverse square law states that beam intensity falls off proportionally to the square of the distance from the source, so doubling the distance reduces intensity to one divided by two squared, which equals one quarter of the original value. Intensity does not simply fall by half, stay the same, or increase, since distance and intensity are inversely related by the square of the change. This is exactly why stepping back is such an effective and simple radiation protection strategy for staff.
  185. The correct answer is A (Approximately 20 mR, since doubling distance reduces intensity to one quarter): Applying I1 multiplied by D1 squared equals I2 multiplied by D2 squared, 80 mR times 40 inches squared equals I2 times 80 inches squared, which solves to an I2 of approximately 20 mR. Doubling the distance from the source reduces intensity to one quarter of its original value, not one half, and intensity clearly does change with distance rather than staying fixed. Option D describes the opposite relationship, since increasing distance decreases rather than increases beam intensity.
  186. The correct answer is B (They can produce fluorescence in certain crystals and form a latent image on a receptor): Among their shared fundamental properties, x-rays can cause certain crystals to fluoresce and can form a latent image on a digital receptor or film, which is the basic physical mechanism underlying diagnostic imaging. X-rays travel through air just as they travel through tissue, so option A is false, and photon wavelength does not systematically increase with every tissue interaction as option C claims. X-rays also remain a form of electromagnetic energy throughout their interaction with the patient rather than losing that character entirely.
  187. The correct answer is D (The electrons travel at a faster, more energetic speed toward the anode): The high voltage applied between the cathode and anode accelerates the free electrons across the tube, and increasing the selected kVp produces faster, more energetic electrons striking the target. Electrons do not slow down as kVp rises, nor do they stop moving or decrease in number simply because kVp was increased. This direct relationship between kVp and electron speed is also what shifts the resulting x-ray spectrum toward higher energy photons, as described elsewhere in this chapter.
  188. The correct answer is C (The student is mistaken, since mAs controls quantity, not the beam's penetrating quality): Beam quantity, the number of photons, is controlled primarily by mAs, while beam quality, the penetrating power of the beam, is controlled primarily by kVp and measured with half-value layer. Increasing mAs alone produces more photons at the same energy level, so it does not improve how deeply the beam penetrates the thick abdomen. Option A incorrectly attributes half-value layer control to mAs, and option D is wrong because kVp does directly influence penetration, even though mAs does not.
  189. The correct answer is A (Approximately 10 mGy, since tripling distance reduces intensity to one ninth): Applying I1 multiplied by D1 squared equals I2 multiplied by D2 squared, 90 mGy times 50 centimeters squared equals I2 times 150 centimeters squared, which solves to an I2 of approximately 10 mGy. Because the inverse square law depends on distance squared rather than distance alone, tripling the distance reduces intensity to one divided by three squared, or one ninth, not one third or one half. Intensity clearly changes with distance in this scenario, ruling out option D entirely.
  190. The correct answer is D (The kVp increase also raises beam quantity as a side effect on its own): Although kVp is understood primarily as the quality control, raising kVp also increases beam quantity as a side effect, since more electrons arrive at the target with sufficient energy to produce photons. This means the technologist should anticipate a change in overall photon output as well as penetration, not purely an improvement in quality alone as stated in option A. Option B states the wrong direction of this side effect, and option C overstates the result, since scatter is not eliminated simply by raising kVp.
  191. The correct answer is B (Characteristic spikes should now appear on the spectrum, since 85 kVp exceeds the K-shell threshold): Because tungsten's K-shell binding energy is approximately 69.5 keV, characteristic radiation only appears once kVp rises above roughly 70, so raising the setting from 65 to 85 crosses that threshold and introduces new discrete characteristic spikes onto the spectrum. The original 65 kVp exposure would have shown bremsstrahlung radiation alone, making option A incorrect since the two spectra are not identical. The continuous bremsstrahlung curve remains present alongside the new spikes rather than disappearing, ruling out options C and D.
  192. The correct answer is C (The exposure is roughly four times what it would have been at full distance): Because intensity varies inversely with the square of distance, standing at half the recommended distance means the coworker receives approximately four times the intensity they would have received at the full recommended distance, not simply double or the same amount. A ninefold increase would instead correspond to standing at one third of the recommended distance rather than one half. This scenario illustrates exactly why maintaining full recommended distance during mobile exposures is such an effective and simple radiation protection strategy for bystanders.
  193. The correct answer is A (Below 70 kVp the spectrum loses its characteristic spikes entirely, changing its actual shape): Because characteristic radiation from tungsten requires an incoming electron energy above the K-shell binding energy of roughly 69.5 keV, dropping kVp below approximately 70 removes the discrete characteristic spikes from the spectrum entirely, leaving only the continuous bremsstrahlung curve. This is a genuine change in the spectrum's shape, not merely a scaled-down version of the same curve as the colleague claims. Options B and C describe changes that do not occur at lower kVp, and option D incorrectly denies that any shape change happens at all.
  194. The correct answer is D (A stationary target concentrates the enormous heat load onto one fixed, small area): Since more than 99 percent of electron kinetic energy converts to heat rather than x-rays at the target, a stationary anode would concentrate that entire heat load onto one small, fixed area rather than spreading it across a continuously rotating surface, risking target damage or tube failure. A stationary target does not itself reduce characteristic photon production, eliminate bremsstrahlung, or interfere with thermionic emission at the filament, since those processes depend on electron energy and target composition rather than anode motion.
  195. The correct answer is B (Approximately 64 mGy per minute, since halving distance quadruples the intensity): Applying I1 multiplied by D1 squared equals I2 multiplied by D2 squared, 16 mGy per minute times 60 centimeters squared equals I2 times 30 centimeters squared, which solves to an I2 of approximately 64 mGy per minute. Halving the distance from the source quadruples intensity because the inverse square law depends on distance squared, not a simple doubling as option C suggests. This sharp increase is exactly why maintaining adequate source-to-skin distance is such an important patient dose consideration during fluoroscopic procedures.
  196. The correct answer is B (The photoelectric effect, which fully absorbs the photon and ejects an inner shell electron): the photoelectric effect occurs when a photon is completely absorbed by an inner shell electron, ejecting it and depositing all of the photon's energy in the tissue. Because the photon is absorbed rather than deflected, no scattered photon continues onward, which is exactly what the scenario describes. Compton scattering instead involves an outer shell electron and a deflected, lower energy photon. Coherent scattering involves no ionization and no energy loss at all.
  197. The correct answer is D (The photon is scattered in a new direction while carrying reduced remaining energy): in Compton scattering the photon ejects a loosely bound outer shell electron but is not fully absorbed, instead continuing on in a new direction with less energy than it started with. This scattered photon is what produces most occupational exposure to radiographers, since it can travel toward staff standing nearby. Full absorption with no scattered photon describes the photoelectric effect instead. Re-emission at the same energy with atom-wide vibration describes coherent scattering, not Compton scattering.
  198. The correct answer is A (Coherent scattering, a low energy interaction producing no ionization of the atom): coherent, or classical, scattering occurs when a low energy photon makes the whole atom vibrate briefly, and the photon is then re-emitted with essentially unchanged energy and no ionization at all. This interaction contributes only a small fraction of interactions in the typical diagnostic range. The photoelectric effect and Compton scattering both involve ionization of an electron, while attenuation describes the combined result of all interaction types together, not one single interaction.
  199. The correct answer is C (Photoelectric absorption varies directly with the cube of the atomic number): photoelectric absorption increases dramatically with atomic number, varying with the cube of that number, which is why bone with its higher effective atomic number absorbs far more radiation than soft tissue. This is also why bone appears white on the finished image. Electron density, not atomic number, is what governs Compton interactions instead. Photoelectric absorption is strongly dependent on atomic number, not independent of it or inversely related to it.
  200. The correct answer is B (The gray, representing joules of energy deposited per kilogram of tissue): absorbed dose is measured in gray, abbreviated Gy, and describes the actual energy deposited per unit mass in the absorbing tissue, expressed as joules per kilogram. It is the most direct physical measurement of the four core radiation quantities described in the chapter. Coulombs per kilogram measures exposure in air rather than absorbed dose. The sievert measures dose equivalent, and the roentgen is the older unit for exposure, not absorbed dose.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (One gray equals exactly one hundred rad of absorbed dose): the chapter states plainly that the conversion between the SI and older conventional units for absorbed dose is straightforward, since one gray equals one hundred rad. This same factor of one hundred also applies between sievert and rem for dose equivalent. Radiographers should default to thinking in gray first while recognizing rad values that may still appear on older equipment or records. None of the other listed factors match the stated conversion.
  2. The correct answer is D (The sievert, which weights absorbed dose by radiation type for harm): dose equivalent is measured in sievert, abbreviated Sv, and it adjusts absorbed dose using a radiation weighting factor that reflects how biologically damaging a given radiation type is. This distinguishes dose equivalent from absorbed dose, which is measured in gray and carries no weighting for radiation type. Coulombs per kilogram and the roentgen both describe exposure in air, an entirely separate quantity from dose equivalent.
  3. The correct answer is C (One sievert equals exactly one hundred rem of dose equivalent): just as one gray equals one hundred rad for absorbed dose, the chapter states that one sievert equals one hundred rem for dose equivalent. Recognizing this parallel factor of one hundred helps radiographers convert between SI and older conventional units confidently on either quantity. The other listed factors do not match the conversion given in the chapter for sievert to rem.
  4. The correct answer is A (Coulombs per kilogram, describing charge produced in a mass of air): exposure is measured in coulombs per kilogram, abbreviated C/kg, and it describes the ionization, or electrical charge, produced in a specific mass of air by the radiation beam itself. This makes exposure a measurement of the beam in air rather than a measurement of dose delivered to a patient. The gray measures absorbed dose in tissue, the sievert measures dose equivalent, and the rad is the older unit paired with the gray.
  5. The correct answer is B (The roentgen, the older conventional unit paired with coulombs per kilogram): the chapter identifies the roentgen as the older conventional unit for exposure, paired with the SI unit coulombs per kilogram. The rad pairs with the gray for absorbed dose, and the rem pairs with the sievert for dose equivalent, so neither of those units belongs with exposure. The curie describes source activity, a different quantity not covered as one of the four core measurements in this section.
  6. The correct answer is C (Fluoroscopic equipment reporting air kerma for tracking patient skin dose): the chapter states that air kerma, measured in gray, is now the standard quantity reported by fluoroscopic equipment for tracking patient skin dose. Air kerma represents the kinetic energy released in a small mass of air at a given reference point near the patient. CT dose index, exposure index, and average glandular dose are all separate dose descriptors used with different imaging modalities, not the one linked to air kerma here.
  7. The correct answer is A (30 rad, calculated using the conversion factor of one hundred rad per gray, then rounded): since one gray equals one hundred rad, multiplying 0.30 gray by one hundred gives 30 rad. The gray and rad are never numerically identical, since the conversion factor between them is always one hundred, not one. A conversion factor of ten (3 rad) or an incorrect one-to-one substitution (0.30 rad) would both misstate the true value, as would the tenfold-too-large 300 rad figure.
  8. The correct answer is B (250 rem, using the conversion factor of one hundred rem per sievert): since one sievert equals one hundred rem, multiplying 2.5 sievert by one hundred gives 250 rem. Sievert and rem are never numerically equal to each other, because the conversion factor connecting them is always one hundred rather than one. Applying a factor of ten or one thousand instead of one hundred would both misstate the true dose equivalent value being reported.
  9. The correct answer is C (4.5 gray, dividing the rad value by the correct factor of one hundred): since one gray equals one hundred rad, dividing 450 rad by one hundred gives 4.5 gray. Rad and gray are never numerically identical, so a direct substitution without dividing would misstate the dose. Dividing by ten or by one thousand instead of the correct factor of one hundred would also produce an inaccurate converted value for the recordkeeping system.
  10. The correct answer is B (0.8 sievert, dividing the rem value by the correct factor of one hundred): since one sievert equals one hundred rem, dividing 80 rem by one hundred gives 0.8 sievert. Rem and sievert are never numerically equal, so submitting the raw rem number as if it were sievert would overstate the dose by a factor of one hundred. Dividing by ten (8 sievert) or by ten thousand (0.008 sievert) instead of the correct factor of one hundred would also give an inaccurate converted value.
  11. The correct answer is D (Compton scattering, since it depends on electron density across soft tissue): Compton scattering does not depend meaningfully on atomic number, only on electron density, and it is the dominant interaction across the diagnostic energy range in soft tissue such as the abdominal wall and organs. The photoelectric effect matters more at lower kVp for contrast but is not the dominant soft tissue interaction overall. Coherent scattering is a minor contributor, and attenuation is the combined result of all interactions rather than a single interaction type.
  12. The correct answer is A (The photoelectric effect, which becomes more likely as photon energy decreases): the photoelectric effect is strongly energy dependent, becoming much more likely at lower photon energies, and it is responsible for most of the useful subject contrast seen at lower kVp techniques. Compton scattering actually becomes relatively more significant as energy rises, not as it falls, so it is not the source of this added contrast. Coherent scattering is a minor contributor, and attenuation describes the combined reduction in beam intensity, not one specific interaction.
  13. The correct answer is B (The rib's higher effective atomic number causes far greater photoelectric absorption): photoelectric absorption varies with the cube of atomic number, and bone's higher calcium content gives it a higher effective atomic number than soft tissue, causing dramatically greater absorption and a whiter appearance. Electron density governs Compton interactions rather than this photoelectric contrast effect. Rib thickness and multidirectional scatter are not what the chapter identifies as the reason bone appears whiter on the image.
  14. The correct answer is C (Compton scattering, since scattered photons travel outward in every direction): Compton scatter is the source of most occupational radiation exposure to radiographers, since scattered photons travel away from the patient in all directions and can reach staff standing some distance away. The photoelectric effect absorbs photons entirely and produces essentially no scatter, so it cannot deliver dose this way. Coherent scattering is a minor contributor, and attenuation describes overall beam reduction rather than a mechanism that directs photons toward staff.
  15. The correct answer is A (4 mSv, because the weighting factor for diagnostic x-rays equals one): the chapter states that for diagnostic x-rays the radiation weighting factor is one, so absorbed dose in gray and dose equivalent in sievert end up numerically equal in this specific setting. Applying that factor of one to 4 mGy yields exactly 4 mSv. Weighting factors of ten, one tenth, or one hundred would all misrepresent the true, unweighted relationship that applies to diagnostic x-ray energies.
  16. The correct answer is D (Effective dose, which applies tissue weighting factors across the organs involved): effective dose goes a step further than simple dose equivalent by applying tissue weighting factors that account for how radiosensitive each specific organ is, producing one number representing equivalent whole body risk from an uneven organ dose distribution. Air kerma and exposure both describe the beam itself rather than organ risk. Absorbed dose is a raw, unweighted energy measurement with no organ-specific weighting applied at all.
  17. The correct answer is D (Air kerma, the standard quantity fluoroscopic equipment reports for skin dose): air kerma, measured in gray, is now the standard quantity reported by fluoroscopic equipment for tracking patient skin dose at a defined reference point during a procedure. Effective dose requires combining weighted doses across multiple organs rather than a single running skin dose display. Exposure is expressed in coulombs per kilogram, not gray, and dose equivalent is expressed in sievert, not the gray unit described here.
  18. The correct answer is C (The atom experiences no ionization at all, since none of its electrons are ejected): coherent scattering causes the entire atom to vibrate briefly and re-emits the photon at essentially unchanged energy, with no ionization occurring during the process at all. This distinguishes it from both the photoelectric effect and Compton scattering, which each eject an electron and therefore do ionize the atom. Full absorption with all energy deposited describes the photoelectric effect, not coherent scattering.
  19. The correct answer is A (The pelvis projection, because a thicker part gives photons more chances to interact): attenuation increases with the thickness of the body part being imaged, since a thicker part gives photons more opportunities to interact, so the thicker pelvis attenuates more of the primary beam than the thinner neck. Claiming thinner parts always attenuate more directly contradicts this relationship. Attenuation is not identical across differing thicknesses, and soft tissue does interact meaningfully with the beam, particularly through Compton scattering.
  20. The correct answer is D (Compton scattered photons travel away from the patient in all directions): since scattered photons from Compton interactions travel away from the patient in every direction, staff can be exposed from angles other than directly facing the tube, which is exactly why protective aprons need to shield the body broadly. Photoelectric absorption does not send energy outward at all, since the photon is fully absorbed. Coherent scattering and attenuation do not describe a mechanism that redirects photons specifically toward staff.
  21. The correct answer is C (Exposure, describing ionization produced in a defined mass of air): exposure describes the amount of ionization, or electrical charge, produced in a specific mass of air by the radiation, making it a measurement of the beam itself rather than a measurement of dose delivered to a patient. Absorbed dose and dose equivalent both describe quantities delivered to tissue, not to air alone. Effective dose requires organ-specific weighting across an actual patient, which this air measurement does not involve.
  22. The correct answer is B (The photoelectric effect becomes less likely as photon energy increases, reducing full absorption): the chapter states the photoelectric effect is strongly energy dependent and becomes much less likely as photon energy rises, so fewer photons are fully absorbed and less energy is deposited that way, lowering dose from this mechanism specifically. This is the opposite of claiming higher energy photons are more likely to be absorbed. Compton scattering does not disappear at higher energy, and attenuation does not simply decrease uniformly with rising kVp for every tissue.
  23. The correct answer is C (Compton interactions dominate soft tissue at diagnostic energies and scatter photons outward): Compton scattering is the dominant interaction across the diagnostic energy range in soft tissue and, unlike the photoelectric effect, produces a photon that continues on in a new direction rather than being fully absorbed, which is why it is the source of most occupational exposure. Photoelectric interactions produce essentially no scatter at all. Coherent scattering is a minor contributor, and attenuation is a combined outcome, not an interaction that scatters photons on its own.
  24. The correct answer is B (The weighting factor of one only applies to x-rays, so other radiation types would differ): for diagnostic x-rays the radiation weighting factor is one, which makes absorbed dose in gray and dose equivalent in sievert numerically equal in that specific case, but other radiation types like neutrons or alpha particles carry different weighting factors that would break this equality. Gray and sievert are not universally identical by definition. Gray measures absorbed dose in tissue, not exposure in air, so that option misstates what gray represents.
  25. The correct answer is D (Both patients face a comparable equivalent whole body risk despite differing organ exposure): effective dose applies tissue weighting factors to produce a single number representing equivalent whole body risk from a dose delivered unevenly across different organs, so two very different exposure patterns can still be meaningfully compared this way. It does not imply identical absorbed dose to every organ, since the whole point is weighting across differing organ doses. It also says nothing about matching interaction types or raw air exposure values between the two patients.
  26. The correct answer is D (A thicker part gives photons more chances for Compton interactions, increasing both attenuation and scatter): attenuation increases with part thickness because photons get more opportunities to interact, and since Compton scattering dominates in soft tissue at diagnostic energies, more interactions also means more scattered photons produced and traveling outward toward staff. Claiming Compton scatter stays fixed or decreases with thickness contradicts this relationship. Attenuation and Compton scattering are directly connected mechanisms, not unrelated phenomena occurring separately.
  27. The correct answer is A (Air kerma is measured at a defined reference point, not measured within the patient's tissue): air kerma represents kinetic energy released in a small mass of air at a given reference point, used as a practical surrogate for skin dose, rather than a direct measurement taken within the patient's own tissue. Gray is used for both air kerma and absorbed dose, so it is not exclusive to air alone. These two are related but distinct measurement approaches, and fluoroscopic units display air kerma in gray, not sievert-based readings, for this purpose.
  28. The correct answer is A (Coherent scattering only contributes a small fraction of interactions in the typical diagnostic range): the chapter states coherent scattering contributes only a small fraction of interactions within the typical diagnostic range and becomes more significant only at very low photon energies, so it cannot be treated as equally clinically significant to Compton scattering for occupational risk. Coherent scattering does not absorb the photon, it re-emits it, so that reasoning is also wrong. Compton scattering, not coherent scattering, is the interaction that actually dominates across the diagnostic range.
  29. The correct answer is B (Exposure measures ionization in air; converting to absorbed dose and weighting is required): exposure in coulombs per kilogram measures ionization produced in air by the beam itself, not dose equivalent delivered to tissue, so a badge reading in that unit is not directly the dose equivalent risk without further conversion steps. It is not already dose equivalent, and it can in fact be related to other radiation quantities through established relationships. Exposure and dose equivalent are distinct quantities measured in different units, not identical ones.
  30. The correct answer is A (Coherent scattering becomes more significant only at very low photon energies like mammography uses): the chapter states coherent scattering contributes only a small fraction of interactions within the typical diagnostic range and becomes more significant only at very low photon energies, which matches the low kVp range used in mammography. Compton scattering is not described as becoming more significant at low energies in this way. The photoelectric effect actually becomes more likely, not absent, at lower energies, and attenuation continues to apply across the entire diagnostic energy range.
  31. The correct answer is C (Linear energy transfer, the amount of energy deposited per unit length of travel): LET quantifies how densely a given radiation type ionizes tissue along its path. Alpha particles and neutrons are high LET radiations that deposit energy in a small area, producing more severe biological damage. Diagnostic x-rays are low LET because their energy is deposited more sparsely over a longer path. This distinction is directly tied to the concept of relative biological effectiveness described in the same section.
  32. The correct answer is B (250 kVp x-rays, compared at the dose needed to produce the identical biological effect): Relative biological effectiveness is defined by comparing the dose of a test radiation needed to produce a specific biological effect to the dose of a reference beam of 250 kVp x-rays needed to produce that same biological effect. High LET radiations generally require a smaller absorbed dose than the reference beam to produce the same biological effect, giving them a higher RBE. Low LET diagnostic x-ray beams have an RBE close to the reference standard. This comparison lets researchers express biological damage from different radiation types on a common scale.
  33. The correct answer is A (Cells that divide frequently, are undifferentiated, and have a long dividing future): The law of Bergonie and Tribondeau states that radiosensitivity increases with mitotic rate, decreases with the degree of cellular differentiation, and increases with the length of a cell's future dividing life. Bone marrow stem cells, the intestinal lining, and reproductive cells all fit this profile and are highly radiosensitive as a result. Mature nerve and muscle cells rarely divide and are correspondingly radioresistant. This law forms the conceptual basis for predicting which tissues are most vulnerable to radiation damage.
  34. The correct answer is B (Blood-forming stem cells located within the bone marrow): Bone marrow stem cells divide rapidly and remain relatively undifferentiated, which makes them highly radiosensitive under the law of Bergonie and Tribondeau. This same rapid turnover is why the hematopoietic system is often the first system affected after a significant whole body exposure. Mature nerve, muscle, and cartilage cells divide rarely or not at all and are therefore radioresistant. Recognizing which tissues fit the radiosensitive profile is essential for anticipating where radiation damage will appear first.
  35. The correct answer is C (Mature neurons within the brain, which do not actively divide): Mature nerve cells are highly differentiated and essentially stop dividing after development, placing them among the most radioresistant tissues in the body under the law of Bergonie and Tribondeau. The basal layer of the skin, the intestinal lining, and reproductive cells all divide frequently and remain relatively undifferentiated, making them radiosensitive instead. This contrast is a common way the ARRT exam tests whether a candidate can apply the law rather than just recite it.
  36. The correct answer is D (Approximately 3 to 4 gray delivered to the whole body): Without medical treatment, a single whole body dose of roughly 3 to 4 gray is lethal to about half of an exposed population within 30 days, primarily through damage to the bone marrow. This benchmark is used to compare radiosensitivity across species and to gauge the severity of an acute whole body exposure. Doses in the 6 to 10 gray range instead produce a lethal gastrointestinal syndrome. This LD50/30 figure is one of the most frequently tested numeric values in radiobiology.
  37. The correct answer is D (The hypoxic tumor cells would be measurably less sensitive to the dose): The oxygen effect describes how well-oxygenated cells are more sensitive to radiation damage than poorly oxygenated, hypoxic cells receiving the same dose. Tumors with a poor blood supply often contain hypoxic regions that are comparatively resistant to radiation, which is a significant consideration in radiation therapy planning. Strategies to improve tumor oxygenation before treatment are sometimes used to increase radiosensitivity. This principle explains why hypoxic tumor tissue can be harder to control with a standard radiation dose.
  38. The correct answer is B (Deterministic effects, which have a dose threshold and increasing severity): Deterministic effects, also called tissue reaction effects, occur only once a threshold dose has been exceeded, and their severity then increases with additional dose. Skin erythema, cataracts, epilation, and sterility are all classic examples of deterministic effects. This is the opposite of stochastic effects, which have no threshold and instead show an increasing probability, not severity, as dose rises. Understanding this distinction lets a technologist predict how tissue damage will behave once a known threshold is crossed.
  39. The correct answer is B (A stochastic effect with no dose threshold at all): Stochastic effects such as cancer induction and genetic effects have no dose threshold, meaning any dose, however small, carries some probability of producing the effect. As dose increases, the probability of the effect rises, but the severity of the effect if it occurs stays the same. This all-or-nothing severity model is the reason radiation protection standards assume there is no truly safe dose. Deterministic effects behave differently because they require a threshold dose before any effect appears at all.
  40. The correct answer is B (The skin redness is an early effect and the cataract is a late effect): Early effects appear within minutes to weeks of exposure, and skin erythema is a classic example. Late effects, such as cataracts, may not appear for years or even decades after the exposure that caused them. This timing classification is separate from the deterministic versus stochastic classification, though both erythema and cataracts also happen to be deterministic effects. Recognizing the timing pattern helps explain why some radiation injuries are detected immediately while others surface much later.
  41. The correct answer is C (The acute exposure would generally cause greater harm than the chronic exposure): A large dose delivered acutely, over a short period, is generally far more harmful than the identical total dose delivered chronically, in small fractions spread over a long period. This is because cells have time to repair sublethal damage between fractions of a protracted, chronic exposure. The acute worker's cells receive the full damaging effect at once with little opportunity for repair before the next insult. This principle underlies why occupational dose limits are structured around cumulative exposure over time rather than a single large dose.
  42. The correct answer is D (Approximately 0.5 to 2 gray to the lens of the eye): Cataracts are a deterministic effect of the lens of the eye that can develop after a threshold dose in the range of roughly 0.5 to 2 gray. There is often a latent period of years between the exposure and the point at which the cataract becomes clinically apparent. Because the lens has no mechanism to remove damaged cells, radiation damage tends to accumulate there over time. This makes lens dose an important quantity to monitor for radiation workers and interventional staff.
  43. The correct answer is D (Skin erythema, a reddening of the skin at that site): Skin erythema is a deterministic effect that can appear at doses around 2 gray, making it a recognized risk of high-dose interventional fluoroscopic procedures. Epilation, or hair loss, generally requires a higher dose in the range of 3 to 5 gray before it develops. Because erythema is deterministic, its likelihood and severity are tied directly to the entrance skin dose the patient received. Monitoring peak skin dose during lengthy procedures helps predict and prevent this type of tissue reaction.
  44. The correct answer is C (Epilation, meaning noticeable hair loss at the exposed site): Epilation is a deterministic skin effect that can occur at doses in the range of roughly 3 to 5 gray, higher than the threshold for simple erythema. A peak skin dose of approximately 4 gray falls within this range, making hair loss at the exposed site a realistic concern. Erythema alone typically appears at a lower threshold around 2 gray. Tracking peak skin dose during long interventional procedures is important precisely because these thresholds can be reached with extended fluoroscopy time.
  45. The correct answer is D (The thyroid gland and the breast, both sensitive to cancer induction): The thyroid and the breast are both considered highly radiosensitive with respect to cancer induction, and this risk is particularly pronounced in younger patients. This is one reason shielding and dose reduction techniques for these structures receive special attention during imaging of children and young adults. The liver, pancreas, cartilage, tendons, muscle, and mature nerve tissue are comparatively far less sensitive to radiation-induced cancer. Recognizing which organs carry elevated cancer risk helps guide protective practices in daily imaging work.
  46. The correct answer is D (Temporary sterility, since males can show this effect at low doses): Temporary sterility in males is possible from doses as low as approximately 0.15 gray, making the gonads one of the more sensitive tissues in the body to this specific deterministic effect. Permanent sterility, by contrast, requires several gray in either sex before it occurs. Because temporary sterility is a deterministic effect with a relatively low threshold, it can occur at doses well below those needed to cause other serious injuries. This makes gonadal shielding an important consideration whenever the beam is near the reproductive organs.
  47. The correct answer is A (Its stem cells divide rapidly and remain relatively undifferentiated): The bone marrow's high radiosensitivity is explained by the law of Bergonie and Tribondeau, since its blood-forming stem cells divide rapidly and remain relatively undifferentiated, both of which increase radiosensitivity. Physical location within bone does not protect the marrow from radiation damage, since bone itself is a poor barrier to the x-ray energies used diagnostically. This rapid stem cell turnover is also why hematopoietic syndrome develops at relatively modest whole body doses compared to the other acute radiation syndromes. The reasoning connects a cellular property, not an anatomical one, to the observed sensitivity.
  48. The correct answer is D (The prodromal stage, when nausea and malaise first appear): Acute radiation syndrome begins with a prodromal stage marked by nausea and general malaise, typically within hours of a large whole body exposure. This is followed by a latent stage where the patient may feel temporarily better before the manifest illness stage reveals the true organ damage. Recovery or death then follows depending on the dose received and the organ system most affected. The prodromal stage is often the earliest clinical clue that a significant whole body exposure has occurred.
  49. The correct answer is C (The latent stage, marking a period of temporary improvement): The latent stage of acute radiation syndrome follows the initial prodromal symptoms and is marked by a period where the patient may feel temporarily better even though the underlying organ damage continues to progress. This is followed by the manifest illness stage, when the true extent of organ damage becomes clinically apparent. The length of the latent stage generally shortens as the whole body dose increases. Recognizing this stage is important because clinical improvement does not mean the exposure was minor.
  50. The correct answer is B (The manifest illness stage, when true organ damage appears): The manifest illness stage of acute radiation syndrome is when the true organ damage becomes clinically apparent, including the low blood cell counts described in this scenario. This stage follows the prodromal and latent stages and determines whether the patient ultimately recovers or dies, depending on the dose received and the organ system most affected. Falling white blood cell and platelet counts point specifically toward hematopoietic system involvement. The preimplantation stage is an unrelated concept describing early embryonic development, not a stage of acute radiation syndrome.
  51. The correct answer is C (Approximately 1 gray, reflecting damage to the bone marrow stem cells): Hematopoietic syndrome begins to occur at whole body doses starting around 1 gray, as the rapidly dividing stem cells of the bone marrow are damaged and blood cell production falls. This decline leaves patients vulnerable to infection from low white blood cell counts and to bleeding from low platelet counts. Without treatment, hematopoietic syndrome is the mechanism behind the LD50/30 of approximately 3 to 4 gray. Gastrointestinal syndrome and central nervous system syndrome both require substantially higher whole body doses than hematopoietic syndrome.
  52. The correct answer is A (Falling white blood cell and platelet counts from marrow stem cell damage): Hematopoietic syndrome damages the rapidly dividing stem cells of the bone marrow, causing blood cell production to fall. Reduced white blood cell counts leave the patient vulnerable to infection, while reduced platelet counts lead to easy bruising and uncontrolled bleeding. This combination of infection and bleeding tendency is a hallmark of marrow suppression rather than gastrointestinal or central nervous system involvement. Gastrointestinal syndrome instead produces severe fluid and electrolyte loss from destruction of the intestinal lining.
  53. The correct answer is B (Approximately 6 to 10 gray to the whole body): Gastrointestinal syndrome typically appears at whole body doses in the range of 6 to 10 gray, higher than the threshold for hematopoietic syndrome. At this dose, destruction of the rapidly dividing epithelial cells lining the intestinal wall produces severe fluid and electrolyte loss along with overwhelming infection as the gut lining breaks down. Bone marrow damage from the same dose compounds the gastrointestinal injury, since these doses are also well above the hematopoietic threshold. Death typically occurs within one to two weeks even with aggressive medical support.
  54. The correct answer is B (The gastrointestinal injury occurs alongside compounding bone marrow damage from the same dose): A dose high enough to cause gastrointestinal syndrome, typically 6 to 10 gray, is also well above the threshold for hematopoietic syndrome, so bone marrow damage compounds the intestinal injury rather than occurring in isolation. This combination of failing immune defenses and a breached gut lining makes overwhelming infection and fluid loss especially difficult to manage. Death typically occurs within one to two weeks even with aggressive medical support because both systems are failing simultaneously. This is why gastrointestinal syndrome carries a far worse prognosis than hematopoietic syndrome alone.
  55. The correct answer is D (Approximately 50 gray or more to the whole body): Central nervous system syndrome generally requires extremely high whole body doses cited above 50 gray, far exceeding the thresholds for hematopoietic and gastrointestinal syndrome. At this dose level, direct damage to blood vessels and neural tissue causes cerebral edema and vascular collapse. Death typically occurs within hours to a few days, making this the fastest and most severe of the three acute radiation syndromes. The extremely high threshold explains why central nervous system syndrome is rarely seen outside of catastrophic radiation accidents.
  56. The correct answer is D (Direct damage to blood vessels and neural tissue causes rapid cerebral edema and collapse): At doses above 50 gray, central nervous system syndrome causes direct damage to blood vessels and neural tissue, producing cerebral edema and vascular collapse that leads to death within hours to a few days. This is a fundamentally different mechanism from the slower, cell-turnover-driven failure seen in hematopoietic and gastrointestinal syndrome, which is why the clinical course is so much faster despite the far higher dose threshold. The extreme dose required also explains why this syndrome is the least commonly encountered of the three. Its speed and severity make it the deadliest of the acute radiation syndromes.
  57. The correct answer is C (Either loss of the embryo or complete compensation with no lasting defect): During the preimplantation stage, roughly the first two weeks after conception, the prevailing model is all-or-none, meaning a significant radiation dose either kills the embryo outright or the surviving cells compensate completely with no lasting defect. Congenital malformation is instead the primary concern during the later organogenesis stage, when organs are actively forming. Growth retardation and central nervous system effects, including mental retardation, become concerns even later, during the fetal stage. This staged pattern of risk is a key reason gestational timing matters when assessing exposure to a pregnant patient.
  58. The correct answer is A (The organogenesis stage, roughly weeks two through eight): The organogenesis stage, occurring roughly during weeks two through eight of gestation, is when the developing organs are forming and therefore represents the period of greatest risk for radiation-induced congenital malformations. The preimplantation stage that precedes it instead follows an all-or-none pattern, while the fetal stage that follows shifts the risk toward growth retardation and central nervous system effects. The neonatal stage occurs entirely after birth and is not part of prenatal radiosensitivity staging at all. Recognizing which gestational stage an exposure falls within is essential for counseling a pregnant patient.
  59. The correct answer is A (Growth retardation and central nervous system effects rather than organ malformation): During the fetal stage, from about week eight to term, the risk shifts away from congenital malformation and toward growth retardation and central nervous system effects. The all-or-none pattern described for option B applies only to the much earlier preimplantation stage, and the organ malformation risk described in option C is greatest during the organogenesis stage that precedes the fetal stage. The fetus is by no means free of radiosensitivity at this later stage, since stochastic risks such as childhood cancer remain present throughout pregnancy. Understanding this shift helps guide counseling for exposures occurring later in gestation.
  60. The correct answer is A (Roughly week eight through week fifteen of gestation): The window from roughly week eight to week fifteen of gestation carries the highest risk of radiation-induced mental retardation and microcephaly, since this period falls within the fetal stage when the central nervous system is especially vulnerable to a substantial dose. The first two weeks after conception instead follow the all-or-none preimplantation pattern rather than a mental retardation risk. Later gestational windows, closer to term, carry a comparatively lower risk of this specific central nervous system effect. This narrow high-risk window is a frequently tested detail in obstetric radiation safety.
  61. The correct answer is C (No, the stochastic risk of childhood cancer is present at any dose and any gestational age): Throughout every stage of pregnancy, the stochastic risk of childhood cancer from in utero exposure remains present at any dose and at any gestational age, since stochastic effects have no threshold. This is precisely why radiographers ask every patient of childbearing age about the possibility of pregnancy before proceeding with an examination. Deterministic effects such as malformation or growth retardation do have thresholds and vary by gestational stage, but the stochastic cancer risk does not follow that same pattern. This distinction is an important reason pregnancy screening is treated as a routine safety step rather than an occasional precaution.
  62. The correct answer is A (Alpha particles have high LET and deposit energy more densely, causing more severe DNA damage): Alpha particles are a high LET radiation, meaning they deposit energy densely along a short path, and this dense ionization is more likely to cause irreparable damage to DNA. Because RBE compares biological damage against a reference beam at the same absorbed dose, this greater DNA damage translates directly into a higher RBE for alpha particles than for the comparatively low LET diagnostic x-ray beams. Low LET x-rays deposit their energy more sparsely along a longer path, producing comparatively less severe damage per unit dose. This relationship between LET and RBE is a core concept linking the physical and biological sides of radiobiology.
  63. The correct answer is A (Improving tumor oxygenation before treatment to increase radiosensitivity): Because well-oxygenated cells are measurably more sensitive to radiation damage than hypoxic cells, strategies that improve tumor oxygenation before treatment can increase the radiosensitivity of otherwise resistant hypoxic tumor tissue. Reducing blood flow further would move in the wrong direction, worsening hypoxia and radioresistance rather than improving it. This principle is a direct application of the oxygen effect described in cell survival theory and matters directly in radiation therapy planning. Addressing tumor oxygenation is one practical way clinicians work around the natural resistance of poorly perfused tumor regions.
  64. The correct answer is C (Birth defects require a threshold dose while cancer risk has no threshold): Congenital malformation is a deterministic effect that requires a threshold dose to occur, while cancer induction is a stochastic effect with no dose threshold at all, meaning even a very small dose carries some nonzero probability of contributing to it. This is exactly the distinction the ARRT exam expects a candidate to draw between deterministic and stochastic effects. A single diagnostic chest radiograph delivers a very low dose, making a birth defect extremely unlikely given the deterministic threshold model, while a small stochastic cancer risk technically remains present. Explaining this difference accurately helps patients understand why radiation risk communication uses two different models rather than one.
  65. The correct answer is C (The patient who received approximately 8 gray, within the gastrointestinal range): Gastrointestinal syndrome typically occurs at whole body doses in the range of 6 to 10 gray, placing the patient who received approximately 8 gray squarely within that range. The patient who received approximately 2 gray falls instead within the hematopoietic syndrome range, which begins around 1 gray, while the patient who received approximately 60 gray falls within the central nervous system syndrome range, cited above 50 gray. Matching each patient's dose to the correct syndrome threshold is essential for anticipating which organ system will dominate that patient's clinical course. This kind of dose-to-syndrome matching is a common way the exam tests synthesis of the acute radiation syndrome material.
  66. The correct answer is C (Between the patient/grid and the image receptor, so they sample radiation before it strikes the receptor): AEC ionization chambers sit between the patient (below the grid) and the image receptor so they can measure the exit radiation reaching that point and terminate the exposure once enough has accumulated to properly expose the receptor. Placing them inside the tube housing or in front of the collimator would not let them sense radiation that has passed through the patient at all. A chamber mounted on the collimator near the light field would only detect visible light, not exit radiation. This positioning is what allows AEC to guard against both overexposure and underexposure when the patient is centered correctly.
  67. The correct answer is B (Dose will increase in direct proportion to the percentage increase in mAs used): mAs controls the total number of photons produced, so patient dose rises directly and proportionally with any mAs increase while beam quality stays the same. A lower kVp on a future exposure has no bearing on the dose delivered by this repeat. Dose is never unchanged when mAs changes, since mAs is the primary quantity control for photon output. Detector efficiency at the receptor does not reduce the dose the patient absorbed reaching that point.
  68. The correct answer is D (It reduces the volume of tissue irradiated and the amount of scatter produced): restricting the beam to only the anatomy of interest shrinks the tissue volume exposed and cuts down scatter production, which lowers both patient dose and dose to nearby staff. Collimation does not change photon energy, since that is controlled by kVp rather than field size. It does not directly change the mAs needed to satisfy AEC unless the field size change affects which chamber is covered. Collimation also does not alter how efficiently the ionization chambers themselves detect radiation.
  69. The correct answer is A (About double the dose of a single correctly exposed image of that view): a repeat exposure at the same technique factors delivers a second full dose to the patient, so the total for that projection is roughly twice what a single correctly captured image would have delivered. Dose from the first exposure does not disappear or get replaced by the second one, so the two doses add together. AEC does not reduce dose on a repeat simply because a prior attempt existed. Motion itself does not automatically raise mAs on a repeat unless the technologist deliberately changes the technique.
  70. The correct answer is D (Use an appropriate immobilization device instead of having anyone hold the child): immobilization devices eliminate motion without putting anyone in the room during the exposure, which is the preferred solution whenever one is available and clinically appropriate. Allowing a parent to hold the child without a protective apron would needlessly expose that family member. Raising kVp dramatically is not a substitute for proper immobilization and can distort the technique unnecessarily. Staff members, not only parents, are also protected by this same preference for devices over manual holding.
  71. The correct answer is C (Higher kVp and higher mAs to achieve adequate tissue penetration): a larger body habitus needs a beam with more penetrating power and more photons overall, so both kVp and mAs are typically raised above standard chart values. Lowering either factor would worsen penetration and likely produce a nondiagnostic, underexposed image. Leaving mAs unchanged while only adjusting kVp downward moves in the wrong direction entirely. AEC can help select an appropriate exposure time but does not remove the need to manually set higher baseline kVp for adequate penetration.
  72. The correct answer is A (Proceed with the exam using tight collimation and shielding where appropriate): once clinical necessity is confirmed and the exam cannot be avoided, the appropriate response is to proceed while applying tight collimation and shielding to minimize dose rather than refusing a medically indicated study. Automatically cancelling a confirmed necessary exam denies the patient needed care and is not the technologist's role once necessity is established. Doubling technique factors would increase fetal dose with no diagnostic benefit. Skipping collimation entirely removes one of the most effective dose reduction tools available for this exact situation.
  73. The correct answer is B (Decreased subject contrast as beam penetration and scattered radiation both increase): raising kVp increases beam penetration, which lowers dose but also reduces the differential absorption between tissues, producing lower subject contrast along with more scatter reaching the receptor. Quantum mottle is more associated with too few photons at low mAs, not with the higher kVp change described here. Focal spot size is a tube design property unrelated to the kVp selected for an exposure. Exposure latitude on digital receptors is a detector characteristic, not something that shrinks specifically because kVp was raised.
  74. The correct answer is B (The exposure will terminate too early or run too long, requiring a repeat): if the selected chamber is not under the anatomy of interest, it senses the wrong tissue density and either ends the exposure prematurely or lets it continue too long, producing an under or overexposed image that needs to be repeated. The exposure indicator reflects whatever radiation the sensed chamber received, so it will not read correctly for the intended anatomy. Collimator field size is set manually by the technologist and does not respond automatically to chamber selection. kVp is likewise a manual preset and is not adjusted automatically by the AEC system.
  75. The correct answer is C (A total filtration requirement of 2.5 millimeter aluminum equivalent): NCRP Report 102 sets 2.5 millimeter aluminum equivalent as the minimum total filtration for a general purpose radiographic unit operating above 70 kVp, combining inherent tube filtration with added aluminum filters. A value of 1.0 or 1.5 millimeter aluminum equivalent is too low to meet this minimum for that kVp range. A value of 3.5 millimeter aluminum equivalent exceeds the required minimum and is not the standard cited figure. Lower minimums are permitted only at lower operating kVp ranges, not at this one.
  76. The correct answer is A (Reduced skin and superficial tissue dose, since those photons add no useful image): low energy photons would otherwise be absorbed in the patient's skin and superficial tissue without contributing any useful information to the image, so filtering them out directly lowers that dose. Filtration actually narrows the energy spectrum and tends to reduce contrast slightly rather than increase it. Exposure time to reach AEC termination depends on mAs and detector sensitivity, not on filtration itself. Filtration removes weak photons and does not add penetrating power for dense structures beyond raising the average beam energy modestly.
  77. The correct answer is B (Beam hardening, a shift toward higher average photon energy): removing the softest, lowest energy photons from the beam raises its average energy, and this shift is called beam hardening. Collimation refers to restricting the beam's field size, not changing its energy spectrum. Attenuation describes the general reduction of beam intensity as it passes through matter, a broader concept than this specific spectral shift. Scatter describes photons that have changed direction after interacting with matter, which is unrelated to the filtration process being described here.
  78. The correct answer is C (Lower mAs can be used since the detector converts photons more efficiently): a higher DQE means the detector converts incoming photons into usable signal more efficiently, so fewer photons and therefore lower mAs can produce an equivalent diagnostic image, lowering patient dose. Higher DQE moves technique in the opposite direction from needing more photons. DQE is directly tied to dose efficiency, not only to contrast resolution, so technique absolutely can and should change. A higher DQE detector is more sensitive, not less, so raising kVp to compensate for sensitivity would be the wrong direction entirely.
  79. The correct answer is A (Wide exposure latitude can hide overexposure since the image can still look acceptable): digital detectors have wide exposure latitude, so an overexposed image can still display with acceptable brightness and contrast, which is exactly why the technologist must actively check the exposure indicator rather than judging technique from image appearance alone. AEC helps terminate exposure but does not guarantee the technique selected was appropriate in the first place. The exposure indicator is precisely the tool that caught this problem, so it is reliable and should be checked routinely. Image appearance is the less reliable indicator in this scenario, not the more accurate one.
  80. The correct answer is D (To support quality assurance and regulatory compliance regarding patient dose): recording the exposure index for each image supports facility quality assurance programs and documents compliance with dose related regulatory requirements. This indicator reflects radiation reaching the receptor for that patient's exposure, not the technologist's own personal occupational dose. It does not automatically program future patients' technique factors, since each patient still requires individualized technique selection. Visual review of the image for diagnostic quality remains a separate, still necessary step regardless of what the exposure index shows.
  81. The correct answer is B (Dose will increase because higher mAs is needed to compensate for grid absorption): a grid absorbs some of the primary beam along with scatter, so mAs must be raised to compensate, which increases patient dose without a meaningful image quality benefit on a thin body part. A grid does not absorb scatter before it reaches the patient, since scatter is produced within the patient's own tissue during the exposure. Grids change the technique needed to reach the receptor, so patient dose is directly affected, not left unchanged. Grids increase, rather than decrease, the exposure time or mAs needed to compensate for the radiation they absorb.
  82. The correct answer is C (Improved contrast in exchange for increased patient dose from higher mAs needed): grids absorb scatter to improve image contrast, but because they also absorb some primary beam, higher mAs is required to compensate, raising patient dose in exchange for that contrast improvement. Grid use is never free of a dose cost, so the first option describing no cost is inaccurate. Grids improve rather than reduce contrast, so describing reduced contrast paired with lower dose reverses the actual trade-off. The trade-off centers on contrast and dose, not primarily on spatial resolution or exposure time.
  83. The correct answer is D (A grid is unnecessary here since it would raise dose without meaningful benefit): a thin body part like a pediatric hand does not generate enough scatter to justify the dose increase that grid use requires, so skipping the grid is the appropriate choice. Using a grid on every body part regardless of thickness ignores the actual clinical justification needed for the added dose. An adult protocol requirement has no bearing on a pediatric hand exam, which follows its own appropriate technique. Thinner body parts generally benefit less from grid use, not more, since they produce less scatter to begin with.
  84.
