A value of 3.2 millisievert is the dose already received, not the remaining amount available. A value of 0.5 millisievert is instead the separate monthly fetal dose limit, unrelated to this specific remaining-total calculation. A value of 5.0 millisievert is the full pregnancy goal itself, not the amount still remaining after 3.2 millisievert has already been received.
  176. The correct answer is C (Proper labeling of radioactive material to identify its contents and hazard clearly): proper labeling is a required part of handling radioactive material, ensuring anyone who encounters it can clearly identify its contents and the hazard it presents. Radioactive material must be stored separately from general biohazardous waste, not combined together for convenience. Radioactive material cannot be disposed of as ordinary waste immediately, since it must first decay in storage or otherwise be confirmed safe. Access must be restricted to appropriately trained and cleared staff, not left unrestricted to any staff member in the department.
  177. The correct answer is D (Secured storage behind adequate shielding to limit exposure and prevent loss): radioactive material must be kept in secured storage behind adequate shielding, which both limits accidental exposure to staff and prevents unauthorized loss or theft of the material itself. An unlocked cabinet accessible to any staff member fails to meet the security requirement this material demands. Room temperature control alone does not address either the shielding or security requirements this material actually needs. Labeling alone, without secured and shielded storage, does not satisfy the full requirement for proper radioactive material handling.
  178. The correct answer is C (Decay in storage until radioactivity drops to a safe level before disposal): short-lived radioactive materials undergo decay in storage, where they are held securely until radioactivity drops to a safe level, at which point they can be released and disposed of as ordinary waste. Immediate disposal without any decay period would expose waste handlers to radiation still present in the material. Returning material to the manufacturer is not the standard practice described for handling short-lived materials after clinical use. Diluting the material with water does not reduce radioactivity and is not a recognized or safe disposal practice for this purpose.
  179. The correct answer is D (Documented survey and wipe testing to confirm no contamination remains): documented survey and wipe testing confirm that no residual contamination remains present before an area or material is released from radioactive material control, providing objective evidence rather than relying on assumption. A visual inspection alone cannot detect radioactive contamination, which is not visible to the eye. A fixed 24 hour waiting period does not itself confirm the absence of contamination without an actual survey being performed. Reviewing original labeling documentation confirms what the material was, not whether the area is now actually free of contamination.
  180. The correct answer is B (Report the finding to the radiation safety officer for proper identification and handling): reporting an unlabeled suspected radioactive container to the radiation safety officer ensures it is properly identified, surveyed, and handled by someone with the appropriate training and equipment. Disposing of it in general waste risks releasing radioactive material into an uncontrolled waste stream. Opening the container to inspect it visually risks direct exposure without knowing what protective measures are actually needed. Relabeling it based on a guess could lead to improper handling or disposal based on inaccurate information about its actual contents.
  181. The correct answer is A (A calculated lifetime cumulative dose limit of 45 rem): applying the conventional unit formula of 1 rem per year of age, a 45-year-old radiographer's lifetime cumulative dose limit calculates to 45 rem, matching the SI equivalent of 450 millisievert calculated with the other version of this formula. A value of 4.5 rem would only result from dividing rather than directly multiplying age by the correct factor of 1 rem per year. A value of 450 rem is one hundred times too large for this specific conventional unit calculation. A value of 90 rem would only result from using double the correct per-year multiplier for this formula.
  182. The correct answer is A (Visitor B, whose estimated annual dose is listed in the table): the table shows Visitor B with an estimated annual dose of 1.3 millisievert, which exceeds the 1 millisievert annual public exposure limit that applies to non-occupationally exposed individuals. Visitor A shows an estimated annual dose of 0.4 millisievert, well within the public limit for that individual. Visitor C shows the lowest estimated annual dose of the group, at 0.2 millisievert, also within the limit. Visitor D shows an estimated annual dose of 0.8 millisievert, which remains under the 1 millisievert public limit as well.
  183. The correct answer is B (ALARA governs personnel dose, while exposure factor selection and collimation govern patient dose): ALARA is the guiding philosophy for keeping occupational dose to personnel as low as reasonably achievable, while patient dose is managed through separate principles including exposure factor selection, filtration, and collimation. The first option reverses this actual relationship between the two concepts. ALARA and patient dose reduction are not simply the same concept under two different names, since ARRT explicitly distinguishes the two. Both ALARA and exposure factor selection have clearly defined, well established roles within this content area, contrary to the fourth option.
  184. The correct answer is C (No, ALARA specifically describes personnel protection, even though collimation also lowers patient dose): while collimation genuinely lowers patient dose, ALARA as a named term specifically describes personnel protection under ARRT's own guidance, so the action should be described using patient dose reduction terminology rather than being labeled ALARA. ALARA does not apply broadly to any dose reduction for any person, since it is specifically tied to occupational, not patient, protection. Collimation absolutely does reduce patient dose, so the second option's premise is factually incorrect. This terminology distinction applies regardless of patient population, not only for pediatric or pregnant patients specifically.
  185. The correct answer is D (Tight collimation to the smallest field size that still covers the anatomy of interest): tight collimation reduces the volume of tissue irradiated, lowering patient dose, and it simultaneously reduces the amount of scatter produced, which lowers occupational dose to anyone remaining in the room. Increasing mAs raises patient dose rather than reducing it, moving in the wrong direction for this goal. Removing a grid regardless of body part thickness can degrade image quality without a reliable, universal dose benefit and does not directly reduce scatter to personnel. Minimizing source-to-skin distance is not itself a recommended dose reduction practice and does not achieve the dual benefit described in this scenario.
  186. The correct answer is B (ALARA applies to personnel protection, while separate principles govern patient dose): keeping ALARA and patient protection conceptually separate is repeatedly identified as the most important distinction in this content area, since blending the two together is one of the most common mistakes students make on this exact topic. Grids are situationally appropriate rather than never appropriate, so a blanket prohibition misstates their actual clinical role. Distance is one of three methods, not the only one, alongside time and shielding, that meaningfully reduce occupational dose. Filtration is a patient dose reduction tool rather than the primary method for reducing occupational dose to staff.
  187. The correct answer is C (Month 2, the second month shown in the table): the table shows Month 2 with a reading of 0.6 millisievert, which exceeds the 0.5 millisievert monthly fetal dose limit that applies once a worker has formally declared her pregnancy. Month 1 shows a reading of 0.3 millisievert, comfortably within the monthly limit for that period. Month 4 shows the lowest reading of the four months, at 0.2 millisievert, well within the monthly limit. Month 3 shows a reading of 0.4 millisievert, which also remains under the 0.5 millisievert monthly limit.
  188. The correct answer is A (Patient dose uses exposure factor and collimation, personnel dose uses ALARA through time, distance, shielding): patient dose is minimized through exposure factor selection, collimation, filtration, and related principles, while personnel dose is minimized through ALARA applied via time, distance, and shielding, keeping these two halves of the framework correctly matched. Both halves are not governed by a single shared ALARA principle, since ALARA specifically applies to personnel protection rather than patient dose. The third option reverses which set of principles applies to which half of the framework. Both patient dose and personnel dose are clearly addressed by named, well established guiding principles throughout this chapter, contrary to the fourth option.
  189. The correct answer is A (The worker age 30 with a career dose of 310 millisievert shown in the table): a 30-year-old worker's lifetime limit is 30 multiplied by 10 millisievert, or 300 millisievert, and a career dose of 310 millisievert exceeds that individually calculated limit. The 40-year-old worker's limit is 400 millisievert, so a career dose of 380 millisievert remains under that worker's own limit. The 50-year-old worker's limit is 500 millisievert, so a career dose of 480 millisievert also remains under that limit. The 25-year-old worker's limit is 250 millisievert, so a career dose of 200 millisievert stays well under that worker's own calculated limit.
  190. The correct answer is B (A technologist briefly places a gloved hand in the primary beam to steady a cassette): placing any part of the body, including a gloved hand, directly within the primary beam violates the rule that no one should ever be in the primary beam, regardless of how brief the exposure or how well protected the hand appears to be. Stepping behind the control booth barrier keeps the technologist outside the primary beam entirely, which is the correct and recommended practice. Wearing an apron while standing outside the primary beam path does not violate this rule, since the person remains outside the beam itself. Retreating two meters before a mobile exposure also keeps the technologist outside the primary beam, applying the distance principle correctly.
  191. The correct answer is B (Receptor exposure doubles because mAs has a direct straight line relationship with photon output): mAs is the product of mA and exposure time, and it controls how many x-ray photons the tube produces. This relationship is directly proportional, so doubling mAs doubles the number of photons reaching the image receptor. mAs does not change photon energy, so it has no effect on beam penetration, contrast, or distortion. Radiographers rely on this predictable direct relationship whenever a simple receptor exposure adjustment is needed.
  192. The correct answer is D (Milliampere-seconds, since mAs changes photon quantity only and leaves beam energy unchanged): mAs controls only the number of photons produced at the tube, not their energy. Because contrast is governed primarily by photon energy and the resulting scatter production, adjusting mAs alone changes receptor exposure while leaving contrast and gray scale untouched. kVp, by contrast, changes both exposure and contrast simultaneously. This makes mAs the correct tool whenever exposure needs to move without disturbing the existing contrast.
  193. The correct answer is A (It sets photon energy and penetrating power, governing how much beam reaches the receptor): kVp controls the kinetic energy given to electrons accelerated across the x-ray tube, which determines the energy of the resulting photons. Higher energy photons penetrate the patient more efficiently, so more of them reach the image receptor. Because kVp changes photon energy rather than photon quantity, it simultaneously affects receptor exposure, image contrast, and subject contrast. This three-way effect is what makes kVp selection more consequential than a simple mAs adjustment.
  194. The correct answer is C (Receptor exposure approximately doubles, similar to doubling the mAs instead): the fifteen percent rule states that raising kVp by about fifteen percent produces roughly the same increase in receptor exposure as doubling the mAs would. This is why the standard dose reduction technique pairs a fifteen percent kVp increase with cutting mAs in half, keeping receptor exposure essentially constant while lowering overall patient dose. The rule is an approximation used for quick technique estimation at the console. It is not intended to replace a full exposure calculation for large technique changes.
  195. The correct answer is B (92 kVp and 20 mAs, raising kVp about fifteen percent and halving mAs): the standard dose reduction technique raises kVp by approximately fifteen percent, close to 92 kVp from a starting point of 80 kVp, while simultaneously cutting mAs in half. The fifteen percent kVp increase approximately doubles receptor exposure, and halving the mAs cancels that increase out, so receptor exposure stays essentially the same. Overall patient dose still drops because fewer total photons are needed to reach that same receptor exposure. Raising kVp without lowering mAs at all raises dose rather than lowering it, and lowering kVp while raising mAs moves both factors in the wrong direction for dose reduction.
  196. The correct answer is A (Both rows equal 20 mAs, so receptor exposure will be essentially identical between them): mAs is the product of mA and time, and row 1 gives 200 times 0.1 for 20 mAs while row 2 gives 400 times 0.05, also 20 mAs. Since kVp is identical in both rows and receptor exposure is governed by the mAs value rather than the individual mA and time settings, the two combinations deliver the same receptor exposure. This reciprocal mA-time relationship lets a technologist shorten exposure time to reduce motion by raising mA proportionally. Distance is not needed for this comparison since it is unchanged between rows.
  197. The correct answer is D (Image contrast decreases, producing a longer gray scale with more shades of gray): higher kVp produces higher energy photons that generate more Compton scatter relative to primary photons reaching the receptor. This added scatter fogs the image, reducing the difference between light and dark areas and stretching the visible gray scale across more shades. Lower kVp techniques produce comparatively less scatter and therefore shorter, higher contrast gray scales. This inverse relationship between kVp and contrast is a core reason fixed kVp technique charts exist.
  198. The correct answer is C (Subject contrast decreases because a higher energy beam is absorbed less selectively by dense tissue): at higher kVp, photons carry enough energy to penetrate dense structures like bone almost as easily as soft tissue, reducing the differential absorption between the two. This lowers subject contrast, the true underlying difference in tissue attenuation, distinct from image contrast which also involves scatter and display processing. Lower kVp techniques rely more heavily on the photoelectric effect, which is highly sensitive to atomic number and produces stronger subject contrast. This is why bone detail studies traditionally use comparatively lower kVp technique.
  199. The correct answer is B (Receptor exposure is cut in half because mAs drops from 10 to 5): the original technique of 200 mA at 0.05 seconds equals 10 mAs. Halving the exposure time to 0.025 seconds while leaving mA at 200 produces 200 times 0.025, or 5 mAs, exactly half the original value. Because receptor exposure is directly proportional to mAs, this technique change cuts receptor exposure in half unless mA is compensated upward. To restore the original 10 mAs at the shorter time, the technologist would need to raise mA to 400.
  200. The correct answer is A (Receptor exposure remains constant because the mAs product is unchanged by the trade): since receptor exposure depends on the mAs value rather than on the specific mA and time settings individually, raising mA while proportionally lowering time in a way that keeps mAs fixed leaves receptor exposure unchanged. This reciprocal trade is the standard method for reducing motion blur on a moving patient without sacrificing the exposure the technique chart requires. The tube's heat loading is a separate consideration that limits how high mA can practically go. This relationship is tested frequently because it separates true exposure control from simple factor substitution.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (All three rows produce essentially the same receptor exposure since each equals 30 mAs): calculating mA times time for each row gives 300 times 0.10, 600 times 0.05, and 150 times 0.20, all equal to 30 mAs. Receptor exposure tracks the combined mAs value rather than either factor individually, so all three techniques deliver essentially equivalent receptor exposure at the same kVp. A technologist would choose among these rows based on motion control needs or tube heat loading rather than expected exposure differences. This reciprocal behavior is the practical basis for adjusting mA and time together to manage motion.
  2. The correct answer is C (A smaller focal spot size, since it reduces penumbra around fine detail): focal spot size affects spatial resolution exclusively, with no influence on receptor exposure, contrast, or distortion. A smaller focal spot produces less penumbra, the blurred edge surrounding a structure caused by the finite size of the x-ray source, which sharpens recorded detail. A larger focal spot tolerates higher heat loading for thick body parts but sacrifices some of this sharpness. Because the question specifies exposure and contrast must stay unchanged, focal spot size is the only factor that isolates a resolution improvement.
  3. The correct answer is B (Spatial resolution, since movement blurs the fine detail recorded during the exposure): motion, like focal spot size, isolates a single image quality property. Movement during the exposure smears the recorded position of anatomic structures over the exposure time, degrading spatial resolution and fine detail sharpness. It does not change how much radiation reaches the receptor, nor does it alter beam energy or the true geometric relationships that produce contrast or distortion. This is why immobilization and short exposure times, rather than mAs or kVp adjustments, are the correct tools for controlling motion blur.
  4. The correct answer is D (Beam intensity is highest at the cathode end of the field, not the anode end): because the anode surface is angled, photons produced near the anode side of the field must pass through more anode material before exiting, absorbing some of that radiation internally. This makes beam intensity lower toward the anode end and higher toward the cathode end. Understanding this asymmetry lets a technologist position anatomy to take advantage of it. The anode heel effect changes only receptor exposure distribution across the field, not contrast, resolution, or distortion.
  5. The correct answer is C (The thicker superior spine should align with the cathode end, where intensity is higher): the anode heel effect makes beam intensity naturally higher toward the cathode end of the field and lower toward the anode end. Positioning the thicker part of the anatomy toward the cathode end takes advantage of that stronger beam intensity to help penetrate the denser tissue, evening out density across the finished image. If the thicker anatomy were placed toward the anode end instead, that region would receive comparatively less intensity and could appear underexposed relative to the thinner portion. This principle applies to any anatomy with a significant thickness taper along the long axis of the field.
  6. The correct answer is A (The thicker proximal hip should be positioned toward the cathode end of the tube): beam intensity is higher toward the cathode end of the field because of the anode heel effect, so aligning the thicker, more attenuating anatomy with that stronger intensity helps offset the extra tissue it must penetrate. Placing the thick hip toward the anode end instead would compound underexposure in that already denser region. The anode heel effect becomes more pronounced at shorter SID and with larger field sizes, not less. This positioning strategy is a standard technique adjustment for any anatomy with significant thickness variation along the cathode-anode axis.
  7. The correct answer is B (Approximately 32.4 mAs, calculated as 10 times 72 squared over 40 squared): the inverse square law states that receptor exposure falls off with the square of the distance from the source. Applying the formula, new mAs equals 10 times (72 squared divided by 40 squared), which is 10 times (5184 divided by 1600), or 10 times 3.24, giving 32.4 mAs. Simply doubling the distance without this compensation would cut receptor exposure to roughly one quarter of the original rather than keeping it constant. This calculation is one of the most frequently tested numeric relationships on the exam.
  8. The correct answer is D (Receptor exposure, spatial resolution, and distortion, through distance, penumbra, and magnification effects): SID governs receptor exposure through the inverse square law, since intensity falls off with the square of the distance traveled. It also affects spatial resolution, because a longer SID reduces the penumbra created by the finite focal spot size, sharpening the image. Finally, SID affects distortion, specifically magnification, since a longer SID produces less magnification of the imaged part. This three-way influence makes SID one of the more consequential geometric factors a technologist selects.
  9. The correct answer is C (The 72 inch SID projection, since longer SID reduces penumbra around structures): a longer source-to-image-receptor distance narrows the geometric penumbra created by the finite size of the focal spot, producing a sharper recorded image. The shorter 40 inch SID projection, by contrast, produces a wider penumbra and comparatively more blur around fine edges. This is one reason chest radiography, which prioritizes maximum detail, traditionally uses a 72 inch SID rather than a shorter tabletop distance. OID also affects sharpness but does not change the direction of this particular comparison since it was not varied between the two projections.
  10. The correct answer is B (Approximately 9.8 mAs, calculated as 5 times 56 squared over 40 squared): applying the inverse square law formula, new mAs equals old mAs times the new distance squared divided by the old distance squared. That gives 5 times (3136 divided by 1600), or 5 times 1.96, which equals 9.8 mAs. This value keeps receptor exposure essentially constant despite the longer distance, since the loss in intensity from moving farther away is compensated by the increased mAs. Failing to make this adjustment when changing SID between rooms is a common source of underexposed repeats.
  11. The correct answer is A (Magnification decreases as SID increases because the ratio of SID to SOD approaches one): magnification is calculated as SID divided by source-to-object distance, or SOD. As SID increases while OID, and therefore the difference between SID and SOD, stays fixed, the ratio of SID to SOD moves closer to one, producing less magnification. This is why chest radiography intentionally uses a long 72 inch SID, minimizing cardiac magnification for accurate heart size assessment. OID does not automatically change just because SID was increased in this scenario.
  12. The correct answer is D (SID changes receptor exposure through the inverse square law, while OID does not directly change it): SID governs receptor exposure because it is the full distance the beam travels from source to receptor, making the inverse square law directly applicable. OID, the distance from the object to the receptor, instead affects contrast through the air-gap effect: increasing OID actually causes more scattered photons to diverge away from the receptor and miss it, typically improving rather than degrading contrast, while it separately affects spatial resolution through increased penumbra and distortion through increased magnification, without the same direct exposure relationship SID has. This distinction is why the two distances, though geometrically related, require separate mental models when evaluating an image. Confusing them is a common source of missed boards questions on geometry.
  13. The correct answer is C (Magnification increases because a greater OID enlarges the structure's projected shadow on the receptor): as an object moves farther from the receptor while SID stays fixed, the divergent x-ray beam projects a progressively larger shadow of that object, increasing magnification. This is a direct geometric consequence of the beam's diverging path from a single focal spot. This same relationship is why the standard rule is to keep OID as small as possible for every projection unless magnification is being deliberately used. Focal spot size affects sharpness, not the amount of magnification produced by a given OID.
  14. The correct answer is A (The larger OID let scattered photons diverge away from the receptor, improving contrast, the air-gap effect.): increasing the object-to-image-receptor distance while SID stays fixed lets scattered photons produced in the patient diverge away from the receptor and miss it, the well-known air-gap effect. Because less scatter reaches the receptor, image contrast improves rather than degrades, independent of mAs, kVp, or SID. This is a real clinical technique, used as an alternative to a grid in projections such as the lateral cervical spine. Subject contrast, the true tissue absorption difference, is not directly changed by OID at all.
  15. The correct answer is B (When magnification of a small structure, such as fine vasculature, is the specific diagnostic goal): the general rule to keep OID as small as possible exists to minimize unwanted magnification, blur, and contrast loss. The recognized exception is a deliberate magnification technique, where increasing OID intentionally enlarges a small structure, such as a blood vessel in an angiographic study, to make it easier to evaluate. Outside of this specific diagnostic purpose, a larger OID still increases magnification and reduces sharpness through greater penumbra, and while the air-gap effect can incidentally reduce scatter reaching the receptor as OID increases, that trade-off against increased blur and magnification is why intentional magnification technique remains the primary tested reason to deliberately increase OID beyond the routine minimum. Patient motion, dose reduction, and fixed SID are unrelated to the decision to intentionally magnify.
  16. The correct answer is D (Approximately 5.6 centimeters, based on a magnification factor of roughly 1.11): the magnification factor equals SID divided by SOD, which here is 40 divided by 36, or approximately 1.11. Multiplying the true object size of 5 centimeters by this magnification factor gives roughly 5.56 centimeters, the approximate projected size on the receptor. This calculation demonstrates why even a modest OID produces a measurable increase in apparent size, not just a decrease in sharpness. Recognizing this formula lets a technologist predict how much a measurement taken directly from an image will overstate true anatomic size.
  17. The correct answer is C (SOD equals 40 inches, giving a magnification factor of approximately 1.1): SOD is calculated as SID minus OID, which here is 44 minus 4, equal to 40 inches. Dividing SID by SOD gives the magnification factor: 44 divided by 40, or approximately 1.1. This means any structure imaged under these geometric conditions will appear roughly ten percent larger than its true size on the receptor. Recognizing SOD as a distinct calculated value, rather than confusing it with SID or OID directly, is essential for solving any magnification problem correctly.
  18. The correct answer is A (Foreshortening, since angulation away from the receptor plane shortens the projected image): foreshortening occurs when a structure is angled such that its true length is not fully represented in the plane parallel to the receptor, causing it to project shorter than its actual anatomic length. This is a form of shape distortion, distinct from size distortion, or magnification, which affects the whole structure uniformly rather than compressing it along one axis. Elongation is the opposite effect, stretching a structure longer than its true length. Correct positioning and central ray alignment are the primary tools for preventing both foreshortening and elongation.
  19. The correct answer is B (Elongation, which stretches the structure's projected appearance beyond its true length): elongation is a form of shape distortion where angulation of the tube, part, or receptor stretches a structure's projected image longer than its true anatomic length, the opposite effect from foreshortening. Because this distortion comes from misalignment rather than from distance alone, it is corrected through proper central ray and part positioning rather than through any exposure factor adjustment. Magnification, by contrast, enlarges a structure uniformly in every dimension rather than stretching it disproportionately along one axis. Quantum mottle is a noise phenomenon unrelated to geometric positioning entirely.
  20. The correct answer is C (Receptor exposure, spatial resolution, and distortion are all affected by the misalignment): tube, part, and receptor angulation together change the actual path length the beam travels through the patient, which affects receptor exposure, alter the geometric relationships that determine sharpness, affecting spatial resolution, and directly create shape distortion through foreshortening or elongation. This three-category effect distinguishes angulation from a factor like focal spot size, which the table shows influences spatial resolution only. Because angulation touches so many categories at once, correcting a misaligned image usually requires repositioning rather than simply adjusting an exposure factor.
  21. The correct answer is D (Approximately 6.2 mAs, calculated as 20 times 40 squared over 72 squared): applying the inverse square law formula, new mAs equals old mAs times the new distance squared divided by the old distance squared, which is 20 times (1600 divided by 5184), or 20 times approximately 0.309, giving roughly 6.17 mAs. Because the new distance is shorter than the original, the required mAs decreases substantially rather than increasing, since the receptor is now closer to the source and intercepts a more concentrated beam. Failing to reduce mAs when moving to a shorter distance risks a significantly overexposed image. This inverse relationship works in both directions, whether distance increases or decreases from baseline.
  22. The correct answer is A (Receptor exposure and contrast remain unchanged since focal spot size only affects resolution): focal spot size is one of the exposure geometry factors that isolates a single image quality property, spatial resolution, without touching receptor exposure or contrast. Because mAs, kVp, and SID were all held constant in this scenario, the number of photons reaching the receptor and the beam energy producing scatter are both unaffected by the focal spot switch. The only measurable change will be sharper recorded detail from reduced penumbra. This isolation is exactly why focal spot size is a useful tool when only resolution needs improving.
  23. The correct answer is A (Image contrast improves, but receptor exposure drops and technique must increase): the lead strips absorb scatter before it reaches the receptor, cleaning up image contrast. Those same strips also absorb some of the useful primary beam along with the scatter. This lowers receptor exposure compared with a non-grid exposure of the same technique. A technique increase, guided by the grid conversion factor, restores adequate receptor exposure.
  24. The correct answer is D (The number of times the non-grid mAs must be multiplied for an equivalent grid technique): a grid absorbs part of the primary beam along with scatter, so a non-grid technique alone would underexpose the receptor. The grid conversion factor tells the technologist exactly how many times to multiply the non-grid mAs to reach an equivalent grid technique. Higher grid ratios remove more scatter and carry a larger conversion factor. The factor is not related to lead strip count, dose percentage, or distance.
  25. The correct answer is A (20 mAs, since the approximate grid factor for an 8:1 ratio is about four times): an 8:1 grid ratio carries an approximate grid conversion factor of roughly four times the non-grid technique. Multiplying the original 5 mAs by this factor gives 5 times 4, or 20 mAs. This matches the direction of the grid conversion table, where higher ratios require progressively larger multipliers. Using a factor of two, three, or eight would either underexpose or overexpose the receptor.
  26. The correct answer is C (18 mAs, using a grid conversion factor of roughly six times for this grid): the table lists a 16:1 grid ratio with an approximate grid conversion factor of six times the non-grid mAs. Multiplying the original 3 mAs by six gives 3 times 6, or 18 mAs. A factor of three, four, or eight belongs to a different, lower grid ratio row in the table. Selecting the wrong factor would produce a receptor exposure that is too low or too high.
  27. The correct answer is D (24 mAs, found by multiplying 12 by the ratio of six to three): a 6:1 grid carries an approximate conversion factor of three, while a 16:1 grid carries an approximate factor of six. Switching between two grid ratios requires multiplying the old mAs by the new factor divided by the old factor, or 12 times six divided by three. That calculation equals 12 times two, or 24 mAs. Using the ratios five to three or eight to three does not match either grid's actual conversion factor from the table.
  28. The correct answer is A (It removes the lowest-energy photons, raising the average energy of the remaining beam): added filtration absorbs the lowest-energy photons that would otherwise be absorbed by the patient's skin without ever contributing to the image. Removing those weak photons hardens the beam, raising the average energy of what remains. This is a patient dose safety benefit as much as an image quality effect. The aluminum does not remove high-energy photons, tube-housing scatter, or convert one radiation type into another.
  29. The correct answer is C (2.5 millimeter aluminum equivalent, once the unit operates above 70 kVp): a fixed x-ray unit must carry at least 2.5 millimeters of total aluminum equivalent filtration, combining the tube's inherent filtration with any added filtration, once it is operated above 70 kVp. This minimum protects the patient by removing low-energy photons that would otherwise be absorbed by the skin without reaching the image. A total of 1.0 or 1.5 millimeters falls short of this regulatory minimum, and 4.0 millimeters is more than what is required.
  30. The correct answer is A (Subject contrast decreases and the image gray scale becomes longer): additional filtration hardens the beam, and the more uniform photons that remain penetrate tissue with less variation between different tissue densities. This lowers subject contrast and produces a longer gray scale, in the same direction as raising kVp. Receptor exposure actually drops slightly rather than rising, since some photons are removed. Spatial resolution is a geometry-related property and is not what added filtration primarily affects.
  31. The correct answer is D (Average photon energy increases, producing a harder, more penetrating beam): as the table shows, half-value layer rises alongside total filtration, and a rising half-value layer directly indicates a harder, more penetrating beam. Filtration removes the lowest-energy photons first, so the photons that remain in the beam carry a higher average energy. This is the same beam-hardening effect that lowers subject contrast and lengthens gray scale. Average photon energy does not decrease or become unpredictable as filtration increases.
  32. The correct answer is A (Less scatter is produced within the patient, improving image contrast): a smaller irradiated tissue volume means fewer atoms are available to generate scatter radiation within the patient. With less scatter reaching the receptor, image contrast improves, since scatter is what fogs and flattens contrast. Receptor exposure from scatter also drops somewhat rather than rising. Collimation primarily affects scatter and contrast, not spatial resolution directly.
  33. The correct answer is C (Make a small increase in mAs to compensate for the reduced scatter contribution): very tight collimation removes so much scatter that receptor exposure can drop enough to occasionally require a small technique bump. A small mAs increase directly restores adequate receptor exposure without undoing the contrast benefit collimation just provided. Decreasing kVp, adding a grid, or increasing SID are larger and unrelated changes that do not address this specific small exposure shortfall efficiently.
  34. The correct answer is A (Ascites, which adds fluid density within the abdominal cavity): ascites is classified as an additive pathologic condition because the excess fluid adds tissue density that must be penetrated, requiring a technique increase. Emphysema, osteoporosis, and a resolving fracture are all destructive conditions instead, since each one removes or thins tissue and lowers the attenuation the beam must overcome. Confusing an additive condition with a destructive one leads to the wrong direction of technique adjustment.
  35. The correct answer is B (Emphysema, which destroys alveolar tissue and lowers overall tissue density): emphysema is classified as a destructive pathologic condition because it destroys alveolar tissue and lowers the overall density the beam must penetrate, requiring a technique decrease. Pneumonia, a plaster cast, and edema are all additive instead, since each one adds fluid or material density that requires a technique increase. Applying a decrease to an additive condition would leave the image underexposed.
  36. The correct answer is A (Receptor exposure decreases and internal scatter production increases): the larger, denser patient absorbs more of the primary beam before it can reach the receptor, so receptor exposure falls unless technique is increased to compensate. At the same time, the greater tissue volume produces more internal scatter, which degrades image contrast further. Both factors move in the same direction here, not toward staying unchanged or both decreasing together.
  37. The correct answer is B (Select the shortest practical exposure time to minimize motion blur): motion degrades spatial resolution regardless of its cause, and the most direct countermeasure is shortening exposure time so the moving part travels less distance during the exposure. Increasing kVp changes contrast rather than addressing motion, adding a grid addresses scatter rather than motion, and increasing SID addresses magnification rather than motion blur. None of those three options directly targets the tremor itself.
  38. The correct answer is C (Weigh both factors together, since the destructive lung change may partly offset the added size): body size and pathology are two separate patient factors that both affect required technique, and when they push in opposite directions the technologist must reason about their combined net effect rather than applying either adjustment in isolation. Ignoring either factor entirely risks a technique that is too high or too low. Defaulting to a maximum backup setting skips the reasoning the situation actually requires.
  39. The correct answer is B (The abdominal projection needs an increase while the hip projection needs a decrease): the table classifies ascites as additive, requiring a technique increase for the abdominal projection, while osteoporosis is classified as destructive, requiring a technique decrease for the hip projection. Treating both projections identically, in either direction, ignores that these two conditions belong to opposite classifications. Reading each row correctly for its own body region is what the table is meant to support.
  40. The correct answer is D (It keeps contrast more consistent since kVp, the primary contrast driver, never changes): because kVp is the primary driver of image contrast, holding it fixed for a given body part means contrast stays consistent across patients of different thicknesses, with only mAs adjusted to compensate for thickness. Patient dose, exposure time, and spatial resolution are not the primary advantage this charting system is built around. Consistent contrast is the specific trade-off a fixed kVp chart is designed to preserve.
  41. The correct answer is A (Lower dose is possible on thinner parts, but contrast is less consistent between exams): letting kVp move with part thickness allows lower kVp, and often lower dose, on thinner parts, but since kVp also drives contrast, contrast becomes less consistent from one exam to the next. This is the opposite trade-off of a fixed kVp system, which sacrifices some dose flexibility for consistent contrast. Grid use and exposure time are not what this charting choice primarily determines.
  42. The correct answer is B (It loads a preset kVp, mA, and time based on average patient measurements): anatomically programmed technique loads a preset combination of kVp, mA, and time drawn from average patient measurements for the selected body part and projection. The technologist then fine-tunes that preset using an actual caliper measurement of the specific patient's part thickness. The generator does not automatically select a grid ratio, adjust filtration, or lock out manual adjustment afterward.
  43. The correct answer is C (mAs alone, while leaving kVp exactly as listed on the chart): a fixed kVp system compensates for changes in part thickness, including thickness driven by an elevated body mass index, by adjusting mAs alone while kVp stays at the chart's preset level for that body part. Adjusting kVp instead, or adjusting both factors together, belongs to a variable kVp system rather than this one. SID is a geometry factor and is not how thickness compensation is handled on a technique chart.
  44. The correct answer is B (Fixed kVp, because keeping kVp constant preserves the primary driver of contrast): since kVp is the primary driver of image contrast, a fixed kVp system directly matches a department's priority of consistent contrast across a wide range of patient sizes, because kVp never changes for a given body part. A variable kVp system would work against that priority, since kVp shifting with thickness is exactly what makes contrast less consistent. The reasoning about dose minimization or mAs having no effect on contrast does not correctly justify the choice.
  45. The correct answer is B (A fixed kVp system, since kVp stays constant while only mAs changes): the table shows kVp remaining at 70 across all three thicknesses while mAs rises steadily as thickness increases, which is the defining pattern of a fixed kVp technique chart. A variable kVp system would show kVp changing across the rows as well. Nothing in the table indicates a caliper-based preset system or an automatic termination system, since both mAs values are fixed chart entries rather than device-terminated exposures.
  46. The correct answer is D (A variable kVp system, accepting less consistent contrast for lower dose on thin parts): the table shows both kVp and mAs changing together as part thickness increases, which is the defining pattern of a variable kVp technique chart. This approach allows lower kVp, and lower dose, on the thinnest part shown, but accepts that contrast will be less consistent across the three exposures since kVp itself is changing. A fixed kVp system would hold kVp constant across every row instead.
  47. The correct answer is B (The variable kVp system, since its kVp changes across every row shown): the table shows the variable kVp column's kVp value rising from 64 to 80 across the three thicknesses, while the fixed kVp column holds kVp at 70 throughout. Since kVp is the primary driver of contrast, the system whose kVp is changing is the one most likely to produce less consistent contrast across these exposures. Contrast is not determined only by collimation and grid use, and mAs alone changing does not have the same direct effect on contrast that changing kVp does.
  48. The correct answer is C (Roughly 50 to 60 percent above the baseline technique): a dry plaster cast is highly additive and typically requires mAs to be increased by roughly 50 to 60 percent above the baseline non-cast technique to adequately penetrate the added density. An increase of only 10 to 30 percent would likely leave the image underexposed given how attenuating plaster actually is. Doubling the technique to 90 or 100 percent above baseline goes further than this material typically requires.
  49. The correct answer is D (Little to no adjustment, since fiberglass attenuates far less than plaster): because fiberglass attenuates the beam far less than plaster does, a fiberglass cast typically needs little to no technique adjustment, unlike the roughly 50 to 60 percent increase plaster requires. Fiberglass is not denser than plaster and does not require a larger increase, and it does not remove tissue density the way a destructive pathologic condition would. Applying the plaster adjustment to a fiberglass cast would overexpose the image.
  50. The correct answer is D (Lower mAs, often higher kVp, and a very short exposure time): pediatric patients generally need lower mAs and often higher kVp, combined with a very short exposure time, since children's tissues are thinner and their movement is unpredictable. A longer exposure time in either of the other options increases the risk of motion blur regardless of the mAs or kVp chosen. Higher mAs with lower kVp moves technique in the wrong direction for a thinner, harder-to-immobilize pediatric patient.
  51. The correct answer is C (Reduced technique along with extra time allowed to help prevent motion): geriatric patients with reduced bone density from osteoporosis usually need reduced technique, while limited mobility separately calls for extra time allowed during positioning to help prevent motion, not necessarily a longer exposure time itself. Increasing technique moves in the wrong direction for a destructive condition like osteoporosis. Reducing technique while making no accommodation at all for mobility ignores the second patient factor entirely.
  52. The correct answer is A (A technique increase, similar in direction to an additive pathologic condition): barium and iodinated contrast media increase the attenuating density of the anatomy being imaged, and this typically requires a technique increase similar in direction to an additive pathologic condition. A technique decrease would move in the wrong direction for material that adds rather than removes density. Contrast media does attenuate the beam significantly, and the needed increase applies whether or not a grid happens to also be used.
  53. The correct answer is C (Layer both compensations on top of the chart-based starting technique together): grid and object-to-image distance compensations are each applied on top of a chart-based starting technique, and when both changes occur together, both compensations must be layered onto that starting technique rather than choosing only one. OID changes do affect required technique, since increased distance affects receptor exposure. A technique chart's baseline values do not already account for a grid change or an OID change made afterward.
  54. The correct answer is D (Use a larger combined increase than either single factor's adjustment alone would suggest): since ascites and iodinated contrast media both independently add attenuating density and both call for a technique increase, their combined effect on required technique is greater than what either single factor's adjustment alone would suggest. The two factors reinforce rather than cancel each other, since both push in the same direction. Assuming they rarely occur together ignores the very clinical scenario the question describes.
  55. The correct answer is B (Fine-tune the preset technique using the actual caliper measurement obtained for this patient): anatomically programmed technique loads a preset based on average patient measurements, but the technologist is expected to fine-tune that preset using a caliper measurement of the actual patient's part thickness before exposure. Accepting the preset unchanged ignores a real measured difference for this specific patient. Disabling the feature permanently or switching to a different exposure mode entirely is a larger response than this single measurement discrepancy calls for.
  56. The correct answer is B (It terminates the exposure once a preset amount of radiation reaches the detector cells): AEC uses a timer circuit tied to the active ionization chamber or solid-state cells, ending the exposure automatically once sufficient receptor exposure has accumulated. The technologist still manually selects kVp, mA, and which cell or cells to activate before the exposure begins. AEC does not choose technique factors, calculate dose, or move the detector cells on its own.
  57. The correct answer is D (The kVp, the mA station, and which detector cell or cells to activate): AEC only automates the termination of exposure time, so the technologist still selects kVp, mA station, and the active cell configuration for every AEC exposure. Exposure time and total mAs are values AEC itself determines once these selections are made. The backup timer is a safety maximum, not a routine per-exposure selection, and the exposure indicator target is a manufacturer-set reference value, not something chosen per exposure.
  58. The correct answer is A (The center cell alone, the two outer cells alone, or all three cells together): Most AEC units offer exactly these three activation patterns, and the technologist chooses the combination that places active cells directly under the anatomy of clinical interest. The two outer cells together are commonly used for chest projections so the mediastinum shadow does not fall only on the center cell. A generator does not select cells randomly, and locking all three cells active with no other option is not how standard AEC units are designed.
  59. The correct answer is C (Receptor exposure stays essentially constant because AEC still terminates at the same preset value): AEC terminates the exposure once a fixed quantity of radiation reaches the active cells regardless of which mA station produced it, as long as the resulting exposure time does not fall below the minimum response time. Only exposure time changes with the mA station selected, not the receptor exposure itself. Receptor exposure does not double, halve, or become unpredictable purely from changing mA under normal AEC operation.
  60. The correct answer is B (Exposure time shortens and patient dose can decrease while receptor exposure barely changes): Because AEC cuts off the exposure as soon as the preset receptor exposure is detected, a higher kVp reaches that threshold sooner, shortening exposure time and often lowering patient dose. This differs from manual technique, where raising kVp directly raises receptor exposure since nothing terminates the exposure early. AEC does respond to kVp changes, so exposure time does not lengthen or stay fixed in this scenario.
  61. The correct answer is D (The heart and great vessels will be underexposed because the low-density lungs end the exposure early): When lung field rather than the mediastinum lies over the active cells, the AEC senses the comparatively low-density lung tissue and terminates the exposure before the denser mediastinal structures receive adequate exposure, producing gross underexposure of the heart and great vessels. AEC cannot compensate for this kind of positioning error since it only responds to the tissue actually covering the active cells, and backup time is a maximum safety limit, not the cause of this outcome.
  62. The correct answer is A (Confirm the patient is centered so the active outer cells fall over the mediastinum): The underexposure occurs because the active cells are sensing low-density lung tissue instead of the denser mediastinum, so the real fix is accurate centering that keeps the correct anatomy over the selected cells. Raising backup time only changes the safety maximum and does not correct which tissue the cells are sensing. Switching cell configuration alone does not help if centering is still wrong, and lowering kVp would make the AEC terminate even sooner, worsening the underexposure.
  63. The correct answer is D (Each increment changes the target receptor exposure by roughly 25 to 50 percent): A plus one or minus one setting on the exposure adjustment control typically shifts the AEC's target receptor exposure by about 25 to 50 percent, letting the technologist compensate for tissue that would otherwise fool the system into an image that is too light or dark. This control does not adjust backup time in seconds, SID, or the mA station directly, but instead changes the exposure level the AEC circuitry is aiming for.
  64. The correct answer is B (The plus one setting successfully raised the receptor exposure into the acceptable target range): The deviation index moved from a strongly negative value, indicating significant underexposure from the thinner mastectomy tissue fooling the AEC, to a value near zero after the plus one adjustment, which is the accepted target range for most systems. This confirms the density control correction worked as intended rather than overcorrecting or being unrelated to the adjustment made, and nothing in the resulting value suggests overexposure requiring a lower kVp repeat.
  65. The correct answer is A (It limits the maximum exposure delivered if the cells fail to terminate properly): Backup time or backup mAs protects the patient and the x-ray tube by capping the exposure at a maximum value regardless of what the active cells detect, which matters if a positioning error or equipment malfunction prevents normal termination. It does not select cell configuration, calculate an exposure indicator, or increase receptor exposure for thicker patients, since those functions belong to other parts of the AEC system.
  66. The correct answer is D (The image will be overexposed because the circuitry cannot terminate the exposure fast enough): When the mA station selected is too high for a thin body part, the required exposure time can fall below the system's minimum response time, meaning the AEC circuitry physically cannot cut off the exposure quickly enough once the preset receptor exposure is reached, resulting in overexposure. AEC systems do not automatically switch mA stations mid-exposure, this limitation applies to any patient regardless of age, and it produces overexposure rather than underexposure.
  67. The correct answer is C (The image will likely be significantly overexposed since the full backup mAs was delivered): When dense prosthetic hardware over the active cell prevents the AEC from sensing enough transmitted radiation to terminate normally, the exposure runs until the backup time cuts it off, delivering the full backup mAs and typically producing significant overexposure. The image is not perfectly exposed in this scenario, the exposure does not terminate almost instantly, and the AEC does not silently correct a misread cell value on its own.
  68. The correct answer is B (The center cell alone, since the vertebral bodies lie centrally within the lateral projection): For a lateral lumbar spine, the vertebral bodies and disc spaces of interest sit centrally within the patient, so activating the center cell alone places it directly under that anatomy for accurate termination. Lateral projections do not automatically favor the outer cells, and activating all three regardless of anatomy risks the outer cells sensing soft tissue rather than the spine. AEC can be used effectively for this projection, so manual technique is not required.
  69. The correct answer is A (The configured backup mAs setting is within the 600 mAs regulatory maximum for this unit): Federal performance standards cap backup mAs at 600 mAs for radiographic units operating at or above 50 kVp, and the 550 mAs setting shown in the table falls under that limit, so the unit is compliant. The setting does not exceed the maximum, the 600 mAs limit applies to units at or above 50 kVp rather than below it, and a federal maximum for backup mAs does in fact exist.
  70. The correct answer is D (Select a cell configuration that avoids placing the dense prosthetic hardware over the active cell): Since the dense prosthetic hardware over the active cell is preventing normal termination and driving the exposure to backup time, choosing a cell configuration that keeps the prosthesis off the active cell addresses the actual cause rather than just working around the symptom. Increasing backup time would make any future overexposure even worse, and changing the mA station does not fix a cell being fooled by dense hardware, so abandoning AEC entirely is not necessary once cell selection is corrected.
  71. The correct answer is C (The detector element, or DEL, the physical sensing unit of the flat panel array): Each individual sensing unit within a flat panel detector array is called a detector element, or DEL, and smaller DELs with tighter pitch and higher fill factor produce higher spatial resolution. Pixel pitch measures the distance between DEL centers rather than being the sensing unit itself, and fill factor describes what proportion of a DEL is actually radiation-sensitive. Nyquist frequency describes a resolution limit, not a physical structure within the detector.
  72. The correct answer is A (2.5 line pairs per millimeter, since Nyquist frequency equals half the sampling frequency): Nyquist frequency is defined as one half of the sampling frequency set by pixel pitch, so a sampling frequency of 5 line pairs per millimeter yields a Nyquist frequency of about 2.5 line pairs per millimeter. It is not equal to double or the same as the sampling frequency, and it is not simply one fifth of that value. Any spatial frequency finer than the Nyquist limit risks aliasing artifacts in the resulting digital image.
  73. The correct answer is B (A larger matrix divides the same field of view into smaller pixels, improving resolution): For a fixed field of view, increasing the matrix size means the same anatomic area is divided into more, smaller pixels, and smaller pixels are what improves spatial resolution. The field of view itself is not changed by matrix size in this comparison, matrix size does have a direct relationship to pixel size, and bit depth relates to contrast resolution rather than the spatial resolution improvement shown here.
  74. The correct answer is D (How faithfully the system reproduces true object contrast as spatial frequency increases): MTF measures how accurately a detector system reproduces the true contrast of an object as the spatial frequency, or fineness of detail, increases, with a value closer to 1 indicating better fidelity and sharper images. It is not a measure of patient dose, the number of displayable gray shades, or the percentage of scatter a grid removes, since each of those is described by a different imaging parameter entirely.
  75. The correct answer is C (Detector A will more efficiently capture incoming photons, improving overall image quality): A higher fill factor means a greater proportion of each DEL's surface is actually sensitive to radiation rather than wasted on wiring, allowing the detector to capture incoming photons more efficiently and improving image quality for a given exposure. This does not lower spatial resolution or reduce the bit depth needed, and fill factor is a real, measurable performance factor rather than something with no effect when DEL size is equal.
  76. The correct answer is A (It converts incoming photons into a usable, low-noise signal more efficiently than a lower DQE detector): DQE measures how efficiently a detector converts incoming x-ray photons into a usable, low-noise signal, and a higher DQE means diagnostic quality images can be produced using fewer photons, directly lowering patient dose. A higher DQE detector requires less dose, not more, and DQE does not automatically change bit depth. DQE is closely tied to dose efficiency, not something unrelated to it.
  77. The correct answer is B (The 12-bit setting, because far more gray shades allow subtler density differences to be shown): Bit depth determines the number of gray shades each pixel can represent, and a 12-bit system offers far more gray shades than an 8-bit system, allowing far subtler tissue density differences to be displayed. Fewer gray shades at 8-bit actually make subtle differences harder, not easier, to detect. Bit depth directly affects contrast resolution rather than only file size, so the two settings are not identical in this respect.
  78. The correct answer is D (It allows equivalent diagnostic images to be produced using a lower patient dose): Since a higher DQE detector converts incoming photons into a usable signal more efficiently, it can achieve the same diagnostic image quality using fewer photons, which translates directly into a lower required patient dose. DQE is not primarily about matrix size, dynamic range, or maximum bit depth, even though those are other real detector characteristics; its defining practical benefit is dose efficiency for equivalent image quality.
  79. The correct answer is A (A focused grid uses angled lead strips, while a virtual grid relies on post-processing): A focused grid is a physical device with lead strips angled to match beam divergence at a specific SID, while a virtual grid achieves a similar visual effect through software processing rather than a physical grid. This description is reversed in option B, both grid types are not physically identical, and a focused grid does physically remove scatter that a virtual grid cannot, since virtual processing cannot remove scatter never eliminated at the time of exposure.
  80. The correct answer is C (Virtual grid processing cannot remove scatter that was never eliminated at the time of exposure): Because a virtual grid applies its effect through post-processing rather than physically absorbing scatter before it reaches the detector, it cannot remove scatter radiation that was already recorded in the raw image data, which is the key limitation for a scatter-heavy exam like a large abdomen. Virtual grids do not necessarily increase dose more than a physical grid, they are supported on many current digital systems, and they do not automatically adjust exposure factors during the exposure itself.
  81. The correct answer is B (The span of exposure the detector can meaningfully record without losing usable image data): Dynamic range describes the span of exposure values a detector can meaningfully record, and digital receptors have a far wider dynamic range than film ever did, letting them tolerate a much broader range of technique errors. This is distinct from spatial resolution, the total DEL count across the array, and the percentage of scatter removed by a grid, each of which describes a different detector characteristic entirely.
  82. The correct answer is D (Digital detectors have a much wider dynamic range, tolerating a broader range of technique errors): Digital receptors have a far wider dynamic range than film ever offered, allowing them to record a much broader span of exposure values while still producing an image that appears visually acceptable, even when the underlying technique was off. Digital dynamic range is wider, not narrower or identical to film, and this characteristic is specifically about exposure tolerance rather than spatial resolution, which is a separate detector property.
  83. The correct answer is B (CMOS sensors generally use less power, while CCD sensors have historically offered somewhat better image quality.): CCD and CMOS are two competing sensor technologies for converting light or charge into a digital signal, and CMOS generally uses less power while CCD has historically offered somewhat better image quality, though both remain in current clinical use. CCD sensors are not obsolete, CMOS sensors are actually attractive for portable units due to their lower power draw, and the two technologies are not electrically identical.
  84. The correct answer is C (Too few photons reached the detector, producing quantum mottle and a lower signal-to-noise ratio): A grainy, random appearance from an underexposed technique is quantum mottle, caused by too few photons reaching the detector to form a statistically reliable image, and it corresponds to a lower signal-to-noise ratio. Excessive kVp and grid cutoff produce different artifact patterns entirely, and a matrix size mismatch would cause a different kind of image problem, not this photon-quantity-driven graininess.
  85. These are genuinely separate characteristics, not the same underlying property, and bit depth relates to contrast resolution rather than patient dose, while DQE is a real and measurable detector performance metric.

Digital images absolutely can still be underexposed after processing, monitor brightness is not directly proportional to true receptor exposure, and this behavior is precisely what makes digital different from film, which did look pale when underexposed.
