  116. The correct answer is B (It converts alternating current into direct current so electrons flow only from cathode to anode): Rectification changes AC to DC because the x-ray tube requires electrons to travel in one direction to strike the anode target properly. Without rectification the tube would receive alternating current, which would send electrons in both directions and could damage the filament and anode. Transformers handle voltage changes separately through electromagnetic induction, and filtration and AEC serve entirely different purposes in the imaging chain. Rectification therefore protects tube function while enabling the controlled electron flow x-ray production depends on.
  117. The correct answer is D (Voltage pulsates with large dips between pulses, so the tube rarely operates near peak kilovoltage): A single-phase generator produces a pulsating waveform with substantial voltage drops between each pulse, meaning the tube spends much of the exposure operating below its peak kilovoltage setting. This differs sharply from three-phase and high-frequency generators, which smooth the waveform and keep voltage much closer to a constant peak. The large dips reduce average beam energy and efficiency compared to newer generator types. This waveform behavior is why single-phase equipment is now considered outdated for most clinical imaging.
  118. The correct answer is A (Both produce a nearly constant voltage output with minimal ripple compared to single-phase units): Three-phase generators and high-frequency generators both smooth the voltage waveform so the tube operates much closer to peak kilovoltage throughout the exposure, unlike single-phase units with their large pulsating dips. High-frequency generators achieve this by converting incoming current to a very high frequency before rectification, producing especially low ripple. Neither generator type eliminates rectification, and neither changes how exposure termination or anode design works. This shared low-ripple behavior is why both produce more consistent image quality than single-phase equipment.
  119. The correct answer is C (The exposure could be completed in a shorter time while producing more consistent image quality): High-frequency generators keep voltage almost constant near peak kilovoltage throughout the exposure, so the same mAs can be delivered more efficiently and in less time than with a single-phase unit. This near-constant output also produces more consistent density and contrast than the pulsating waveform of single-phase equipment. Kilovoltage selection is not inherently reduced by generator type, and spatial resolution relates to detector conversion method rather than generator phase. Shorter, more efficient exposures are one of the practical clinical advantages of high-frequency technology.
  120. The correct answer is B (They prevent selection of a technique that would overload and damage the anode with excessive heat): Tube rating charts define the maximum safe combinations of kVp, mA, and exposure time for a given tube and generator, based on how much heat that combination would generate in the anode. Selecting a technique beyond those limits risks overheating and physically damaging the target or the tube housing. Dose calculation, grid selection, and postprocessing display adjustments are handled by entirely separate systems and software. Respecting the rating chart protects equipment longevity as well as patient safety during the exposure.
  121. The correct answer is A (The high-frequency waveform stays near peak voltage, so anode heat accumulates faster): Because a high-frequency generator keeps voltage much closer to peak kilovoltage throughout the exposure, a given mAs technique delivers more actual energy to the anode per unit of time than the same technique run on a single-phase unit. A tube's rating chart is built around the waveform of the generator it is paired with, so a chart calibrated for single-phase pulsation does not correctly predict heat loading on a high-frequency waveform. Rotating anodes work with high-frequency generators, and inherent filtration is unrelated to generator phase. This is exactly why matching tube rating charts to the correct generator type matters clinically.
  122. The correct answer is D (The single-phase waveform causes larger kilovoltage swings between pulses, changing contrast exposure to exposure): Because a single-phase generator's voltage dips substantially between pulses, the effective average kilovoltage reaching the tube can vary more from one exposure to the next than it does on a high-frequency unit with minimal ripple. That variability in effective kVp directly affects the contrast and penetration recorded on each image, even when the console settings are identical. Focal spot size and anode rotation speed are properties of the tube itself, not the generator's waveform, and the two generator types do not produce identical output. Recognizing this helps a technologist understand why matched technique charts do not guarantee matched results across different generator types.
  123. The correct answer is B (The cathode is releasing a consistent number of electrons for each exposure.): Filament current heats the cathode filament and directly controls how many electrons are released through thermionic emission, which in turn determines the tube current, or mA, produced during exposure. Stable filament current readings across repeated exposures confirm the cathode is behaving consistently and that mA output should also remain consistent. Anode rotation, rectification, and collimator alignment are checked by entirely separate quality control tests. Consistent filament current is foundational to reproducible image density from one exposure to the next.
  124. The correct answer is A (Selection of kVp and mAs, along with the exposure switch and AEC settings): The operating console is where the technologist selects exposure factors such as kVp and mAs and where the exposure switch and any automatic exposure control settings are housed. Rectification is handled inside the generator circuit rather than at the console, absorption of scatter is the job of a grid, and grayscale mapping happens during image processing after acquisition. The console is the technologist's direct interface for controlling how an exposure will be made. This distinction matters because different equipment failures point to different components depending on which function is affected.
  125. The correct answer is B (The battery or capacitor charge level may limit the maximum technique the unit can safely deliver): Mobile units carry their own battery or capacitor system so they can generate exposures away from a fixed power supply, and understanding the charge cycle and power limitations of that system is part of using the unit safely. A partially charged unit may not be able to deliver the same high-mAs technique reliably as a fully charged one. Mobile units are not wired into a fixed three-phase room circuit, and the AEC backup timer functions independent of whether the unit is plugged into house current. Checking charge status before a demanding exposure helps avoid an underexposed image or an interrupted exposure.
  126. The correct answer is D (The heated tungsten filament inside the cathode, which releases electrons by thermionic emission): The cathode contains a tungsten filament that is heated to release electrons through thermionic emission, and increasing filament current makes more electrons available to be accelerated toward the anode, which raises mA. The anode is the target the electrons strike rather than the source of those electrons, and the tube housing and induction motor serve entirely different functions. Recognizing that mA is controlled at the cathode filament, not the anode, is fundamental to understanding tube operation. This distinction also explains why filament current is checked separately from anode-related quality control tests.
  127. The correct answer is C (High atomic number for efficient x-ray production and a high melting point for heat tolerance): Tungsten has a high atomic number, which makes it efficient for producing x-rays when struck by accelerated electrons, and it also has a high melting point, which lets the target survive the intense heat generated during exposure. Rhenium is alloyed in to improve the target's resistance to surface damage from repeated heating and cooling. Low atomic number, low melting point, and magnetic permeability are not the properties that make a material suitable for an anode target. This material choice directly supports both efficient x-ray production and tube longevity under repeated clinical use.
  128. The correct answer is A (The induction motor spins the anode so heat is spread across a larger target area): In rotating anode tubes, an induction motor spins the anode disc at high speed so the electron beam does not remain focused on one small spot, spreading heat over a much larger effective target area and letting the tube tolerate higher techniques without melting the target. Filtration material, target atomic number, and required mAs for a given technique are unrelated to whether the anode rotates. A stationary anode lacks this heat-spreading mechanism, which is why stationary anode tubes are limited to lower-output applications. This heat-spreading advantage is the core reason rotating anode tubes dominate general diagnostic radiography.
  129. The correct answer is B (The rotor sits inside the vacuum while the stator remains outside the glass envelope): The rotor is the part of the induction motor connected to the anode disc and sealed inside the tube's vacuum, while the stator windings sit outside the glass envelope and use electromagnetic induction to spin the rotor without any direct electrical connection crossing the vacuum seal. Reversing this arrangement, or placing both components on the same side, would not allow the motor to function as designed. This separation is what lets the anode spin at high speed inside a sealed vacuum environment. Bearing wear in the rotor assembly is a known source of tube noise and eventual failure.
  130. The correct answer is D (These units use low-output techniques, so a stationary anode's lower heat tolerance is an acceptable tradeoff): Dental and some low-output mobile units are designed around consistently small techniques that never approach the heat limits a rotating anode exists to solve, so the simpler and less expensive stationary anode design is an acceptable engineering tradeoff for that specific use case. Focal spot size is a separate design property from whether the anode rotates, and stationary anodes have no relationship to rectification or AEC compensation. This tradeoff illustrates that anode design choices depend on the clinical demands the equipment is actually built to meet. A stationary anode would not be an appropriate choice for a general high-output radiographic room.
  131. The correct answer is A (Filament current increases, releasing more electrons available for acceleration toward the anode): Increasing the mA setting causes the console to increase filament current, which heats the tungsten filament further and releases more electrons through thermionic emission, giving more electrons available to be accelerated toward the anode and producing the higher tube current requested. Rectification waveform and anode target composition do not change based on the mA selected, and kVp remains under the technologist's separate control rather than adjusting automatically. This filament-current relationship is the direct mechanism connecting a console mA selection to actual tube output. Recognizing it helps explain why filament current QC testing is relevant to mA accuracy.
  132. The correct answer is C (Low-energy photons, because they would be absorbed by the skin without reaching the receptor): Filtration removes the lowest-energy photons from the beam before they reach the patient, and those low-energy photons would otherwise be absorbed by the patient's skin and contribute to dose without ever reaching the image receptor. This improves the average energy of the beam and reduces patient skin dose without meaningfully changing image quality. Filtration does not remove scattered photons, which is instead the job of a grid, and it does not remove all photon energies uniformly. Both inherent and added filtration work together to achieve this same low-energy removal effect.
  133. The correct answer is B (Patient skin dose would increase because more low-energy photons are absorbed by the skin): Filtration exists specifically to remove low-energy photons that would otherwise be absorbed by the patient's skin without ever contributing to the image, so less filtration than specified means more of those low-energy photons reach and are absorbed by the skin, raising patient dose without improving diagnostic value. Image contrast is not meaningfully improved by low-energy photons reaching the receptor, since they do not carry useful penetrating information. The AEC backup timer and anode heat loading are unrelated to how much added filtration is present in the beam path. This is why filtration is checked as part of routine equipment quality control.
  134. The correct answer is D (The exposure ends once the preset time or mAs value the technologist selected is reached): Under manual exposure control, the exposure ends when the preset time or mAs is reached, regardless of what dose the patient actually received, because the technologist's judgment and technique chart determine the exposure factors rather than a detector monitoring radiation in real time. Ionization chamber termination and backup timers are features of AEC, not manual control, and histogram analysis is part of digital image processing that happens after the exposure ends. This distinction is central to understanding why manual technique selection places more responsibility on the technologist to choose accurate exposure factors. AEC, by contrast, terminates based on radiation actually reaching the detector.
  135. The correct answer is A (The small focal spot improves spatial resolution but tolerates less heat than the large focal spot): A smaller focal spot concentrates the electron beam onto a smaller area of the target, which improves spatial resolution and image sharpness, but that same concentration means the small focal spot has a lower heat tolerance than the large focal spot filament. This is why technique charts often restrict small focal spot use to lower-mA techniques. Filament selection does not change filtration, generator phase compatibility, or available kVp range. Understanding this heat-versus-resolution tradeoff helps a technologist choose the correct focal spot for a given clinical situation.
  136. The correct answer is C (Position the thicker end of the body part toward the cathode side of the tube): The anode heel effect results in greater beam intensity toward the cathode side of the field and reduced intensity toward the anode side, because the heel of the angled anode absorbs more photons traveling toward that side. Placing the thicker, more attenuating end of the body part toward the cathode side, where beam intensity is higher, helps balance out density across the finished image. Reversing this placement or centering the part ignores the intensity difference the heel effect actually produces. Applying this principle correctly is especially useful for anatomy like the thoracic spine or femur that varies significantly in thickness along its length.
  137. The correct answer is D (The actual focal spot on the anode is larger than the effective focal spot below.): The line focus principle describes how angling the anode target lets the actual area struck by electrons be larger than the effective focal spot projected downward toward the patient and image receptor, because the angle foreshortens that struck area as seen from below. This relationship allows a tube to spread heat over a larger actual target area while still producing a small, sharp effective focal spot. The actual and effective focal spot sizes are not identical, and effective focal spot size depends on the target angle, not on filament current. This principle is central to how modern x-ray tubes balance heat capacity against spatial resolution.
  138. The correct answer is D (The tube would produce a more pronounced anode heel effect, limiting usable field coverage): A steeper, smaller anode angle does shrink the effective focal spot and improve resolution, but it also makes the anode heel effect more pronounced because the heel of a steeply angled target absorbs a greater proportion of photons heading toward the anode side, narrowing the field over which intensity stays reasonably uniform. This tradeoff limits how large a field can be covered without unacceptable density variation, even though resolution improves. A steep angle does not eliminate the need for a rotating anode or change the generator's phase requirement, and dual-focus tubes can still offer a large focal spot option independent of this design choice. Manufacturers balance anode angle against both heat tolerance and field coverage rather than optimizing for resolution alone.
  139. The correct answer is A (Heat units are approaching the tube's cooling chart limit as exposures continue in rapid succession): The recorded values show accumulated heat units rising steadily with each rapid sequential exposure, which is exactly the pattern a tube rating chart and anode cooling chart are designed to monitor so a technologist does not exceed the tube's safe heat capacity. The data shows heat units increasing, not decreasing, so the anode is not cooling faster than it heats during this rapid sequence. Rising heat units reflect cumulative energy deposited in the anode from each exposure, not a filament current problem or a rectification failure. Recognizing this trend is exactly why rapid sequential exposures require careful heat management to avoid tube damage.
  140. This is exactly the phenomenon the chapter describes when it notes that two machines set to the same kVp and mAs can still produce different image quality. Filtration material, AEC calibration, and mAs delivered are not inherently different between generator types unless separately configured that way. Recognizing waveform ripple as the underlying cause helps explain otherwise confusing image quality differences between rooms.
  141. The correct answer is B (They provide inherent filtration and help dissipate heat generated during exposure): The tube housing, made of glass or metal, along with the surrounding oil, forms the inherent filtration present in every x-ray beam before any added aluminum filters are placed in the path, and the oil also helps conduct heat away from the tube during and after exposure. Converting photons into a digital signal, absorbing scatter, and terminating an exposure are functions of the digital receptor, a grid, and AEC respectively, not the tube housing. Inherent filtration exists whether or not additional filtration is added, which is why total filtration always includes some contribution from the housing itself. Understanding this distinction is useful when troubleshooting unexpectedly low beam quality.
  142. The correct answer is A (Manual exposure accuracy depends entirely on the console's timer matching the actual exposure delivered): Because manual exposure control ends the exposure purely based on the preset time or mAs the technologist selected, an inaccurate timer would silently produce an incorrect exposure without any detector-based safeguard to catch the error, unlike AEC. The close agreement between selected and measured times in this data confirms the timer is functioning within acceptable tolerance for manual technique use. Timer accuracy testing does not verify filament current or rectification waveform quality, which are checked through separate dedicated tests. This test is specifically important for manual control because there is no automatic termination system to compensate for a timer that runs fast or slow.
  143. The correct answer is D (The electron beam will remain focused on one small spot, risking a melted target): The entire purpose of the rotating anode's induction motor is to spin the anode disc so the electron beam does not stay focused on one small spot, spreading heat over a much larger area of the target. Bearing wear or motor failure that prevents the anode from reaching full rotational speed means the beam effectively strikes a much smaller area for a longer relative time, risking localized melting or pitting of the target well below the tube's normal rated capacity. This mechanical issue is unrelated to the digital receptor's conversion process, the AEC backup timer, or the rectification system. Reporting unusual tube noise immediately, rather than continuing to use the equipment, is exactly the kind of malfunction recognition technologists are trained to act on.
  144. The correct answer is C (Actual mA output may no longer match the mA value selected at the console): Because filament current directly controls how many electrons are released through thermionic emission and therefore the mA actually produced, a gradual upward drift in filament current for the same selected technique suggests the relationship between console mA selection and true tube output may be shifting over time. If left uncorrected, this drift could cause images to become progressively overexposed even though the technologist has not changed any settings. Anode rotational speed, AEC chamber sensitivity, and grid lead strip condition are unrelated to filament current and are evaluated through separate quality control tests. Trending this kind of data over months is exactly how gradual equipment drift is caught before it visibly affects patient images.
  145. The correct answer is B (Rotating anodes tolerate far more heat because the beam's impact is spread over a larger area): The dramatic difference in published heat unit capacity between these two tube types reflects the core purpose of anode rotation, spinning the target disc so the electron beam does not remain focused on one small spot, which spreads heat over a much larger effective target area than a fixed, stationary target can offer. Anode target atomic number and generator phase compatibility are not what drives this heat capacity difference, and rotation has no effect on inherent filtration. This comparison illustrates why rotating anode tubes are standard for general diagnostic rooms where higher techniques are routinely needed. A stationary anode's much lower capacity confines it to lower-output clinical applications.
  146. The correct answer is D (The selected combination could exceed the tube's instantaneous heat loading limit and damage the anode): Very high mA paired with a very short exposure time still produces a large amount of instantaneous heat loading on the anode, and the tube rating chart exists specifically to define which combinations of kVp, mA, and time are safe for a given tube, so skipping that check risks selecting a combination that damages or pits the target. The AEC backup timer, the image receptor, and the generator's rectification system do not enforce tube rating limits and cannot substitute for checking the chart. This is exactly the scenario tube rating charts and the console software that enforces them are designed to prevent. Verifying technique against the rating chart remains the technologist's responsibility even under time pressure during trauma imaging.
  147. The correct answer is B (They measure accumulated radiation reaching the receptor and signal the generator to stop): Ionization chambers sit between the patient and the receptor and continuously sense radiation passing through the patient. Once a preset radiation quantity accumulates, the chamber circuit signals the generator to terminate the exposure automatically. This real time measurement is what allows AEC to compensate for differing patient thickness. The chambers do not store records or generate images themselves.
  148. The correct answer is D (When the chambers never accumulate enough radiation to trigger normal termination, as from a failed detector): The backup timer exists specifically for situations where the AEC circuit cannot terminate itself, including a failed detector or a technique where the selected chambers will never receive sufficient radiation. It cuts the exposure off at a preset maximum time or mAs value. This protects the patient from an excessively long exposure and protects the x-ray tube from thermal overload. It is a safety net, not the routine method of ending an exposure.
  149. The correct answer is A (The chambers terminate exposure once a preset radiation quantity reaches the receptor, regardless of thickness): AEC compensates automatically for differences in patient thickness and tissue density because termination depends on the radiation quantity actually reaching the receptor, not on a fixed time or mAs value. A thicker patient simply requires a longer exposure for the chambers to accumulate the same preset value, while a thinner patient reaches that value faster. This is the core mechanism that allows one chamber selection to serve a wide range of patient sizes. Neither kVp nor the backup timer performs this compensation.
  150. The correct answer is C (The shortest exposure time the AEC circuit is physically capable of producing before termination): Minimum response time is the fastest an AEC circuit can terminate an exposure once triggered. If a chosen technique would normally require an exposure shorter than that minimum, the chambers cannot shut the beam off quickly enough, and the exposure runs longer than intended, overexposing the image. This becomes most relevant with thin body parts, pediatric patients, and high mA techniques on high output generators. It is unrelated to the backup timer's maximum limit or to resetting between exams.
  151. The correct answer is A (It biases the AEC system to produce a lighter or darker image than standard): The exposure adjustment control lets the technologist deliberately shift the AEC's target density up or down from its standard calibration point. This is useful for situations the standard calibration was not designed around, such as a plaster cast, contrast media, or unusual pathology. The chambers still terminate the exposure automatically, just at the new biased density target instead of the default. It does not alter chamber wiring or the mA station selected.
  152. The correct answer is D (The two outer chambers, since they lie beneath the lung fields needing exposure): For a chest projection, the center chamber sits under the dense mediastinum and spine, which would prolong the exposure trying to penetrate that density and overexpose the lung fields the radiologist actually needs to evaluate. Selecting the two outer chambers instead places termination control beneath the air filled lungs, producing an image correctly exposed for the anatomy of true diagnostic interest. Using every chamber or an arbitrary prior selection does not account for the mediastinum's density.
  153. The correct answer is B (The center chamber, since the vertebral bodies of interest lie along the midline): For an AP lumbar spine projection, the anatomy of diagnostic interest, the vertebral bodies, lies along the patient's midline. Selecting the center chamber places AEC termination control directly beneath that anatomy rather than beneath soft tissue at the lateral margins. The outer chambers would instead respond to less dense paraspinal tissue, producing an inaccurate termination point. Correct chamber placement means the activated chamber sits beneath the specific structure the exposure must correctly represent.
  154. The correct answer is C (The chamber reaches its preset value quickly and ends the exposure early, underexposing the anatomy): When a selected chamber lies outside attenuating tissue, it receives radiation at a much higher intensity than a properly placed chamber would. This causes the chamber to accumulate its preset radiation value very quickly and signal termination well before the actual anatomy has received adequate exposure. The result is an underexposed image of the anatomy of interest despite an apparently normal sounding exposure time. Correct chamber placement beneath the anatomy of interest is essential to avoiding this error.
  155. The correct answer is D (The exposure adjustment or density control, to bias the AEC toward this situation): The exposure adjustment control exists precisely for situations the standard AEC calibration was not built around, and plaster casts are one of the specific examples where this control is useful. It lets the technologist bias the resulting image lighter or darker relative to the default calibration point. The backup timer and collimator switch do not influence receptor density. The kVp selector still affects the exam but does not substitute for the dedicated density adjustment control.
  156. The correct answer is A (Select a lower mA station so the required time no longer falls below the minimum): The table's calculated exposure time of one millisecond falls below the generator's two millisecond minimum response time, meaning the AEC circuit cannot terminate that quickly. The exposure would actually run for at least two milliseconds, delivering more mAs than intended and overexposing this pediatric chest image. Selecting a lower mA station lengthens the time needed to reach the same mAs value, moving it back above the minimum response time. This issue is most common with thin body parts, pediatric patients, and high mA techniques.
  157. The correct answer is C (Investigate the chamber selection and positioning for an error before repeating the exposure): A backup timer termination signals that the selected chambers never accumulated enough radiation to terminate the exposure normally, which often points to incorrect chamber selection or the anatomy being positioned outside the activated chamber. Repeating the exposure without correcting the underlying cause risks another overexposed or diagnostically inadequate image. The backup timer is a safety feature for failures, not the intended routine termination method, and disabling it would remove an important patient and tube protection.
  158. The correct answer is B (The added attenuation from contrast may call for biasing AEC away from standard): Contrast media meaningfully changes how much the beam is attenuated compared to the tissue densities the standard AEC calibration was designed around, similar to how a plaster cast or unusual pathology can. The exposure adjustment control lets the technologist bias the resulting density lighter or darker to account for this difference. Contrast media does not share soft tissue's attenuation properties, so assuming no adjustment is needed can produce an image that is not diagnostically optimal.
  159. The correct answer is A (AEC terminates exposure based on radiation actually reaching the receptor, so time self adjusts): Because the ionization chambers measure the radiation quantity actually reaching the receptor rather than a fixed preset time, exposure duration automatically lengthens for a thicker patient and shortens for a thinner one until the same preset receptor value is reached. This is the mechanism behind AEC's ability to compensate for patient thickness without the technologist recalculating mAs. The kVp selected by the technologist stays fixed, and dose and scatter actually differ substantially between the two patients even though receptor exposure ends up similar.
  160. The correct answer is D (A limit set too close to the anticipated technique risks terminating correctly exposing images early): The backup timer exists to catch genuine failures, such as a chamber that never triggers normal termination, not to interrupt an exposure that is proceeding correctly. If the backup limit sits too close to the anticipated mAs, normal variation in patient thickness could cause the backup timer to cut off an image that was actually still exposing appropriately. Setting the limit comfortably above the anticipated technique preserves its role as a safety net rather than a routine termination method, without raising dose on exposures that terminate normally through the chambers.
  161. The correct answer is C (The exposure may run longer, since AEC cannot terminate below the minimum time): A very high mA station paired with a thin pediatric chest can require an extremely short exposure time to reach the correct mAs. If that required time falls below the generator's minimum response time, the AEC circuit cannot terminate the exposure that quickly, and the beam continues until the minimum response time elapses. This delivers more mAs than intended and overexposes the image. This scenario is exactly why thin body parts, pediatric patients, and high mA techniques deserve special attention to minimum response time.
  162. The correct answer is B (The outer chambers may no longer sit beneath the vertebral bodies once positioning turns lateral): Chamber selection must be reconsidered for each projection because the position of the anatomy of interest relative to the chambers changes with patient positioning. Outer chambers appropriate for a chest projection do not necessarily correspond to where the vertebral bodies fall in a lateral lumbar spine projection. Reusing a chamber selection without checking placement risks the same underexposure or overexposure problems caused by any other chamber positioning error. The backup timer is only a failure safeguard and does not correct for a poor chamber choice.
  163. The correct answer is A (Barium fluorohalide crystals activated with europium to trap and store the exposure signal): CR imaging plates use a photostimulable phosphor layer, most often barium fluorohalide doped with a europium activator, that traps electrons in a higher energy state when struck by x-ray photons. This trapped energy forms the latent image until the plate is scanned by a laser reader. Amorphous silicon and thin film transistor arrays belong to flat panel DR detectors, not reusable CR plates. Cesium iodide and gadolinium oxysulfide are scintillators used in indirect conversion DR systems rather than in photostimulable phosphor plates.
  164. The correct answer is C (The laser stimulates release of the trapped electron energy as visible light): Laser stimulation causes the trapped electrons in the photostimulable phosphor to fall back to a lower energy state, releasing that stored energy as visible light through a process called photostimulated luminescence. A photomultiplier tube captures this emitted light and converts it into an electrical signal that becomes the digital image. The plate is not heated, and erasure happens afterward using bright light rather than during readout. The phosphor itself never converts directly into an electrical charge, since the light emission step is required first.
  165. The correct answer is B (Exposing the plate to a bright erasure light source before its next use): After a photostimulable phosphor plate is read, some trapped electron energy can remain if the plate is not fully erased with a bright light source, and that residual signal can appear as a faint ghost image on the next exposure. AEC chamber recalibration and photomultiplier tube replacement address different components of the system and would not cause a residual image. Flat fielding corrects for detector sensitivity variation, not for leftover latent signal on a reusable plate.
  166. The correct answer is D (Amorphous selenium, which generates charge pairs directly from x-ray exposure): Amorphous selenium is a photoconductor that absorbs x-ray photons and directly generates electron-hole charge pairs, which are then read out by an underlying thin film transistor array without ever producing an intermediate light signal. Amorphous silicon paired with cesium iodide describes an indirect conversion detector instead. Gadolinium oxysulfide with a charge-coupled device is an older indirect conversion design, and barium fluorohalide is the phosphor material used in computed radiography plates, not flat panel direct conversion detectors.
  167. The correct answer is B (Direct conversion detectors avoid the light spread caused by an intermediate scintillator step): Because direct conversion detectors convert x-ray photons straight into an electrical signal without a light-producing scintillator, there is no lateral spread of light before the signal reaches the detector elements, which preserves finer spatial detail. Indirect conversion systems introduce a scintillator step that can spread light slightly before it is captured. Housing thickness and calibration frequency do not determine inherent spatial resolution, and kVp selection is a technique choice unrelated to detector conversion type.
  168. The correct answer is A (The new detector eliminates the light spread step present in the prior scintillator design): Because the scenario keeps technique and positioning unchanged, the improvement in trabecular detail is best explained by the physical detector conversion mechanism itself. Direct conversion detectors like amorphous selenium bypass the scintillator light-conversion step that indirect conversion detectors use, which removes a source of lateral light spread and improves inherent spatial resolution. The scenario states technique charts stayed the same, ruling out an exposure increase, and nothing in the scenario mentions a processing or panel size change.
  169. The correct answer is C (The direct conversion amorphous selenium panel, based on its recorded DQE value): The recorded values show the amorphous selenium panel with the highest detective quantum efficiency of the three systems, meaning it converts a larger share of the incoming x-ray signal into usable image information at that dose. A higher DQE generally allows a facility to maintain diagnostic image quality while using less radiation dose. The CR system recorded the lowest value, and the three systems clearly do not perform identically, since their DQE values differ meaningfully in the chart.
  170. The correct answer is D (A ratio comparing output signal-to-noise squared against input signal-to-noise squared): DQE expresses how efficiently a detector converts the x-ray signal it receives into a usable image signal, calculated as the ratio of the squared output signal-to-noise ratio to the squared input signal-to-noise ratio. A higher DQE means less dose is needed to achieve an equivalent level of image quality. Pixel count describes spatial resolution capability rather than dose efficiency, and detector damage thresholds are a separate durability specification. Photon capture percentage alone does not account for the noise that DQE specifically measures.
  171. The correct answer is A (It channels light more directly toward the photodiode, reducing lateral spread and blur): The needle-like crystal structure of cesium iodide acts somewhat like a fiber optic bundle, guiding light produced during scintillation more directly down toward the underlying photodiode array rather than allowing it to scatter sideways. This reduces the light spread that granular phosphors like gadolinium oxysulfide allow, improving spatial resolution. Filtration and detector conversion type are unrelated to crystal structure. Describing charge pairs generated without any light step actually describes a direct conversion detector, not this indirect conversion design.
  172. The correct answer is B (DQE decreases as spatial frequency increases, dropping off at finer detail levels): The recorded values fall from 0.62 at the lowest spatial frequency down to 0.19 at the highest spatial frequency tested, showing that this detector converts incoming signal into useful image information less efficiently as finer detail is being resolved. This decline at higher spatial frequencies is a normal characteristic of digital detector performance, not a sign of malfunction by itself. The values clearly are not constant or steadily increasing, and DQE data recorded this way is a standard, meaningful way to evaluate detector performance.
  173. The correct answer is D (The detector's wide dynamic range, which captures a broad span of exposure values): Digital detectors can accurately capture and digitize a much wider range of exposure values than film-screen systems could, which lets postprocessing produce a diagnostic-looking image even from exposures that were somewhat too high or too low. Pixel matrix size relates to spatial resolution, not exposure tolerance, and grid conversion factor and collimator alignment do not explain a detector's ability to accommodate a wide exposure range. This wide dynamic range is also why dose creep can go unnoticed without exposure index monitoring.
  174. The correct answer is C (Digital receptors can produce a usable image across a much broader exposure range): A wide dynamic range means the detector can accurately capture and digitize signal across a large span of exposure values, from relatively low to relatively high, and still generate a diagnostic-looking image after processing. Film-screen systems have a much narrower exposure latitude, so an exposure outside that range produces a visibly too light or too dark image directly on the film. Digital receptors are not limited to one narrow setting, are not physically capped at a fixed dose ceiling in this sense, and do not require exact technique matching the way film-screen did.
  175. The correct answer is B (The receptor is likely receiving excess exposure beginning on the fourth recorded day): Exposure index values stayed consistent around 405 to 412 for the first three days, then jumped to 680 and 695 on days four and five despite the technologist using the same technique and similar patient sizes, which points to a technique or equipment change causing overexposure rather than normal variation. Exposure index values directly track relative radiation reaching the detector, so they are not unrelated to patient dose. The sudden shift is a real finding worth investigating, but the data alone does not justify immediate replacement before further QC testing is performed.
  176. The correct answer is A (It is an indirect conversion detector, since light is produced before the electrical signal): A CCD paired with a scintillator layer first converts x-ray photons into visible light at the scintillator, then the CCD converts that light into an electrical signal, which is the defining two-step process of indirect conversion. This is the same basic conversion sequence used by newer amorphous silicon and cesium iodide panels, just with an older sensor technology. It cannot be a direct conversion detector, since a scintillator light-producing step is explicitly present. It also is not a photostimulable phosphor plate system, since CCD-based fluoroscopy detectors do not require separate plate handling for each exposure.
  177. The correct answer is C (Laser spot size and plate structure in CR limit resolution more than newer DR designs): Computed radiography resolution is limited by practical factors such as the laser beam spot size used during readout and the physical granularity of the phosphor plate structure, both of which place an inherent ceiling on fine detail compared to many modern DR flat panel designs. This is a general physical characteristic of the technology, not a calibration or technique issue, matching the scenario's description that both systems were properly calibrated and used identical technique. DR panels do not require a higher kVp by rule, CR erasure method does not affect resolution, and a DR panel does not store or develop a latent image the way CR plates do.
  178. The correct answer is D (It converts incoming x-ray photons into visible light at the tube's entrance): The input phosphor is the first component in the image intensifier chain, converting x-ray photons into light photons. That light then strikes a photocathode, which releases electrons proportional to the light received. Those electrons are accelerated and focused before striking the output phosphor to produce a much brighter image.
  179. The correct answer is D (It reduces equipment bulk and eliminates image intensifier distortion artifacts): Flat panel detectors used in fluoroscopy convert x-rays directly into a digital signal, similar to digital radiography detectors, bypassing the electron acceleration and focusing chain used in an image intensifier. Skipping that chain removes the bulky vacuum tube housing and the distortions inherent to image intensifier geometry. Automatic brightness control, dose limits, and shielding requirements are unrelated to the detector conversion method.
  180. The correct answer is A (ABC continuously adjusts technique throughout the exposure instead of terminating a single exposure): AEC in radiography terminates a single exposure once the ionization chambers accumulate a preset radiation amount. ABC, or AERC, operates continuously during fluoroscopy, adjusting kVp, mA, or both in real time to maintain consistent image brightness as the anatomy or positioning changes. It functions during both live viewing and recording, and it does not measure patient dose directly, it responds to radiation reaching the detector.
  181. The correct answer is A (Patient dose increases because the system raises technique to keep brightness over a smaller receptor area): Magnification mode narrows the electron beam's focus to display a smaller field of view at higher apparent resolution. Because the imaged area shrinks while ABC still needs to maintain consistent monitor brightness, the system compensates by increasing technique. This raises patient dose even though the anatomical region being irradiated is roughly unchanged from standard mode.
  182. The correct answer is C (That the table locks securely and the patient is safely secured against sliding): Fluoroscopic tables tilt and move during many procedures, and this motion presents a real fall or slide risk for patients who are sedated, elderly, or unstable. The technologist must confirm the table locks securely at each position and that the patient is adequately supported before and during movement. Spot image counters, collimator checks, and grid frequency are unrelated to table movement safety.
  183. The correct answer is A (The recording system captures still images or video for the permanent medical record): The viewing system shows the fluoroscopic image on a monitor in real time so the radiologist can guide the procedure. The recording system, through digital spot imaging or recorded video sequences, captures still images or clips that become part of the permanent medical record. Table tilt and collimation are separate mechanical functions, not part of either system.
  184. The correct answer is C (A higher grid frequency packs more lead strips per inch, keeping the lines less visually distracting): Because a stationary grid does not move during exposure, its lead strips are visible as fine lines on the finished image. Using a higher grid frequency means more lead strips per inch, which makes those lines finer and less noticeable to the eye. Grid frequency does not change kVp requirements, remove the need for central ray alignment, or correct for motion.
  185. The correct answer is B (A software algorithm that mathematically reduces the effect of scatter on the displayed image): A virtual grid is a form of software-based scatter correction used with digital detectors. Instead of physically absorbing scatter with lead strips, the algorithm analyzes the raw image data and reduces the visual effect of scatter on the resulting image. This is a computational process, not a physical device or a backup chamber.
  186. The correct answer is B (Some exposures can be made without a physical grid, avoiding the usual technique increase): A physical grid absorbs a portion of the primary beam along with scatter, so technique must be increased to compensate. Because a virtual grid corrects for scatter computationally rather than by physically absorbing it, certain exposures can be made without a physical grid at all, potentially avoiding that technique increase and reducing patient dose. It does not automatically alter kVp, filtration, or SID.
  187. The correct answer is A (It moves the grid to blur the lead strip lines a stationary grid would show): The Bucky assembly houses a moving grid called the Potter-Bucky diaphragm, which oscillates or reciprocates during the exposure. This motion blurs the lead strip lines so they do not appear as visible lines on the finished radiograph, unlike a stationary grid. AEC chamber position, SID, and anode rotation are separate functions unrelated to grid motion.
  188. The correct answer is B (The AEC ionization chambers positioned beneath the tabletop within the Bucky tray): The Bucky tray typically houses the AEC detector chambers beneath the tabletop, alongside the moving grid. If these chambers are not properly aligned with the anatomy or the central ray, exposure termination can be inconsistent from one side of the image to the other. The anode motor, collimator lamp, and generator circuit do not directly control AEC termination.
  189. The correct answer is A (Grid cutoff caused by misalignment between the grid, the AEC chambers, and the central ray): The grid, the AEC chambers, and the central ray of the beam must all be properly aligned relative to each other. When this alignment drifts, grid cutoff can occur, producing a loss of density on one side of the image. A failing filament, a change in inherent filtration, or generator ripple would typically affect the whole image rather than producing a one-sided density loss.
  190. The correct answer is D (The Normal Mode Retest reading, which measured 11.4 R per minute): Federal regulations limit standard normal mode fluoroscopy to approximately 10 R per minute at the point where the beam enters the patient. The retest reading of 11.4 R/min exceeds that normal mode limit and would require the unit to be taken out of service until corrected. The other normal and magnification mode readings fall below 10 R/min, and the high-level control mode reading falls within its separately allowed higher limit.
  191. The correct answer is B (They are accelerated and focused across the vacuum tube toward the output phosphor): Once the photocathode releases electrons in proportion to the light striking it, those electrons are accelerated and focused as they travel through the vacuum tube. This acceleration and focusing step is what produces a much brighter image at the output phosphor. Electrons are not converted back into x-rays, and they are not absorbed at the input phosphor.
  192. The correct answer is D (The smaller field of view in magnification mode requires more technique to maintain the same brightness): Magnification mode narrows the field of view displayed by the image intensifier without changing the actual anatomy being irradiated. Because ABC must maintain consistent brightness over that smaller effective receptor area, it increases kVp, mA, or both. This response is a direct consequence of the mode change itself, not of patient movement, generator phase, or grid removal.
  193. The correct answer is A (Digital spot imaging, which captures still images for the permanent record during the procedure): Digital spot imaging is a function of the recording system that allows still images or brief clips to be captured for the permanent medical record while the live fluoroscopic examination continues. ABC adjusts brightness rather than capturing images, the output phosphor is part of the viewing chain, and the Potter-Bucky diaphragm is a radiographic grid component, not part of fluoroscopic recording.
  194. The correct answer is C (The grid, the AEC chambers, and the central ray must all be properly centered together): The Bucky assembly must be mechanically aligned so that the grid, the AEC detector chambers, and the central ray of the x-ray beam are all properly centered with one another. This alignment applies to fixed table and Bucky installations, since misalignment in any of these three relationships can cause grid cutoff or inaccurate exposure termination.
  195. The correct answer is C (Density decreases progressively from the left side of the image to the right side): Grid cutoff from misalignment between the grid, the AEC chambers, and the central ray typically produces a progressive loss of density toward one side of the image, since more of the primary beam is absorbed on that side. The data shown, with density falling steadily from the left edge toward the right edge, matches this pattern. Uniform density, symmetric edge loss, or a random pattern would point to a different cause.
  196. The correct answer is D (Distortion artifacts such as pincushion and vignetting inherent to image intensifier geometry): Image intensifiers rely on an electron-optic chain that focuses electrons from a curved input surface toward the output phosphor, which inherently produces geometric distortions such as pincushion distortion and vignetting. Flat panel detectors convert x-rays directly into a digital signal without that electron-optic chain, eliminating these particular distortion artifacts. Quantum mottle, motion artifact, and grid line artifact arise from different causes unrelated to the electron-optic chain.
  197. The correct answer is A (An improperly functioning compression device could cause patient discomfort or injury during use): Compression devices are used during fluoroscopic procedures to displace overlying structures or manage patient anatomy, and they must move smoothly and lock securely. A device that malfunctions could cause patient discomfort or injury, which is a real clinical concern, particularly for patients who are sedated, elderly, or unstable. It has no electrical connection to kVp selection, AEC chambers, or grid frequency.
  198. The correct answer is B (A grid requires higher mA since it absorbs part of the primary beam too): The data shown reflects that the grid technique uses substantially higher mA than the non-grid technique for the identical projection, kVp, and SID. This is because a physical grid absorbs a portion of the primary beam along with scattered radiation, requiring increased technique to compensate. The exposure time, kVp, and SID remain unchanged between the two setups in this comparison.
  199. The correct answer is C (The technique selected did not adequately compensate for the grid's overall scatter absorption): A uniform loss of density across the entire image, rather than a one-sided loss, points toward inadequate overall technique rather than a positioning or alignment problem. Since the stationary grid absorbs a portion of the beam, the technique must account for that absorption. Alignment problems and AEC centering issues typically produce asymmetric density differences, and the Bucky diaphragm is not used with stationary grids.
  200. The correct answer is C (It packs more lead strips per inch, making the visible grid lines less noticeable): Because a stationary grid does not oscillate to blur its lead strips, a higher grid frequency, meaning more lead strips per inch, is commonly used to keep those lines from being visually distracting on the finished image. Grid frequency does not change the required kVp, does not remove the need for central ray alignment, and does not eliminate the technique increase a grid requires.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (It converts the accelerated electrons back into a much brighter light image): After electrons are released at the photocathode and accelerated and focused across the vacuum tube, the output phosphor converts those electrons back into a light image. This output image is much brighter than the original light produced at the input phosphor, which is the basis of image intensification. It does not filter the beam or convert x-rays directly into a digital signal, since that describes a flat panel detector.
  2. The correct answer is C (Larger patients generate more scatter, requiring more raw signal for effective correction without added noise): Virtual grids reduce scatter's effect computationally rather than by physically absorbing it, and their dose-reduction potential is greatest when scatter volume is more modest. In larger patients, greater scatter volume can require more raw signal for the correction to work effectively without introducing excess image noise, which narrows the practical dose advantage. Virtual grids work with digital detectors, do not require disabling AEC chambers, and do not automatically raise kVp.
  3. The correct answer is C (ABC exposure rate rises as the field of view narrows toward magnification mode): The data shown demonstrates that exposure rate climbs progressively as the field of view narrows from large to magnification. This reflects how ABC increases technique to maintain consistent brightness over a smaller effective receptor area, which is the same mechanism that raises patient dose in magnification mode. The readings do not decrease or stay flat, and field of view clearly affects the recorded rate.
  4. The correct answer is C (Verify the table has locked securely at that angle before continuing the exam): The fluoroscopic table and its tilting mechanism must move smoothly and lock securely, since patient safety during table movement and tilting is a genuine clinical concern, especially for sedated, elderly, or unstable patients. Confirming the lock is engaged before continuing protects the patient from unexpected movement. Switching recording modes or adjusting ABC brightness does not address the mechanical safety of the table position.
  5. The correct answer is A (AEC terminates a single exposure, while ABC continuously adjusts technique throughout an ongoing fluoroscopic exposure): Both systems rely on radiation reaching the detector to guide exposure, but AEC in radiography terminates a single, discrete exposure once a preset radiation amount accumulates. ABC in fluoroscopy instead operates continuously throughout the ongoing exposure, adjusting kVp, mA, or both as the anatomy or positioning changes. The exam roles are reversed from the student's claim, and neither system's grid requirement nor measurement location matches the other options.
  6. The correct answer is D (Table Position 3, with a 1.8 cm offset): Grid cutoff results from misalignment between the central ray and the grid, and the severity of the resulting density loss generally corresponds to the degree of offset. The 1.8 cm offset at Table Position 3 is substantially larger than the other three measurements, which fall within a range unlikely to produce a visible effect on the image. This position would be the most likely to show noticeable grid cutoff and should be flagged for correction.
  7. The correct answer is C (It narrows the field of view and displays the anatomy at higher apparent resolution): Magnification mode narrows the electron beam's focus within the image intensifier to display a smaller field of view at higher apparent resolution, which helps evaluate fine detail such as small vessels during angiography. It does not broaden the field of view, lower the exposure rate needed for brightness, or apply a virtual grid, and it actually increases patient dose rather than reducing exposure demands.
  8. The correct answer is D (ABC raises both kVp and mA to maintain brightness over the smaller magnified field): The data shows both kVp and mA increasing from Normal mode to Magnification mode for the same procedure. This confirms that ABC can adjust either or both factors as needed to maintain consistent image brightness when the field of view narrows in magnification mode. The technique clearly changes with mode selection, and ABC is not limited to adjusting mA alone.
  9. The correct answer is C (Quantization, performed by the analog-to-digital converter as it samples the detector's raw signal): The analog-to-digital converter, or ADC, samples the detector's analog output and converts it into discrete numeric values the computer can store and manipulate. The number of possible gray levels this process can produce depends on the system's bit depth. Flat fielding, rescaling, and equalization are later preprocessing and processing steps applied only after quantization has already created the raw digital data.
  10. The correct answer is A (It increases the number of possible gray levels available to represent the image): Quantization converts the detector's analog signal into discrete numeric values, and the number of possible gray levels that conversion can produce depends directly on the system's bit depth. A higher bit depth does not change spatial resolution, which depends on detector element size, nor does it remove the need for flat fielding or automatically trigger edge enhancement. Those remain separate correction and processing steps applied later in the imaging chain.
  11. The correct answer is D (Flat fielding, which evens out the detector's naturally uneven response across its surface): Flat fielding corrects for the fact that a detector's individual elements are not perfectly uniform in sensitivity, evening out that variation before the image is built. Dead pixel correction addresses only individual failed elements rather than an overall uneven response, and rescaling standardizes pixel value scale rather than surface sensitivity. Smoothing is a later noise-reduction step applied to the already-corrected image data.
  12. The correct answer is B (Dead pixel correction, which substitutes interpolated values from surrounding healthy pixels): Dead pixel correction identifies a detector element that has failed and no longer responds correctly, then substitutes an interpolated value drawn from the surrounding healthy pixels so the failed element does not appear as a distracting spot. Flat fielding addresses overall sensitivity variation rather than a single failed element, and histogram analysis and edge enhancement serve entirely different roles in exposure indexing and boundary contrast. This uniformity finding calls specifically for dead pixel correction.
  13. The correct answer is A (A histogram, generated during preprocessing to guide anatomy recognition and exposure index): During preprocessing the system generates a histogram, a graph of how many pixels fall at each exposure value, and the software analyzes that histogram to identify the anatomy present and calculate an appropriate exposure index. A look-up table is a display-mapping tool used later in processing, not the anatomy-recognition step itself. A dose-area-product report and a modulation transfer function curve describe entirely different quantities unrelated to this histogram analysis.
  14. The correct answer is C (Edge enhancement, which sharpens the appearance of boundaries such as catheter tips): Edge enhancement increases contrast at boundaries between tissue densities, which is exactly what makes structures like bone edges or a catheter tip appear sharper on the displayed image. Smoothing works in the opposite direction, reducing noise sometimes at the cost of fine detail rather than sharpening edges. Equalization and flat fielding address dynamic range compression and detector sensitivity, neither of which targets catheter-tip sharpness.
  15. The correct answer is B (Equalization, which compresses both histogram extremes so dense and lucent areas remain readable): Equalization compresses the very bright and very dark ends of the histogram so that both dense and lucent areas of a single image, such as the mediastinum and the lung fields on one chest image, can be evaluated together on the same display. Edge enhancement sharpens density boundaries rather than compressing the overall dynamic range, and rescaling standardizes scale rather than reshaping the histogram extremes. Dead pixel correction addresses failed detector elements and has no role in balancing region brightness.
  16. The correct answer is D (Smoothing, applied automatically at lower doses where noise is otherwise more prominent): Smoothing reduces the visual appearance of noise, sometimes at the cost of fine detail, and is often applied automatically for lower-dose exposures where noise would otherwise be more prominent, matching this pediatric scenario exactly. Edge enhancement sharpens boundaries rather than suppressing noise, and equalization reshapes the histogram's extremes rather than smoothing pixel-to-pixel variation. Grayscale processing maps values into a useful range but does not specifically target noise reduction.
  17. The correct answer is A (Values of interest processing and a look-up table, tailoring the captured range for display): Values of interest (VOI) processing together with a look-up table map the wide range of digital pixel values captured by the detector down to the specific brightness levels the display monitor and the human eye can actually distinguish. Flat fielding and dead pixel correction are earlier preprocessing steps addressing detector defects rather than display mapping, and histogram generation and rescaling occur before this display-tailoring step. Image stitching and electronic cropping are postprocessing tools unrelated to translating dynamic range for display.
  18. The correct answer is C (Window level and window width, adjusting brightness and contrast respectively): Adjusting brightness is sometimes called window level, while adjusting contrast is sometimes called window width, and both are postprocessing tools available to the technologist and radiologist after the image is generated. Naming the two terms in the reversed order does not match the sequence described in the scenario. Rescaling and equalization, and grayscale processing and edge enhancement, are unrelated preprocessing and processing steps rather than the postprocessing brightness and contrast controls being described here.
  19. The correct answer is D (A region of interest tool, which measures pixel values in a specific area): A region of interest, or ROI, tool allows measurement of pixel values in a specific area, which is useful for evaluating density in a specific structure such as a suspicious lucent bone region. Electronic cropping only removes unwanted areas from the displayed image and does not measure pixel values, and image stitching combines separate exposures rather than measuring density. A look-up table maps values for overall display and is not a targeted measurement tool.
  20. The correct answer is B (Electronic cropping or masking, which removes unwanted areas without altering acquired data): Electronic cropping or masking removes unwanted areas from the displayed or printed image, most often the collimated border, without altering the underlying acquired data. Image stitching combines separate exposures rather than trimming a border, and a region of interest tool measures values rather than removing an unwanted area. Equalization reshapes histogram extremes and has no role in removing a collimated border from view.
  21. The correct answer is C (Image stitching, most commonly used for long-bone or scoliosis studies of this kind): Image stitching combines multiple separate exposures into a single continuous image, and it is most commonly used for long-bone or scoliosis studies where the anatomy of interest is longer than a single detector can capture in one exposure. Electronic cropping only trims a single displayed image and cannot join separate exposures together. Equalization and flat fielding are single-image processing and preprocessing steps that play no role in combining separate detector captures into one long image.
  22. The correct answer is A (Ambient light washing out gray-scale differences, and viewing angle degrading perceived contrast): Ambient room lighting that is too bright will wash out subtle gray-scale differences, which is why reading rooms are kept dim, and viewing angle affects perceived brightness and contrast on many monitor types, which is why radiologists view monitors from directly in front. Spatial resolution and gray-level count are fixed hardware properties of the monitor rather than viewing-condition factors controlled by room setup. Detector drift and network downtime are unrelated equipment and informatics concerns, not viewing-condition factors.
  23. The correct answer is B (The monitor's spatial resolution, limited by its own pixel size and pitch): Spatial resolution of a monitor depends on pixel size and pixel pitch, meaning how small and how closely packed the individual pixels are, and a monitor with coarser pixels cannot display the fine detail that a higher-resolution detector actually captured no matter how good the acquisition was. Brightness and contrast resolution describe gray-level count and maximum brightness rather than fine spatial detail. PACS storage capacity and DICOM conformance are informatics and networking concerns unrelated to whether a monitor can physically display captured detail.
  24. The correct answer is D (Every technologist accesses protected health information daily and must follow facility login policy directly): Security and confidentiality of these systems is a real technologist responsibility, not just an IT department concern, since every technologist accesses protected health information through these systems daily and must follow facility policy on login credentials, workstation locking, and appropriate access. Sharing a login bypasses that individual accountability regardless of good intentions or how briefly the access lasts. PACS storage capacity, DICOM conformance testing, and order processing are unrelated technical concerns and are not affected by which technologist happens to be logged in.
  25. The correct answer is C (The Electronic Medical Record or Electronic Health Record, holding broader clinical history): The Electronic Medical Record or Electronic Health Record holds the patient's broader clinical history, which includes information like prior surgeries and medications that extends beyond scheduling and order data. The Radiology Information System manages scheduling, demographics, orders, and reporting rather than the patient's full clinical history. The Picture Archiving and Communication System stores images, and DICOM is a data exchange format, neither of which functions as a clinical history repository.
  26. The correct answer is A (DICOM, the universal standard format that allows different manufacturers' equipment to communicate): DICOM, Digital Imaging and Communications in Medicine, is the universal standard format that allows equipment from different manufacturers to communicate images and data with each other reliably, which is exactly the interoperability concern in this scenario. The Radiology Information System and Electronic Health Record manage scheduling and clinical documentation rather than cross-vendor image exchange formatting. Downtime procedure documentation addresses outage response, not manufacturer interoperability confirmed through conformance testing.
  27. The correct answer is B (That the visible light field matches the actual invisible radiation field within tolerance): Beam restriction quality control exists specifically to confirm that the light field a technologist uses for positioning agrees with the radiation field patients actually receive. Misalignment beyond the accepted tolerance can needlessly irradiate tissue outside the intended anatomy or clip anatomy the physician needs, causing a repeat exposure. Central ray perpendicularity, automatic exposure control sensitivity, and grid frequency are each evaluated by separate, distinct quality control tests.
  28. The correct answer is A (Within one degree of true perpendicularity to the image receptor plane): Central ray alignment is generally held to a tolerance of one degree from true perpendicularity to the image receptor. Exceeding this tolerance introduces measurable distortion into the resulting image and can misalign the beam relative to a focused grid, producing grid cutoff. Tighter or looser tolerances than one degree are not the accepted standard for this specific test. Technologists rely on a plumb bob or a dedicated alignment tool to confirm this angle during routine equipment quality control.
  29. The correct answer is C (Debris, physical damage, or component aging causing inconsistent detector response): Plate uniformity testing exposes the detector to a flat, even beam and checks that every region of the resulting image reads the same value. Any variation reveals damage, debris, or aging components that would otherwise appear as an unexplained artifact on an actual patient image. Focal spot selection, light field alignment, and automatic exposure control calibration are each assessed through separate quality control procedures. Catching this kind of detector inconsistency early prevents it from being mistaken for real pathology.
  30. The correct answer is D (Because internal cracks in the lead composite are often invisible to the eye): A cracked or degraded shielding accessory can look intact from the outside while the lead composite material has already developed gaps that let radiation pass through. Imaging the apron, glove, or thyroid shield directly reveals these hidden defects that a visual inspection alone would miss. Relying only on visual inspection creates a false sense of protection for both staff and patients wearing the compromised accessory. Departments remove any accessory that fails this scheduled test from service immediately.
  31. The correct answer is A (The collimator passes, since every measured edge offset falls within 2% of SID): At 40 inches SID, the 2% tolerance allows up to 0.8 inches of misalignment per edge, and every recorded edge offset in this data set falls below that limit. The combined offset of any two edges also stays under the separate 3% of SID limit used for that comparison. Central ray perpendicularity is evaluated as its own distinct test and does not need to pass before beam restriction results can be interpreted. This collimator meets the accepted tolerance and requires no further adjustment.
  32. The correct answer is C (The 90 kVp station fails, since its measured value exceeds the 5% limit): The 90 kVp station measured 97.4 kVp, an error of roughly 8.2%, which exceeds the accepted 5% accuracy tolerance for kVp reproducibility on a radiographic generator. The 70, 80, and 100 kVp stations each measured within a few percent of their console setting and remain within tolerance. An inaccurate kVp station changes both patient dose and image contrast in ways the technologist cannot predict at the console. Biomedical engineering should recalibrate this station before it is used clinically again.
  33. The correct answer is B (The unit passes, since the coefficient of variation is far below 0.05): The five recorded exposure readings cluster tightly around 42 mR, and the calculated coefficient of variation from a data set this consistent falls well under the accepted 0.05 limit for exposure reproducibility. A low coefficient of variation confirms the generator produces a nearly identical output every time the same technique factors are selected. Five exposures at one technique is an accepted sample size for this specific test, so the result is valid. This generator does not require recalibration based on this reproducibility result.
  34. The correct answer is D (At least 2.3 mm of aluminum equivalent filtration at this kVp): Regulatory standards require a radiographic unit operating at 80 kVp to produce a half-value layer of at least 2.3 mm of aluminum equivalent filtration. The recorded data set shows the exposure output dropping to roughly half of its original value once 2.3 mm of added aluminum filtration is in the beam, matching this minimum. Insufficient filtration leaves excessive low-energy photons in the beam that add patient dose without contributing to the diagnostic image. Total filtration must still be documented, but documentation alone does not replace meeting the minimum HVL requirement.
  35. The correct answer is C (Up to 50% larger than the nominal 0.6 mm stated by the manufacturer): NEMA tolerances allow a measured focal spot smaller than 0.8 mm, such as this 0.6 mm nominal small focal spot, to measure as much as 50% larger than its stated size and still pass quality control. Larger nominal focal spots are held to a slightly tighter 40% tolerance under the same standard. A focal spot that grows beyond its allowed tolerance reduces spatial resolution and can indicate anode damage from thermal stress. Pinhole imaging, along with a slit camera or star pattern, are the accepted methods for measuring true focal spot size.
  36. The correct answer is B (A gradually degrading backlight that quietly reduces contrast resolution over time): Periodic luminance testing measures a diagnostic monitor's maximum brightness and its consistency across the screen to catch an aging backlight before the loss in contrast resolution becomes obvious to the naked eye. This gradual degradation can hide subtle findings on an image long before anyone notices the monitor looks dimmer. Window and level settings are chosen per study and are not a quality control finding. Detector drift and collimator alignment are each evaluated by separate, unrelated quality control procedures.
  37. The correct answer is D (Report the noise to a supervisor or biomedical engineering rather than continuing): Technologists are trained to recognize signs of equipment malfunction such as unusual noises, inconsistent readings, or console error codes and to report those findings immediately rather than continuing to use compromised equipment. An image that still looks diagnostic today does not guarantee the rotor or tube will perform safely on the next patient. Adjusting exposure factors to compensate does not address the underlying mechanical problem and could mask a worsening malfunction. Waiting for the next scheduled quality control date risks the equipment failing or causing harm before that date arrives.
  38. Spatial resolution and luminance drift are display and detector quality control issues that would not produce this specific pattern of grid cutoff.   40. Treating each passing month in isolation misses the trend and delays intervention until the receptor eventually fails a monthly check outright. A worn test pattern or a measurement error is possible but far less likely than genuine detector aging given a full year of consistent decline. The department should trend this data over time and plan for detector service or replacement proactively.
