There is no radial or ulnar fat pad structure named this way, and the olecranon fat pad is not the normally visible structure described in this context.
  85. The correct answer is B (An occult fracture with joint effusion not otherwise visible on the image): an elevated or displaced anterior fat pad, sometimes called a sail sign, suggests joint effusion from an occult fracture that may not be directly visible on the bony image itself. It does not represent a chronic degenerative process, normal pediatric variation, or simple superficial soft tissue swelling unrelated to the joint.
  86. The correct answer is C (The supinator fat stripe, the third normal structure evaluated on the lateral elbow): the supinator fat stripe is the third normal fat structure evaluated on a correctly positioned lateral elbow, alongside the anterior and posterior fat pads. There is no standard pronator, brachialis, or triceps fat stripe referenced in elbow fat pad evaluation.
  87. The correct answer is C (Forcing rotation risks displacing the fracture fragments further): the AP humerus keeps the arm in neutral rotation whenever fracture is suspected because forcing rotation risks displacing the fracture fragments further, not because of dose reduction, wrist joint inclusion, or breathing artifact, none of which describe the actual reason for this positioning precaution.
  88. The correct answer is C (A rotational lateral humerus, requiring arm movement not safe in trauma): a non-trauma patient can safely undergo a rotational lateral humerus, which requires moving the arm into position, since there is no fracture displacement risk to avoid. The transthoracic, tangential, and axillary lateral terms describe different projections not used for this routine non-trauma lateral humerus.
  89. The correct answer is A (A transthoracic lateral humerus, keeping the injured arm at the patient's side): the transthoracic lateral humerus keeps the injured arm hanging at the patient's side while the opposite arm raises overhead, projecting a true lateral without moving the injured limb. The rotational lateral requires arm movement that trauma patients cannot safely perform, and the AP axial and Grashey terms describe unrelated projections.
  90. The correct answer is A (Shallow breathing during a longer, low milliamperage exposure to blur the ribs): the transthoracic lateral uses shallow breathing during a longer, low mA exposure to blur the ribs and lung markings while the denser humerus stays comparatively sharp, making it one of the few extremity views where breathing technique matters. Suspended inspiration, suspended expiration, or rapid deep breathing do not achieve the intended blurring effect of this specific technique.
  91. The correct answer is D (The shoulder and elbow joints, included on the AP humerus when possible): the AP humerus ideally includes the shoulder and elbow joints when body habitus allows, giving context to the full length of the humerus. The wrist and hand, cervical spine and clavicle, and bilateral scapula and ribs are not the adjacent structures this projection is designed to include.
  92. The correct answer is C (The transthoracic lateral humerus, designed for patients who cannot move the injured arm): the transthoracic lateral humerus is specifically designed for patients who cannot tolerate movement of an injured arm, since it keeps the injured limb hanging at the side while imaging through the thorax. A rotational lateral requires arm movement the patient cannot tolerate, an obliqued AP does not provide a true lateral, and the scapular Y images the shoulder rather than the humeral shaft.
  93. The correct answer is B (Most extremity exams need no breathing instruction since the chest stays out of the beam): most extremity examinations need no breathing instruction because the chest stays out of the beam, which is exactly why the transthoracic lateral humerus stands out as an exception when the beam does pass through the thorax. Extremity exams do not universally require suspended respiration, the rule is not split by upper versus lower extremity, and long bone measurement does not routinely require special breathing technique.

  102. The correct answer is C (Rotating the arm can worsen the underlying injury): internal and external rotation are avoided with suspected fracture or dislocation because rotating the arm can worsen the underlying injury, which is exactly why dedicated trauma views exist instead. These rotations do not require contrast, are not avoided purely for time, and are not avoided because of a substantially higher radiation dose.
  103. The correct answer is C (The AP neutral rotation shoulder, the safe first trauma view): the AP neutral rotation shoulder leaves the arm exactly as the patient presents it, without attempting rotation, and serves as the safe first trauma view. Internal and external rotation both require potentially unsafe arm movement, and the Lawrence method requires abduction that a trauma patient may not tolerate.
  104. The correct answer is D (The Grashey method, a posterior oblique shoulder projection): the Grashey method rotates the patient thirty five to forty five degrees from a true AP with the affected side against the receptor, aligning the beam tangent to the true glenohumeral joint space that overlaps on a straight AP. The Lawrence, Neer, and scapular Y projections serve different purposes and do not correct this specific anterolateral glenoid orientation problem.
  105. The correct answer is B (Thirty five to forty five degrees from a true AP): the Grashey method rotates the patient thirty five to forty five degrees from a true AP with the affected side against the receptor, which is the standard rotation for opening the true glenohumeral joint space. Ten to fifteen degrees is insufficient, and sixty to seventy degrees or a full ninety degree rotation overshoot the intended obliquity.
  106. The correct answer is A (The Lawrence method, an inferosuperior axial view requiring arm abduction): the Lawrence method is the inferosuperior axial projection that shows the humeral head-glenoid relationship from below, useful for posterior dislocation, and requires ninety degrees of abduction when tolerated. The Grashey method images the joint from a posterior oblique angle rather than from below, and the Neer and Stecher methods address entirely different anatomy.
  107. The correct answer is A (Acute trauma patients often cannot abduct the arm ninety degrees due to pain): because the Lawrence method requires ninety degrees of arm abduction, it has limited use in acute trauma, since injured patients often cannot tolerate that degree of motion due to pain. The method does not require contrast, is not distinguished by a substantially higher radiation dose, and can be performed portably when abduction is possible.
  108. The correct answer is C (The scapular Y projection, requiring no arm movement at all): the scapular Y positions the patient at roughly a sixty degree anterior oblique with no arm movement required, making it valuable in trauma, and the scapular body, coracoid, and acromion form a Y shape on the image. The Grashey and Lawrence methods both require patient or arm positioning the scapular Y avoids, and internal rotation requires arm movement entirely.
  109. The correct answer is B (An anterior or posterior shoulder dislocation, depending on which arm the head sits toward): on the scapular Y, the humeral head normally centers at the junction of the Y, and displacement toward one arm of the Y indicates an anterior or posterior dislocation depending on which arm the head sits toward, which is why this view is valuable for distinguishing dislocation direction in trauma. This finding is not a normal variant, rotator cuff tendinopathy alone, or simply the result of patient motion.
  110.   114.   115. Gripping weights, contrast-outlined tendons, and gadolinium-outlined labrum are not part of this stressed AC joint technique.
  116.   117.   118.   146.   159.   164.   165. The correct answer is B (The Settegast method, a tangential patellofemoral view unrelated to the fossa): the Settegast method is prone with the knee flexed as sharply as tolerated, central ray angled fifteen to twenty degrees cephalad into the joint, distinguishing it from the supine Merchant method.   166. The correct answer is D (Forced sharp flexion in the Settegast method can displace a suspected patellar fracture): the Settegast method is used less often today since forced sharp flexion can displace a suspected patellar fracture, a safety concern the Merchant method's more moderate forty degree flexion avoids. Contrast requirements, an inability to demonstrate the joint space, and a substantially higher radiation dose are not the reason for the Settegast method's decline in use.
  167. The correct answer is A (The Merchant method, since its moderate forty degree flexion is safer): the Merchant method's more moderate forty degree flexion is preferred when patellar fracture is suspected, since the Settegast method's forced sharp flexion can displace a suspected fracture. The Holmblad and Camp Coventry methods target the intercondylar fossa rather than the patellofemoral joint and still involve meaningful knee flexion themselves.
  168. The correct answer is C (Prone positioning brings the patella closer to the image receptor): the standalone patella view is taken prone to bring the patella closer to the image receptor, which improves detail on this anterior structure. This positioning is not primarily about including the entire femur, eliminating central ray angulation, or universal patient comfort, none of which is the stated rationale for prone positioning here.
  169. The correct answer is D (Perpendicular, exiting the patella, the standard centering point for this projection): the standalone PA patella uses a central ray perpendicular, exiting the patella, matching its prone positioning that brings the patella closer to the receptor. Angled projections exiting the tibial tuberosity or femoral condyles, or a perpendicular ray exiting the fibular head, do not match this specific projection's centering.
  170. The correct answer is B (The hip joint, since it is closest to the suspected pathology): the femur AP and lateral generally include one adjacent joint, hip or knee, depending on where the pathology is suspected, so proximal femoral pathology calls for including the hip joint. Always including the knee regardless of location, excluding both joints entirely, or always including both together do not match this depends-on-location principle.
  171. The correct answer is A (Image the joint closer to the suspected pathology, with the opposite joint imaged separately): when the femur is too long for one image receptor, the joint closer to the suspected pathology is imaged with the femur, and the opposite joint is imaged separately, avoiding the distortion of trying to force the entire length onto one exposure. Omitting both joints, reducing source to image distance to force a fit, or angling the central ray to compress the image are not the correct approach for this situation.
  172. The correct answer is B (A direct frontal view of the patella itself, rather than its relationship to the femoral groove): the standalone patella view provides a direct frontal view of the patella itself for evaluating the bone's own cortical detail, while the tangential Merchant and Settegast views instead show the patella's relationship to the femoral groove for tracking and subluxation. Patellar tilt angle, lateral subluxation under load, and trochlear groove width are specifically what the tangential views assess, not the standalone patella view.
  173. The correct answer is C (The structure labeled number 1 on the accompanying diagram image): the patella is labeled as structure number one in this schematic knee diagram and is the structure the Merchant and Settegast tangential methods are designed to profile free of the femur. The other numbered structures correspond to the medial femoral condyle, intercondylar fossa, and tibial plateau rather than the patella.
  174. The correct answer is A (The structure labeled number 2 on the accompanying diagram image): the medial femoral condyle is labeled as structure number two in this schematic knee diagram and is one of the two condyles the oblique knee projections clear of overlap. The other numbered structures correspond to the patella, lateral femoral condyle, and tibial plateau rather than the medial femoral condyle.
  175. The correct answer is D (The structure labeled number 3 on the accompanying diagram image): the lateral femoral condyle is labeled as structure number three in this schematic knee diagram and is evaluated alongside the fibular head on the lateral oblique knee projection. The other numbered structures correspond to the patella, medial femoral condyle, and intercondylar fossa rather than the lateral femoral condyle.
  176. The correct answer is B (The structure labeled number 4 on the accompanying diagram image): the intercondylar fossa is labeled as structure number four in this schematic knee diagram and is the structure the Holmblad, Camp Coventry, and Béclère methods are all designed to demonstrate. The other numbered structures correspond to the patella, medial femoral condyle, and tibial plateau rather than the intercondylar fossa.
  177. The correct answer is C (The structure labeled number 5 on the accompanying diagram image): the tibial plateau is labeled as structure number five in this schematic knee diagram and is the proximal tibial surface evaluated for joint space narrowing on the weight-bearing AP knee. The other numbered structures correspond to the patella, medial femoral condyle, and lateral femoral condyle rather than the tibial plateau.
  178. The correct answer is D (Long bone measurement, or scanogram, evaluating limb length discrepancy directly): long bone measurement, or scanogram, evaluates limb length discrepancy, often in pediatric patients or before joint replacement, which matches this surgical planning scenario exactly. A skeletal survey and a non-accidental trauma workup serve entirely different clinical purposes, and a bone age study assesses skeletal maturity rather than limb length.
  179. The correct answer is A (A radiopaque ruler placed alongside the limb for length calibration): a radiopaque ruler is placed alongside the limb during long bone measurement so the radiologist can accurately calculate true bone length from the resulting images. A lead orientation marker, a gonadal shield, and a dosimetry badge do not serve this length-calibration purpose.
  180. The correct answer is B (Centering each joint separately avoids magnification distortion from one divergent beam): centering each joint separately avoids the magnification distortion a single divergent beam would cause, since a joint far from the central ray would appear artificially larger or smaller than one centered directly under it, corrupting the very measurement being taken. This approach is not primarily about dose reduction, receptor size limitations, or varying kilovoltage between exposures.
  181. The correct answer is D (A long bone measurement, using three separately centered exposures on one receptor): a long bone measurement uses three separately centered exposures over the hip, knee, and ankle on one receptor to avoid magnification distortion, providing the precise measurement needed for implant planning. A single overview exposure would introduce magnification error, a skeletal survey serves a different purpose entirely, and weight-bearing knee views alone do not measure overall limb length.
  182. The correct answer is A (A skeletal survey, screening for metastatic disease in an adult patient): a skeletal survey covers essentially the entire skeleton and is ordered for metastatic disease screening in adults, since cancers such as breast, prostate, and lung commonly spread to bone. Non-accidental trauma workups use a survey format for a different population, and long bone measurement and bone age studies serve entirely different clinical purposes.
  183. The correct answer is D (Lytic or sclerotic lesions from metastatic spread, the target of this survey): a metastatic disease survey of the skull, spine, ribs, pelvis, and long bones catches lytic or sclerotic lesions before a pathologic fracture occurs, which is exactly the finding pattern cancers such as breast, prostate, and lung produce when they spread to bone. Stress fractures, congenital ossification defects, and osteochondral loose bodies are not the target finding of this metastatic screening survey.
  184. The correct answer is C (A non-accidental trauma, or child abuse, skeletal survey): a suspected non-accidental trauma workup in a young child is a child abuse survey, imaging the skull, chest, spine, pelvis, and each extremity individually, which matches this clinical concern about inflicted injury. A metastatic survey addresses adult cancer spread, a bone age study assesses skeletal maturity, and long bone measurement addresses limb length rather than suspected abuse.
  185. The correct answer is B (It documents every skeletal injury present, including old healed fractures the caregiver's history omits): inflicted injuries in infants often include fractures at different healing stages that are not clinically obvious or reported by a caregiver, so imaging each region individually documents every skeletal injury present, including old healed fractures the history omits, rather than risking a single overview exposure missing subtle findings. This individualized approach is not primarily chosen for procedure time, shielding equipment needs, or sedation avoidance.
  186. The correct answer is C (The documentation can become part of a legal record used in court proceedings): because non-accidental trauma survey documentation can become part of a legal record, accurate positioning, correct side markers, and technically sound images matter as much here as anywhere in radiography. This heightened importance is not because of a substantially higher radiation dose, a contrast requirement, or being the only exam type subject to second radiologist review.
  187. The correct answer is C (Patient age and the clinical indication of malignancy versus suspected abuse): patient age and the clinical indication, adult malignancy screening versus suspected pediatric abuse, are the key factors distinguishing these two survey types, even though both broadly image much of the skeleton. The total exposure count is not fixed between the two, both surveys are performed with radiography rather than one always using CT, and the order of body parts imaged is not the distinguishing factor.
  188. The correct answer is D (Arthrography, the procedure that injects contrast directly into the joint capsule): arthrography is a fluoroscopically or ultrasound-guided procedure injecting contrast directly into a joint capsule to outline soft tissue plain radiographs cannot show, such as the menisci. Long bone measurement, a skeletal survey, and a bone age study are unrelated examinations that do not involve joint contrast injection.
  189. The correct answer is B (Single contrast with iodinated contrast alone, or double contrast adding air): conventional arthrography uses either single contrast, iodinated contrast alone, or double contrast, iodinated contrast plus air, to outline the joint capsule lining, cartilage, ligaments, and menisci or labrum. The other option combinations involving air alone, gadolinium and barium, or barium and iodinated contrast do not match the two standard conventional arthrography techniques.
  190. The correct answer is A (The fluid is aspirated first if present before contrast is injected): under sterile technique during arthrography, fluid is aspirated if present before contrast is injected while filling of the capsule is monitored. Leaving fluid undisturbed, diluting it with contrast directly, or draining it through a separate incision do not match the standard arthrography procedure sequence.
  191. The correct answer is B (MRI, since it shows soft tissue without an invasive injection): MRI has largely replaced conventional double-contrast arthrography for many indications since it shows soft tissue without an invasive injection. CT still involves radiation exposure, ultrasound alone does not fully replace joint imaging needs, and nuclear medicine bone scan does not directly visualize cartilage and ligament detail the way MRI does.
  192. The correct answer is C (Direct MR arthrography, injecting dilute gadolinium into the joint before scanning): direct MR arthrography injects dilute gadolinium into the joint before scanning, and is still used when the extra distension improves visualization of a subtle labral or ligament tear that standard MRI alone did not clarify. Conventional double-contrast arthrography uses iodinated contrast and air rather than gadolinium, and a non-accidental trauma survey and scanogram are unrelated procedures.
  193. The correct answer is A (Joint aspiration, withdrawing fluid for diagnostic testing or symptomatic relief): joint aspiration withdraws fluid for diagnostic testing such as infection, gout, or other inflammatory arthritis, which matches this acutely swollen and painful joint presentation. Joint injection instead delivers medication into the joint, arthrography injects contrast for imaging, and a skeletal survey is an unrelated imaging examination.
  194. The correct answer is B (Joint injection, delivering corticosteroid medication into the joint instead): joint injection delivers a corticosteroid and local anesthetic to reduce inflammation and pain, matching this therapeutic scenario for chronic osteoarthritis unresponsive to oral medication. Joint aspiration is primarily diagnostic or for symptomatic fluid removal, and the MR and conventional arthrography options describe imaging procedures rather than a therapeutic medication delivery.
  195. The correct answer is D (Fluoroscopy or ultrasound, providing real-time confirmation of correct needle placement): joint injection and aspiration are typically guided by fluoroscopy or ultrasound to confirm the needle sits inside the capsule rather than the surrounding soft tissue. CT, nuclear medicine, MRI, mammography, and bone densitometry are not the modalities used for this real-time procedural guidance.
  196. The correct answer is C (Introducing infection into a joint space is a serious complication): strict sterile technique is essential throughout joint aspiration and injection since introducing infection into a joint space is a serious complication, which is why this precaution carries heightened importance compared with routine non-invasive imaging. Sterile technique does not reduce radiation dose, is not required only because of contrast injection, and does not primarily function to shorten procedure time.
  197. The correct answer is D (Documenting the procedure with pre- and post-procedure images): the radiographer's role usually includes maintaining the sterile field, operating the equipment, and documenting the procedure with pre- and post-procedure images. Selecting and administering medication, independently interpreting final images, and independently determining which joint requires injection all fall outside the radiographer's scope of practice in this procedure.
  198. The correct answer is A (PA, lateral, and oblique projections of the affected finger alone): isolating the affected digit with PA, lateral, and oblique projections fully characterizes a suspected PIP dislocation, since the lateral view in particular shows displacement a PA alone would hide. A single hand PA, wrist-only projections, or a single fanned lateral of the whole hand do not isolate or fully characterize this specific digit's dislocation.
  199. The correct answer is B (Immobilize the wrist and repeat radiographs in ten to fourteen days): because an occult scaphoid fracture can be radiographically invisible initially, the wrist is typically immobilized and radiographs repeated in ten to fourteen days once bone resorption along the fracture line makes it visible. Immediate discharge without follow-up, proceeding straight to surgery, and simply repeating the same technique right away do not match this standard occult fracture management approach.
  200. The correct answer is D (Weight-bearing foot projections taken standing to assess the bone under load): weight-bearing foot projections are taken standing to evaluate the foot under physiologic load, which can be a useful adjunct when subtle stress-related changes are suspected and a supine image has not clearly shown them. A bone age study, an adapted Gaynor-Hart projection, and an adapted transthoracic lateral are not relevant techniques for evaluating a suspected metatarsal stress fracture.
